The place resided in by a bodhisattva is similar to a cetiya shrine; it is not fit for the enjoyment of others, and it is not possible to remove the bodhisattva's mother and install another in the position of chief queen, because the lifespan of the bodhisattva's mother is only that long; therefore, then she dies.

In which period of life does she die? In middle age.

For, in early life, the desire and lust of beings is strong in their own existence; therefore, at that time, a woman who has conceived is not able to protect the fetus; the fetus is subject to many ailments.

But in middle age, having passed two parts, in the third part the physical base is clear; in a clear physical base, the children born are healthy; therefore, the bodhisattva's mother also, having experienced prosperity in early life, gives birth in the third part of middle age and then dies. This is the natural law here.
25
25. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yathā aññā itthikā nava vā dasa vā māse gabbhaṃ kucchinā pariharitvā vijāyanti, na hevaṃ bodhisattaṃ bodhisattamātā vijāyati. Daseva māsāni bodhisattaṃ bodhisattamātā kucchinā pariharitvā vijāyati. Ayamettha dhammatā.
It is the rule (dhammatā), monks, that whereas other women carry the child in their womb (gabbhaṃ kucchinā pariharitvā) for nine or ten months before giving birth (vijāyanti), it is not so with the bodhisattva; the bodhisattva's mother gives birth (vijāyati) after carrying him in her womb (kucchinā pariharitvā) for exactly ten months. This is the natural law (dharmatā).
25.Nava vā dasa vāti ettha vā saddassa vikappanavasena satta vā aṭṭha vā ekādasa vā dvādasa vāti evamādīnaṃ saṅgaho veditabbo. Tattha sattamāsajāto jīvati, sītuṇhakkhamo pana na hoti. Aṭṭhamāsajāto na jīvati, avasesā jīvanti.
25. It should be understood that here, the word "or" (vā) includes groupings such as seven, eight, eleven, or twelve, according to the options.

There, one born in the seventh month lives, but is not resistant to cold and heat.

One born in the eighth month does not live, but the rest live.
26
26. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yathā aññā itthikā nisinnā vā nipannā vā vijāyanti, na hevaṃ bodhisattaṃ bodhisattamātā vijāyati. Ṭhitāva bodhisattaṃ bodhisattamātā vijāyati. Ayamettha dhammatā.
‘This, monks, is the natural law: just as other women give birth sitting or lying down, the Bodhisattva’s mother does not give birth in that way. The Bodhisattva’s mother gives birth standing up. This is the natural law (dharmatā).’
27
27. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yadā bodhisatto mātukucchimhā nikkhamati, devā paṭhamaṃ paṭiggaṇhanti, pacchā manussā. Ayamettha dhammatā.
‘This is the rule (dhammatā), monks (bhikkhave), that when a Bodhisattva (bodhisatto) issues from his mother’s womb (mātukucchimhā nikkhamati), devas (devā) welcome (paṭiggaṇhanti) him first (paṭhamaṃ), and then (pacchā) humans (manussā). This is the natural law (ayamettha dhammatā).’
27.Devā paṭhamaṃ paṭiggaṇhantīti khīṇāsavā suddhāvāsabrahmāno paṭiggaṇhanti. Kathaṃ paṭiggaṇhanti? ‘‘Sūtivesaṃ gaṇhitvā’’ti eke. Taṃ pana paṭikkhipitvā idaṃ vuttaṃ – ‘tadā bodhisattamātā suvaṇṇakhacitaṃ vatthaṃ nivāsetvā macchakkhisadisaṃ dukūlapaṭaṃ yāva pādantā pārupitvā aṭṭhāsi. Athassā sallahukagabbhavuṭṭhānaṃ ahosi, dhamakaraṇato udakanikkhamanasadisaṃ. Atha te pakatibrahmaveseneva upasaṅkamitvā paṭhamaṃ suvaṇṇajālena paṭiggahesuṃ. Tesaṃ hatthato cattāro mahārājāno ajinappaveṇiyā paṭiggahesuṃ. Tato manussā dukūlacumbaṭakena paṭiggahesuṃ’. Tena vuttaṃ – ‘‘devā paṭhamaṃ paṭiggaṇhanti, pacchā manussā’’ti.
27. "devā paṭhamaṃ paṭiggaṇhantīti" means the khīṇāsava pure abode (śuddhāvāsa) Brahmās receive [him].

How do they receive [him]? "Having taken the appearance of childbirth," say some.

However, rejecting that, this was said: "Then the mother of the Bodhisattva, having dressed in a golden-ornamented cloth, stood having covered herself with a fine linen cloth similar to a fish's eye down to her feet. Then she had an easy emergence from the womb, like water flowing from a water filter. Then they, in their natural Brahmā appearance, approached and first received [him] in a golden net. From their hands, the Four Great Kings (mahārājāno) received [him] on antelope skin. Then humans received [him] on a fine cloth."

Therefore, it was said, "First the gods receive [him], then humans."
28
28. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yadā bodhisatto mātukucchimhā nikkhamati, appattova bodhisatto pathaviṃ hoti, cattāro naṃ devaputtā paṭiggahetvā mātu purato ṭhapenti – ‘attamanā, devi, hohi; mahesakkho te putto uppanno’ti. Ayamettha dhammatā.
It is the natural law (dharmatā) , monks, that when a Bodhisattva emerges from his mother's womb (mātukucchimhā nikkhamati), he does not touch the earth (pathaviṃ). Four divine beings (devaputtā) receive him, and placing him before his mother, they say: “Rejoice (attamanā), O queen (devi), a son of great power (mahesakkho) has been born to you!” This, monks, is the rule (ayamettha dhammatā).
28.Cattāro naṃ devaputtāti cattāro mahārājāno. Paṭiggahetvāti ajinappaveṇiyā paṭiggahetvā. Mahesakkhoti mahātejo mahāyaso lakkhaṇasampanno.
28. "catāro naṃ devaputtā" means the Four Great Kings ( cattāro mahārājāno).

"paṭiggahetvā" means having accepted with an antelope hide (ajinappaveṇiyā paṭiggahetvā).

"mahesakkho" means of great power (mahātejo mahāyaso lakkhaṇasampanno).
29
29. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yadā bodhisatto mātukucchimhā nikkhamati, visadova nikkhamati amakkhito udena uddena (syā.), udarena (katthaci) amakkhito semhena amakkhito ruhirena amakkhito kenaci asucinā suddho visuddho (syā.) visado. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, maṇiratanaṃ kāsike vatthe nikkhittaṃ neva maṇiratanaṃ kāsikaṃ vatthaṃ makkheti, nāpi kāsikaṃ vatthaṃ maṇiratanaṃ makkheti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Ubhinnaṃ suddhattā. Evameva kho, bhikkhave, yadā bodhisatto mātukucchimhā nikkhamati, visadova nikkhamati amakkhito, udena amakkhito semhena amakkhito ruhirena amakkhito kenaci asucinā suddho visado. Ayamettha dhammatā.
‘It is the rule, bhikkhus, that when the bodhisatta issues from his mother’s womb, he issues forth stainless, not defiled by water (udena), mucus, blood or any impurity, pure and spotless (visado). Just as when a jewel (maṇiratanaṃ) is laid on muslin from Kasi, the jewel does not stain the muslin, or the muslin the jewel. Why is that? Because both are pure (suddhattā). In the same way, bhikkhus, when the bodhisatta issues from his mother’s womb, he issues forth stainless, not defiled by water, mucus, blood or any impurity, pure and spotless. This is the natural law (dhammatā).’
29.Visadova nikkhamatīti yathā aññe sattā yonimagge laggantā bhaggavibhaggā nikkhamanti, na evaṃ nikkhamati, alaggo hutvā nikkhamatīti attho udenāti udakena. Kenaci asucināti yathā aññe sattā kammajavātehi uddhaṃpādā adhosirā yonimagge pakkhittā sataporisaṃ narakapapātaṃ patantā viya, tāḷacchiddena nikkaḍḍhiyamānā hatthī viya mahādukkhaṃ anubhavantā nānāasucimakkhitāva nikkhamanti, na evaṃ bodhisatto. Bodhisattañhi kammajavātā uddhapādaṃ adhosiraṃ kātuṃ na sakkonti. So dhammāsanato otaranto dhammakathiko viya, nisseṇito otaranto puriso viya ca dve hatthe ca dve pāde ca pasāretvā ṭhitakova mātukucchisambhavena kenaci asucinā amakkhitova nikkhamati. Udakassa dhārāti udakavaṭṭiyo. Tāsu sītā suvaṇṇakaṭāhe patati uṇhā rajatakaṭāhe. Idañca pathavitale kenaci asucinā asammissaṃ tesaṃ pānīyaparibhojanīyaudakañceva aññehi asādhāraṇaṃ kīḷāudakañca dassetuṃ vuttaṃ, aññassa pana suvaṇṇarajataghaṭehi āhariyamānaudakassa ceva haṃsavattakādipokkharaṇīgatassa ca udakassa paricchedo natthi. Udakassa dhārā
29. "Visadova Nikkhamatīti" Just as other beings exit stuck in the birth canal, broken and shattered, he does not exit like that, un-stuck. Therefore, udenā means by water (udakena).

"Kenaci Asucinā" Just as other beings, tossed into the birth canal by winds of karma, head downwards, experiencing great suffering like an elephant being dragged out through a hole made by a palm leaf, covered in various impurities, they exit; the Bodhisattva does not exit like that.

Indeed, the winds of karma cannot make the Bodhisattva head downwards.

He descends from the Dharma seat like a preacher, he descends from a ladder like a man, and having stretched out both his hands and both his feet, he stands, unsoiled by any impurity arising from his mother's womb, and exits.

"Udakassa Dhārā" means water circles.

Cool water falls in a golden bowl, warm water in a silver bowl.

And it is said that on the surface of the earth, unmixed with any impurity, there is water for drinking and eating, water for playing that is uncommon to others, but there is no limit to the water which is brought in golden and silver pots, nor to the water in ponds inhabited by swans, ducks, etc.

Udakassa Dhārā
30
30. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yadā bodhisatto mātukucchimhā nikkhamati, dve udakassa dhārā antalikkhā pātubhavanti – ekā sītassa ekā uṇhassa yena bodhisattassa udakakiccaṃ karonti mātu ca. Ayamettha dhammatā.
“This is the rule, monks, that when a bodhisatta emerges from his mother's womb, two streams of water appear from the sky, one cold and one warm, with which they perform the water ritual for the bodhisatta and his mother. This is the natural law.
31
31. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, sampatijāto bodhisatto samehi pādehi patiṭṭhahitvā uttarābhimukho uttarenābhimukho (syā.) uttarenamukho (ka.) sattapadavītihārena gacchati setamhi chatte anudhāriyamāne, sabbā ca disā anuviloketi, āsabhiṃ vācaṃ bhāsati ‘aggohamasmi lokassa, jeṭṭhohamasmi lokassa, seṭṭhohamasmi lokassa, ayamantimā jāti, natthidāni punabbhavo’ti. Ayamettha dhammatā.
‘This is the rule, monks, that as soon as he is born, the bodhisattva takes a firm stance with both feet, faces north, and proceeds with a stride of seven steps (sattapadavītihārena), while a white parasol is held over him. He surveys all directions and utters a bull-like speech (āsabhiṃ vācaṃ bhāsati): “I am the chief (aggo) in the world (lokassa), the eldest (jeṭṭho) in the world (lokassa), the best (seṭṭho) in the world (lokassa). This is my last birth (antimā jāti); now there is no more rebirth (punabbhavo)”. This is the natural law (dharmatā).’
31.Sampatijātoti muhuttajāto. Pāḷiyaṃ pana mātukucchito nikkhantamatto viya dassito, na evaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Nikkhantamattañhi naṃ paṭhamaṃ brahmāno suvaṇṇajālena paṭiggaṇhiṃsu, tesaṃ hatthato cattāro mahārājāno ajinappaveṇiyā, tesaṃ hatthato manussā dukūlacumbaṭakena. Manussānaṃ hatthato muccitvā pathaviyaṃ patiṭṭhito. Setamhi chatte anudhāriyamāneti dibbasetacchatte anudhāriyamānamhi. Ettha ca chattassa parivārāni khaggādīni pañca rājakakudhabhaṇḍānipi āgatāneva. Pāḷiyaṃ pana rājagamane rājā viya chattameva vuttaṃ. Tesu chattameva paññāyati, na chattaggāhako. Tathā khaggatālavaṇṭamorahatthakavāḷabījanīuṇhīsamattāyeva paññāyanti, na tesaṃ gāhakā. Sabbāni kira tāni adissamānarūpā devatā gaṇhiṃsu. Vuttañcetaṃ – ‘‘Anekasākhañca sahassamaṇḍalaṃ, Chattaṃ marū dhārayumantalikkhe; Suvaṇṇadaṇḍā vipatanti cāmarā, Na dissare cāmarachattagāhakā’’ti. (su. ni. 693); Sabbā ca disāti idaṃ sattapadavītihārūpari ṭhitassa viya sabbadisānuvilokanaṃ vuttaṃ, na kho panevaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Mahāsatto hi manussānaṃ hatthato muccitvā paṭhaviyaṃ patiṭṭhito puratthimaṃ disaṃ olokesi. Anekāni cakkavāḷasahassāni ekaṅgaṇāni ahesuṃ. Tattha devamanussā gandhamālādīhi pūjayamānā – ‘‘mahāpurisa, idha tumhehi sadisopi natthi, kuto uttaritaro’’ti āhaṃsu. Evaṃ catasso disā, catasso anudisā, heṭṭhā, uparīti dasa disā anuviloketvā attanā sadisaṃ adisvā – ‘‘ayaṃ uttarā disā’’ti uttarābhimukho sattapadavītihārena agamāsīti evamettha attho veditabbo. Āsabhinti uttamaṃ. Aggoti guṇehi sabbapaṭhamo. Itarāni dve padāni etasseva vevacanāni. Ayamantimā jāti, natthi dāni punabbhavoti padadvayena imasmiṃ attabhāve pattabbaṃ arahattaṃ byākāsi. Sabbā ca disā Āsabhi Aggo Ayamantimā jāti, natthi dāni punabbhavo Ettha ca samehi pādehi pathaviyā patiṭṭhānaṃ caturiddhipādapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ, uttarābhimukhabhāvo mahājanaṃ ajjhottharitvā abhibhavitvā gamanassa pubbanimittaṃ, sattapadagamanaṃ sattabojjhaṅgaratanapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ, dibbasetacchattadhāraṇaṃ vimuttivarachattapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ, pañcarājakakudhabhaṇḍānaṃ paṭilābho pañcahi vimuttīhi vimuccanassa pubbanimittaṃ, sabbadisānuvilokanaṃ anāvaraṇañāṇapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ, āsabhivācābhāsanaṃ appaṭivattiyadhammacakkappavattanassa pubbanimittaṃ, ‘‘ayamantimā jātī’’ti sīhanādo anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbānassa pubbanimittanti veditabbaṃ . Ime vārā pāḷiyaṃ āgatā, sambahulavāro pana nāgato, āharitvā dīpetabbo. Mahāpurisassa hi jātadivase dasasahassilokadhātu kampi. Dasasahassilokadhātumhi devatā ekacakkavāḷe sannipatiṃsu. Paṭhamaṃ devā paṭiggaṇhiṃsu, pacchā manussā. Tantibaddhā vīṇā cammabaddhā bheriyo ca kenaci avāditā sayameva vajjiṃsu. Manussānaṃ andubandhanādīni khaṇḍākhaṇḍaṃ chijjiṃsu. Sabbarogā vūpasamiṃsu, ambilena dhotatambamalaṃ viya vigacchiṃsu. Jaccandhā rūpāni passiṃsu. Jaccabadhirā saddaṃ suṇiṃsu. Pīṭhasappī javasampannā ahesuṃ. Jātijaḷānampi eḷamūgānaṃ sati patiṭṭhāsi. Videsapakkhandā nāvā supaṭṭanaṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Ākāsaṭṭhakabhūmaṭṭhakaratanāni sakatejobhāsitāni ahesuṃ. Verino mettacittaṃ paṭilabhiṃsu. Avīcimhi aggi nibbāyi. Lokantaresu āloko udapādi. Nadīsu jalaṃ nappavattati. Mahāsamudde madhurasaṃ udakaṃ ahosi. Vāto na vāyi. Ākāsapabbatarukkhagatā sakuṇā bhassitvā pathavigatā ahesuṃ. Cando ativiroci. Sūriyo na uṇho, na sītalo, nimmalo utusampanno ahosi. Devatā attano attano vimānadvāre ṭhatvā apphoṭanaseḷanacelukkhepādīhi mahākīḷakaṃ kīḷiṃsu. Cātuddīpikamahāmegho vassi. Mahājanaṃ neva khudā na pipāsā pīḷesi. Dvārakavāṭāni sayameva vivariṃsu. Pupphūpagaphalūpagā rukkhā pupphaphalāni gaṇhiṃsu. Dasasahassilokadhātu ekaddhajamālā ahosi. Tatrāpi dasasahassilokadhātukampo sabbaññutaññāṇapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Devatānaṃ ekacakkavāḷe sannipāto dhammacakkappavattanakāle ekappahāreneva sannipatitvā dhammaṃ paṭiggaṇhanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Paṭhamaṃ devatānaṃ paṭiggahaṇaṃ catunnaṃ rūpāvacarajjhānānaṃ paṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Pacchā manussānaṃ paṭiggahaṇaṃ catunnaṃ arūpāvacarajjhānānaṃ paṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Tantibaddhavīṇānaṃ sayaṃ vajjanaṃ anupubbavihārapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Cammabaddhabherīnaṃ vajjanaṃ mahatiyā dhammabheriyā anussāvanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Andubandhanādīnaṃ chedo asmimānasamucchedassa pubbanimittaṃ. Mahājanassa rogavigamo catusaccapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Jaccandhānaṃ rūpadassanaṃ dibbacakkhupaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ . Badhirānaṃ saddassavanaṃ dibbasotadhātupaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Pīṭhasappīnaṃ javasampadā caturiddhipādapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Jaḷānaṃ satipatiṭṭhānaṃ catusatipaṭṭhānapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Videsapakkhandanāvānaṃ supaṭṭanasampāpuṇanaṃ catupaṭisambhidādhigamassa pubbanimittaṃ. Ratanānaṃ sakatejobhāsitattaṃ yaṃ lokassa dhammobhāsaṃ dassessati, tassa pubbanimittaṃ. Verīnaṃ mettacittapaṭilābho catubrahmavihārapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Avīcimhi agginibbāyanaṃ ekādasaagginibbāyanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Lokantarikāloko avijjandhakāraṃ vidhamitvā ñāṇālokadassanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Mahāsamuddassa madhuratā nibbānarasena ekarasabhāvassa pubbanimittaṃ. Vātassa avāyanaṃ dvāsaṭṭhidiṭṭhigatabhindanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Sakuṇānaṃ pathavigamanaṃ mahājanassa ovādaṃ sutvā pāṇehi saraṇagamanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Candassa ativirocanaṃ bahujanakantatāya pubbanimittaṃ. Sūriyassa uṇhasītavivajjanautusukhatā kāyikacetasikasukhappattiyā pubbanimittaṃ. Devatānaṃ vimānadvāresu ṭhatvā apphoṭanādīhi kīḷanaṃ buddhabhāvaṃ patvā udānaṃ udānassa pubbanimittaṃ. Cātuddīpikamahāmeghavassanaṃ mahato dhammameghavassanassa pubbanimittaṃ. Khudāpīḷanassa abhāvo kāyagatāsatiamatapaṭilābhassa pubbanimittaṃ. Pipāsāpīḷanassa abhāvo vimuttisukhena sukhitabhāvassa pubbanimittaṃ. Dvārakavāṭānaṃ sayameva vivaraṇaṃ aṭṭhaṅgikamaggadvāravivaraṇassa pubbanimittaṃ. Rukkhānaṃ pupphaphalaggahaṇaṃ vimuttipupphehi pupphitassa ca sāmaññaphalabhārabharitabhāvassa ca pubbanimittaṃ. Dasasahassilokadhātuyā ekaddhajamālitā ariyaddhajamālamālitāya pubbanimittanti veditabbaṃ. Ayaṃ sambahulavāro nāma. Ettha pañhaṃ pucchanti – ‘‘yadā mahāpuriso pathaviyaṃ patiṭṭhahitvā uttarābhimukho padasā gantvā āsabhiṃ vācaṃ abhāsi, tadā kiṃ pathaviyā gato, udāhu ākāsena; dissamāno gato, udāhu adissamāno; acelako gato, udāhu alaṅkatapaṭiyatto; daharo hutvā gato , udāhu mahallako; pacchāpi kiṃ tādisova ahosi, udāhu puna bāladārako’’ti? Ayaṃ pana pañho heṭṭhālohapāsāde samuṭṭhito tipiṭakacūḷābhayattherena vissajjitova. Thero kira ettha niyatipubbekatakammaissaranimmānavādavasena taṃ taṃ bahuṃ vatvā avasāne evaṃ byākari – ‘‘mahāpuriso pathaviyā gato, mahājanassa pana ākāsena gacchanto viya ahosi. Dissamāno gato, mahājanassa pana adissamāno viya ahosi. Acelako gato, mahājanassa pana alaṅkatapaṭiyatto viya upaṭṭhāsi. Daharova gato, mahājanassa pana soḷasavassuddesiko viya ahosi. Pacchā pana bāladārakova ahosi, na tādiso’’ti. Parisā cassa – ‘‘buddhena viya hutvā bho therena pañho kathito’’ti attamanā ahosi. Lokantarikavāro vuttanayo eva. Imā ca pana ādito paṭṭhāya kathitā sabbadhammatā sabbabodhisattānaṃ hontīti veditabbā. Dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇavaṇṇanā
31. Sampatijāto means born at that very moment.

In the Pāḷi, however, it is shown as just having come out of the mother's womb; it should not be seen like that.

For, immediately after he came out, the Brahmās received him in a golden net; from their hands the Four Great Kings ( cattāro mahārājāno) [received him] on antelope skins (ajinappaveṇiyā), from their hands men [received him] on a roll of fine cloth (dukūlacumbaṭakena).

Having been freed from the hands of men, he stood on the earth.

"While a white umbrella was being held over [him]" means while a divine white umbrella was being held over [him].

And here, the paraphernalia of the umbrella, swords and the like, and the five royal emblems came into being.

In the Pāḷi, however, in the case of a king's procession, only the umbrella is mentioned as being like a king.

Among these, only the umbrella is evident, not the umbrella-holder.

Likewise, swords, palm-leaf fans, peacock-feather fans, hand fans, whisks, and head-dresses are evident, but not their holders.

It is said that all those deities, whose forms are invisible, took [them].

And it was said:

"And an anekasākha with a thousand circles,

An umbrella the Maruts held in the sky;

Golden rods and chowries fall,

Chowrie and umbrella bearers are not seen."

(Su. Ni. 693);

"And all directions" means this looking around in all directions is said to be like [something] standing above the seven strides. However, it should not be seen like that.

For the Great Being ( mahāsatto), having been freed from the hands of men and standing on the earth, looked at the eastern direction.

Innumerable world-systems (cakkavāḷasahassāni) became a single courtyard.

There, gods and men, honoring [him] with fragrant garlands and the like, said: "Great man (mahāpurisa), there is no one here equal to you, how could there be a superior?"

In this way, having looked around at the four directions (catasso disā), the four intermediate directions (catasso anudisā), below, and above—ten directions—and not seeing anyone equal to himself, [he said], "This is the northern direction (uttarā disā)," and went northwards by the seven-stride gait. Thus, the meaning should be understood here.

"Āsabhi" means the best (uttamaṃ).

"Aggo" means the first of all in qualities (guṇehi sabbapaṭhamo).

The other two words are synonyms for this very thing.

"This is my last birth, now there is no future existence"—with this pair of words, he declared the state of an arhat to be attained in this very existence.

And:

Sabbā ca disā āsabhi aggo ayamantimā jāti, natthi dāni punabbhavo

Here, the standing on the earth with even feet is a preliminary sign of obtaining the four bases of supernormal power ( caturiddhipāda); the state of being facing north is a preliminary sign of going having overwhelmed and surpassed the great mass of people; the going of seven strides is a preliminary sign of obtaining the seven jewels of enlightenment factors ( sattabojjhaṅgaratana); the holding of the divine white umbrella is a preliminary sign of obtaining the umbrella of the best liberation ( vimuttivarachatta); the obtaining of the five royal emblems is a preliminary sign of being freed by the five liberations (pañca vimutti); the looking around in all directions is a preliminary sign of obtaining unobstructed knowledge ( anāvaraṇañāṇa); the speaking of the āsabhi speech is a preliminary sign of the turning of the wheel of the irreversible Dharma ( appaṭivattiyadhamma cakka); the lion's roar (sīhanāda) "This is my last birth" is to be known as a preliminary sign of the parinirvāṇa of the element of nirvāṇa without remainder (anupādisesāya nibbānadhātu).

These turns have come in the Pāḷi, but the abundant turn has not come; it should be brought and explained.

For, on the day of the Great Man's (mahāpurisa) birth, the ten-thousand world-system (dasasahassilokadhātu) trembled.

In the ten-thousand world-system, the deities assembled in one cakkavāḷa.

First the gods received him, afterwards men.

Stringed lutes (vīṇā) and leather-covered drums (bheriyo) that were not played by anyone sounded by themselves.

The bonds and so on of men were cut into pieces.

All diseases were calmed; they disappeared like dirt on copper that has been washed with vinegar.
Congenitally blind people saw forms (rūpāni).
Congenitally deaf people heard sounds (saddaṃ).
Well-fed people were endowed with swiftness (javasampannā).
Mindfulness was established in even the inherently dull and mute (jātijaḷānampi eḷamūgānaṃ sati patiṭṭhāsi).
Immigrant boats reached a good harbor (supaṭṭanaṃ).
Jewels in the sky and on the earth shone with their own brilliance (sakatejobhāsitāni).
Enemies obtained minds of loving-kindness (mettacittaṃ).
Fire went out in Avīci Hell (avīcimhi aggi nibbāyi).
Light arose in other worlds (lokantaresu āloko udapādi).
Water did not flow in the rivers (nadīsu jalaṃ nappavattati).
Sweet-tasting water existed in the great ocean (mahāsamudde madhurasaṃ udakaṃ ahosi).
The wind did not blow (vāto na vāyi).
Birds in the sky, on mountains, and in trees, having chirped, went to the earth (ākāsapabbatarukkhagatā sakuṇā bhassitvā pathavigatā ahesuṃ).
The moon shone exceedingly (cando ativiroci).
The sun was not hot, not cold, pure, and endowed with the season (sūriyo na uṇho, na sītalo, nimmalo utusampanno ahosi).
Gods, standing at the doors of their own palaces (attano attano vimānadvāre ṭhatvā), played a great game with clapping, making noises, tossing up their garments, and so forth (apphoṭanaseḷanacelukkhepādīhi mahākīḷakaṃ kīḷiṃsu).
A four-continent great cloud rained (cātuddīpikamahāmegho vassi).
Neither hunger nor thirst afflicted the great multitude (mahājanaṃ neva khudā na pipāsā pīḷesi).
The doors and gates opened by themselves (dvārakavāṭāni sayameva vivariṃsu).
Trees bearing flowers and fruits took flowers and fruits (pupphūpagaphalūpagā rukkhā pupphaphalāni gaṇhiṃsu).
The ten-thousand world-system was a single-banner garland (dasasahassilokadhātu ekaddhajamālā ahosi).

Even there, the shaking of the ten-thousand world-system was a preliminary sign of the attainment of omniscience (sabbaññutaññāṇa).
The gathering of the gods in a single cakravāḷa at the time of the turning of the wheel of the Dharma, having gathered together with a single strike, was a preliminary sign of receiving the Dharma.
The first reception of the gods was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the four rūpāvacara jhānā (catunnaṃ rūpāvacarajjhānānaṃ paṭilābhassa).
Afterwards, the reception of humans was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the four arūpāvacara jhānā (catunnaṃ arūpāvacarajjhānānaṃ paṭilābhassa).
The playing of stringed lutes by themselves was a preliminary sign of the attainment of successive dwelling.
The playing of leather-bound kettle drums was a preliminary sign of the proclamation of the great Dharma-kettle drum.
The cutting of elephant-chains and so forth was a preliminary sign of the destruction of conceit in "I am."
The disappearance of disease from the great multitude was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the four truths (catusacca).
The seeing of forms by the congenitally blind was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the divine eye (dibbacakkhu).
The hearing of sounds by the deaf was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the divine ear-element (dibbasotadhātu).
The swiftness of well-fed people was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the four bases of miraculous power (caturiddhipāda).
The mindfulness established in the dull was a preliminary sign of the attainment of the four establishments of mindfulness (catusatipaṭṭhāna).
The good attainment of harbors by immigrant boats was a preliminary sign of the mastery of the four kinds of analytical knowledge (catupaṭisambhidādhigama).
The self-shining of the jewels, which will show the light of the Dharma to the world, was a preliminary sign of that.
0441"/> The attainment of loving-kindness ( mettā) is a preliminary sign of the attainment of the four Brahmā-vihāras.

The extinguishing of fire in Avīci is a preliminary sign of the extinguishing of the eleven fires.

The light of the world-in-between, after dispelling the darkness of ignorance, is a preliminary sign of seeing the light of knowledge (ñāṇā)

The sweetness of the great ocean is a preliminary sign of the single taste of nibbāna.

The blowing of the wind is a preliminary sign of shattering the sixty-two kinds of [false] views.

The birds going to the earth is a preliminary sign of the multitude, having heard the advice, going for refuge with their lives.

The excessive brilliance of the moon is a preliminary sign of being pleasing to many people.

The agreeable seasonal comfort of the sun, devoid of heat and cold, is a preliminary sign of the attainment of bodily and mental happiness (sukha).

The gods, standing at the doors of the mansions, playing with hand-clapping etc., having attained the state of a Buddha, the utterance [of joy] is a preliminary sign of the utterance.

The raining of the great cloud in the four continents is a preliminary sign of the raining of the great Dharma cloud.

The absence of suffering from hunger is a preliminary sign of the attainment of mindfulness related to the body and the immortal.

The absence of suffering from thirst is a preliminary sign of the state of being happy with the bliss of liberation (vimutti).

The spontaneous opening of the door panels is a preliminary sign of the opening of the door of the eightfold path.

The trees taking flowers and fruits is a preliminary sign of being flowered with the flowers of liberation and the state of being filled with the burden of the fruits of asceticism.

The ten-thousand world-system being arrayed with a single flag is to be known as a preliminary sign of being arrayed with a garland of noble flags.

This is called the Discourse on a Multitude of Occurrences.

Here they ask a question - "When the great man, having stood on the earth, having gone northwards on foot, spoke the bull-like speech, then did he go on the earth, or in the sky; did he go visibly, or invisibly; did he go naked, or adorned and prepared; did he go young , or old; afterwards also was he just like that, or again a young boy?" This question, however, having arisen in the Lower Copper Palace, was solved by Tipiṭaka Cūḷābhaya Thera.

The Elder, there, having said much about the doctrine of fate, past action, the Lord, and creation, in the end, he declared thus - "The great man went on the earth, but to the multitude, he was as if going in the sky.

He went visibly, but to the multitude, he was as if invisible.

He went naked, but to the multitude, he appeared as if adorned and prepared.

He went young, but to the multitude, he was as if sixteen years old.

Afterwards, however, he was a young boy, not like that."

And his assembly was delighted, saying - "The question was spoken by the Elder, as if having become a Buddha."

The discourse on the world-in-between is as already stated.

These, however, all the qualities of the Dharma spoken from the beginning, are to be known as belonging to all Bodhisattvas.

Description of the Thirty-Two Marks of a Great Man
32
32. ‘‘Dhammatā esā, bhikkhave, yadā bodhisatto mātukucchimhā nikkhamati, atha sadevake loke samārake sabrahmake sassamaṇabrāhmaṇiyā pajāya sadevamanussāya appamāṇo uḷāro obhāso pātubhavati, atikkammeva devānaṃ devānubhāvaṃ. Yāpi tā lokantarikā aghā asaṃvutā andhakārā andhakāratimisā, yattha pime candimasūriyā evaṃmahiddhikā evaṃmahānubhāvā ābhāya nānubhonti, tatthapi appamāṇo uḷāro obhāso pātubhavati atikkammeva devānaṃ devānubhāvaṃ. Yepi tattha sattā upapannā, tepi tenobhāsena aññamaññaṃ sañjānanti – ‘aññepi kira, bho, santi sattā idhūpapannā’ti. Ayañca dasasahassī lokadhātu saṅkampati sampakampati sampavedhati appamāṇo ca uḷāro obhāso loke pātubhavati atikkammeva devānaṃ devānubhāvaṃ. Ayamettha dhammatā. Dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇā
32. It is the rule (dharmatā), monks, that when a bodhisattva departs from his mother’s womb, then in this world with its deities (sadevake loke), with Māras (samārake), with Brahmās (sabrahmake), with its ascetics and Brahmins (sassamaṇabrāhmaṇiyā), with its princes and people (sadevamanussāya), an immeasurable, splendid light (appamāṇo uḷāro obhāso) appears, surpassing the glory of the gods (devānaṃ devānubhāvaṃ).

And whatever dark spaces lie beyond the world’s end (lokantarikā), chaotic (aghā), blind and black (asaṃvutā andhakārā andhakāratimisā), such that they are not even reached by the moon and the sun (candimasūriyā) so mighty and powerful (evaṃmahiddhikā evaṃmahānubhāvā ābhāya nānubhonti), are yet illumined by this immeasurable splendid light (appamāṇo uḷāro obhāso) surpassing the glory of the gods (devānaṃ devānubhāvaṃ).

And those beings that have been reborn there (tatra sattā upapannā) recognise each other by this light (tenobhāsena aññamaññaṃ sañjānanti) and know: “Other beings, too, have been born here (aññepi kira, bho, santi sattā idhūpapannā)!”

And this ten-thousandfold world system (dasasahassī lokadhātu) shakes and quakes and trembles (saṅkampati sampakampati sampavedhati), and there too a great immeasurable light (appamāṇo ca uḷāro obhāso) surpassing the splendour of the gods (devānaṃ devānubhāvaṃ) appears in the world (loke pātubhavati).

This is the natural law (ayamettha dhammatā).

The Thirty-Two Marks of a Great Man (dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇā)
33
33. ‘‘Jāte kho pana, bhikkhave, vipassimhi kumāre bandhumato rañño paṭivedesuṃ – ‘putto te, deva deva te (ka.), jāto, taṃ devo passatū’ti. Addasā kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā vipassiṃ kumāraṃ, disvā nemitte brāhmaṇe āmantāpetvā etadavoca – ‘passantu bhonto nemittā brāhmaṇā kumāra’nti. Addasaṃsu kho, bhikkhave, nemittā brāhmaṇā vipassiṃ kumāraṃ, disvā bandhumantaṃ rājānaṃ etadavocuṃ – ‘attamano, deva, hohi, mahesakkho te putto uppanno, lābhā te, mahārāja, suladdhaṃ te, mahārāja, yassa te kule evarūpo putto uppanno. Ayañhi, deva, kumāro dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇehi samannāgato, yehi samannāgatassa mahāpurisassa dveva gatiyo bhavanti anaññā. Sace agāraṃ ajjhāvasati, rājā hoti cakkavattī dhammiko dhammarājā cāturanto vijitāvī janapadatthāvariyappatto sattaratanasamannāgato. Tassimāni sattaratanāni bhavanti. Seyyathidaṃ – cakkaratanaṃ hatthiratanaṃ assaratanaṃ maṇiratanaṃ itthiratanaṃ gahapatiratanaṃ pariṇāyakaratanameva sattamaṃ. Parosahassaṃ kho panassa puttā bhavanti sūrā vīraṅgarūpā parasenappamaddanā. So imaṃ pathaviṃ sāgarapariyantaṃ adaṇḍena asatthena dhammena abhivijiya ajjhāvasati. Sace kho pana agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajati, arahaṃ hoti sammāsambuddho loke vivaṭacchado.
33. Monks, when Prince Vipassi was born, they reported to King Bandhuma: "A son is born to you, O King, may the King see him."

Then, monks, King Bandhuma saw Prince Vipassi, and having seen him, summoned the Brahmins skilled in interpreting signs and said this: "Let the honorable Brahmins skilled in signs examine the prince."

Then, monks, the Brahmins skilled in signs examined Prince Vipassi, and having seen him, said this to King Bandhuma: "Rejoice, O King, be happy, a mighty son has been born to you. It is a gain for you, Great King, a great gain for you, Great King, that such a son has been born into your family.

Indeed, O King, this prince is endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great man (dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇehi samannāgato), and for a great man endowed with these, there are only two possible destinies (dveva gatiyo bhavanti anaññā).

If he lives the household life, he will be a king, a wheel-turning monarch (cakkavattī), a righteous king (dhammarājā), a conqueror of the four quarters (cāturanto), a subjugator, who has attained stability in his country and is possessed of the seven treasures (sattaratanasamannāgato).

These are the seven treasures (sattaratanāni bhavanti):

namely, the wheel treasure (cakkaratanaṃ), the elephant treasure (hatthiratanaṃ), the horse treasure (assaratanaṃ), the jewel treasure (maṇiratanaṃ), the woman treasure (itthiratanaṃ), the householder treasure (gahapatiratanaṃ), and the counselor treasure (pariṇāyakaratanameva), as the seventh.

He has more than a thousand sons, who are brave (sūrā), heroic (vīraṅgarūpā), and crush the armies of others (parasenappamaddanā).

He dwells having conquered this earth bounded by the ocean, without stick or sword, by the Dharma (dhammena).

But if he goes forth from the household life into homelessness (agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajati), he becomes an Arhat (arahaṃ hoti), a Fully Enlightened Buddha (sammāsambuddho), one who draws back the veil in the world (loke vivaṭacchado)."
33.Addasa khoti dukūlacumbaṭake nipajjāpetvā ānītaṃ addasa. Mahāpurisassāti jātigottakulapadesādivasena mahantassa purisassa. Dve gatiyoti dve niṭṭhā, dve nipphattiyo. Ayañhi gatisaddo – ‘‘pañca kho imā, sāriputta, gatiyo’’ti (ma. ni. 1.153) ettha nirayādibhedāya sattehi gantabbagatiyā vattati. ‘‘Imesaṃ kho ahaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ sīlavantānaṃ kalyāṇadhammānaṃ neva jānāmi āgatiṃ vā gatiṃ vā’’ti (ma. ni. 1.508) ettha ajjhāsaye. ‘‘Nibbānaṃ arahato gatī’’ti (pari. 339) ettha paṭissaraṇe. ‘‘Api ca tyāhaṃ brahme gatiñca pajānāmi, jutiñca pajānāmi evaṃmahiddhiko bako brahmā’’ti (ma. ni. 1.503) ettha nipphattiyaṃ vattati. Svāyamidhāpi nipphattiyaṃ vattatīti veditabbo. Anaññāti aññā gati nipphatti nāma natthi. Dhammikoti dasakusaladhammasamannāgato agatigamanavirahito. Dhammarājāti idaṃ purimapadasseva vevacanaṃ. Dhammena vā laddharajjattā dhammarājā. Cāturantoti puratthimasamuddādīnaṃ catunnaṃ samuddānaṃ vasena caturantāya pathaviyā issaro. Vijitāvīti vijitasaṅgāmo. Janapado asmiṃ thāvariyaṃ thirabhāvaṃ pattoti janapadatthāvariyappatto. Caṇḍassa hi rañño balidaṇḍādīhi lokaṃ pīḷayato manussā majjhimajanapadaṃ chaḍḍetvā pabbatasamuddatīrādīni nissāya paccante vāsaṃ kappenti. Atimudukassa rañño corehi sāhasikadhanavilopapīḷitā manussā paccantaṃ pahāya janapadamajjhe vāsaṃ kappenti, iti evarūpe rājini janapado thirabhāvaṃ na pāpuṇāti. Imasmiṃ pana kumāre rajjaṃ kārayamāne etassa janapado pāsāṇapiṭṭhiyaṃ ṭhapetvā ayopaṭṭena parikkhitto viya thiro bhavissatīti dassento – ‘‘janapadatthāvariyappatto’’ti āhaṃsu. Dhammiko Dhammarājā Cāturanto Vijitāvī janapadatthāvariyappatto. Sattaratanasamannāgatoti ettha ratijananaṭṭhena ratanaṃ. Apica – Sattaratanasamannāgato ratanaṃ. ‘‘Cittīkataṃ mahagghañca, atulaṃ dullabhadassanaṃ; Anomasattaparibhogaṃ, ratanaṃ tena vuccati’’. Cakkaratanassa ca nibbattakālato paṭṭhāya aññaṃ devaṭṭhānaṃ nāma na hoti, sabbe gandhapupphādīhi tasseva pūjañca abhivādanādīni ca karontīti cittīkataṭṭhena ratanaṃ. Cakkaratanassa ca ettakaṃ nāma dhanaṃ agghatīti aggho natthi, iti mahagghaṭṭhenāpi ratanaṃ. Cakkaratanañca aññehi loke vijjamānaratanehi asadisanti atulaṭṭhenāpi ratanaṃ. Yasmā ca pana yasmiṃ kappe buddhā uppajjanti, tasmiṃyeva cakkavattino uppajjanti, buddhā ca kadāci karahaci uppajjanti, tasmā dullabhadassanaṭṭhenāpi ratanaṃ. Tadetaṃ jātirūpakulaissariyādīhi anomassa uḷārasattasseva uppajjati, na aññassāti anomasattaparibhogaṭṭhenāpi ratanaṃ. Yathā cakkaratanaṃ, evaṃ sesānipīti. Imehi sattahi ratanehi parivārabhāvena ceva sabbabhogūpakaraṇabhāvena ca samannāgatoti sattaratanasamannāgato. Idāni tesaṃ sarūpato dassanatthaṃ tassimānītiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha cakkaratanantiādīsu ayaṃ saṅkhepādhippāyo – dvesahassadīpaparivārānaṃ catunnaṃ mahādīpānaṃ sirivibhavaṃ gahetvā dātuṃ samatthaṃ cakkaratanaṃ pātubhavati. Tathā purebhattameva sāgarapariyantaṃ pathaviṃ anusaṃyāyanasamatthaṃ vehāsaṅgamaṃ hatthiratanaṃ, tādisameva assaratanaṃ, caturaṅgasamannāgate andhakāre yojanappamāṇaṃ andhakāraṃ vidhamitvā ālokadassanasamatthaṃ maṇiratanaṃ, chabbidhadosavivajjitaṃ manāpacāri itthiratanaṃ, yojanappamāṇe antopathavigataṃ nidhiṃ dassanasamatthaṃ gahapatiratanaṃ, aggamahesiyā kucchimhi nibbattitvā sakalarajjamanusāsanasamatthaṃ jeṭṭhaputtasaṅkhātaṃ pariṇāyakaratanaṃ pātubhavati. tassimānī cakkaratana hatthiratanaṃ, assaratanaṃ, maṇiratanaṃ, itthiratanaṃ, gahapatiratanaṃ, pariṇāyakaratanaṃ Parosahassanti atirekasahassaṃ. Sūrāti abhīrukā. Vīraṅgarūpāti vīrānaṃ aṅgaṃ vīraṅgaṃ, vīriyassetaṃ nāmaṃ, vīraṅgaṃ rūpametesanti vīraṅgarūpā, vīriyajātikā vīriyasabhāvā vīriyamayā akilāsuno ahesuṃ. Divasampi yujjhantā na kilamantīti vuttaṃ hoti. Sāgarapariyantanti cakkavāḷapabbataṃ sīmaṃ katvā ṭhitasamuddapariyantaṃ. Adaṇḍenāti ye katāparādhe satte satampi sahassampi gaṇhanti, te dhanadaṇḍena rajjaṃ kārenti. Ye chejjabhejjaṃ anusāsanti, te satthadaṇḍena. Ayaṃ pana duvidhampi daṇḍaṃ pahāya adaṇḍena ajjhāvasati. Asatthenāti ye ekatodhārādinā satthena paraṃ vihesanti, te satthena rajjaṃ kārenti nāma. Ayaṃ pana satthena khuddamakkhikāyapi pivanamattaṃ lohitaṃ kassaci anuppādetvā dhammeneva – ‘‘ehi kho mahārājā’’ti evaṃ paṭirājūhi sampaṭicchitāgamano vuttappakāraṃ pathaviṃ abhivijinitvā ajjhāvasati, abhibhavitvā sāmī hutvā vasatīti attho. Parosahassa Sūrā Vīraṅgarūpā Sāgarapariyanta Adaṇḍenā Asatthenā Evaṃ ekaṃ nipphattiṃ kathetvā dutiyaṃ kathetuṃ sace kho panātiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha rāgadosamohamānadiṭṭhikilesataṇhāsaṅkhātaṃ chadanaṃ āvaraṇaṃ vivaṭaṃ viddhaṃsitaṃ vivaṭakaṃ etenāti vivaṭacchado. ‘‘Vivaṭṭacchadā’’tipi pāṭho, ayameva attho. sace kho panā vivaṭacchado.
33. "Addasa kho" means he saw, he saw the one brought after being laid down on a dukūla mat.
"Mahāpurisassa" means of a great man (mahāpurisa), due to birth, lineage, family, place, and so forth.
"Dve gatiyo" means two conclusions, two accomplishments.
Now, this word gati [means] – "There are these five destinations, Sāriputta" (Majjhima Nikāya, Aṅguttara Nikāya 1.153). Here, gati refers to the destination to be reached by beings, for example, the various hells.
"Imesaṃ kho ahaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ sīlavantānaṃ kalyāṇadhammānaṃ neva jānāmi āgatiṃ vā gatiṃ vā" (Majjhima Nikāya, Aṅguttara Nikāya 1.508) refers to their inclinations.
"Nibbānaṃ arahato gatī" (Pāri 339) refers to relying [on something].
"Api ca tyāhaṃ brahme gatiñca pajānāmi, jutiñca pajānāmi evaṃmahiddhiko bako Brahmā" (Majjhima Nikāya, Aṅguttara Nikāya 1.503) is related to the accomplishment [of something].
It should be understood that here too, it relates to accomplishment.
"Anaññā" means there is no other gati, accomplishment is just a name.

"Dhammiko" means possessing the ten wholesome qualities, free from going to bad destinations.
"Dhammarājā" means this is a synonym of the preceding word.
Or, because the Dharma King (dhammarājā) has obtained sovereignty through the Dharma.
"Cāturanto" means the lord of the earth (pathavī) bounded by the four oceans, beginning with the eastern ocean, on account of the four oceans.
"Vijitāvī" means victorious in battle (vijitasaṅgāmo).
Janapada having attained stability in this thāvariya means janapadatthāvariyappatto: one who has achieved stability in the territory.
Indeed, when a cruel king oppresses the world with forced levies and punishments, people abandon the central region (majjhimajanapada) and, relying on mountains, seashores, and the like, they establish their homes in outlying regions.
When a very gentle king is oppressed by thieves who violently plunder wealth, people abandon the outlying regions and establish their homes in the central region. Thus, the territory does not attain stability in such a king's reign.
However, when this prince makes the kingdom function, showing that his territory will be as stable as if it were placed on a stone slab and surrounded by an iron fence, they said, "He has achieved stability in the territory (janapadatthāvariyappatto)."

Dhammiko, Dhammarājā, Cāturanto, Vijitāvī, Janapadatthāvariyappatto.

"Sattaratanasamannāgato" means here, ratana [means] because of producing delight.
Moreover–

Sattaratanasamannāgato ratanaṃ.

‘‘Adorned, very valuable, incomparable, rarely seen;
The non-common enjoyment of beings, therefore it is called ratana."

And from the time of the arising of the wheel-jewel (cakkaratana), there is no other place of the gods, all make offerings and pay homage to it with fragrances, flowers, and the like. Therefore ratana [means] adorned.
And the value of the wheel-jewel is such that...
There is no price for a jewel.

And the wheel-jewel is dissimilar to other jewels existing in the world, therefore it is a jewel because of its matchlessness.

And since in whatever kappa Buddhas arise, in that very one cakravartins arise, and Buddhas arise only rarely, therefore it is a jewel because it is rarely seen.

This arises only for an excellent being who is uncommon due to birth, appearance, family, and power, and not for another; therefore it is a jewel because an uncommon being enjoys it.

Just as the wheel-jewel, so are the remaining ones.

Because he is endowed with these seven jewels as retinue and as means of enjoying all possessions, [he is called] one who is endowed with the seven jewels (sattaratnasamannāgata).


Now, in order to show their specific forms, it is said, "These are..."

There, in the [passages] beginning with "wheel-jewel," this is the abbreviated intention: the wheel jewel appears capable of taking the splendor and wealth of the four great islands that are surrounded by two thousand islands and giving it [to the cakravartin].

Likewise, the elephant-jewel (hastiratna) is capable of travelling over the earth up to the edge of the sea in time for the morning meal and moves through the sky; similarly, the horse-jewel (aśvaratna); the jewel-gem (maṇiratna) is capable of dispelling the darkness measuring a yojana and giving a view of light; the woman-jewel (strīratna) is free from six kinds of faults and behaves agreeably; the householder-jewel (gṛhapatiratna) is capable of showing the treasure hidden beneath the earth measuring a yojana; the counselor-jewel (pariṇāyakaratna), reckoned as the eldest son, having arisen in the womb of the chief queen, is capable of ruling the entire kingdom.

These are the wheel-jewel (cakkaratana), elephant-jewel (hastiratna), horse-jewel (aśvaratna), jewel-gem (maṇiratna), woman-jewel (strīratna), householder-jewel (gṛhapatiratna), and counselor-jewel (pariṇāyakaratna). The phrase parosahassanti means more than a thousand.

Sūrā means fearless.

Vīraṅgarūpā means vīrānaṃ aṅgaṃ vīraṅgaṃ, this is a name for energy; vīraṅgaṃ rūpametesanti vīraṅgarūpā, they were energetic by birth, energetic by nature, made of energy, and untiring.

It is said that even fighting during the day, they did not get tired.

Sāgarapariyanta means up to the ocean which stands having made the cakravāḷa mountain the boundary.

Adaṇḍenā means those who seize beings who have committed offenses, even a hundred or a thousand [lashes], they rule the kingdom with the punishment of wealth.

Those who instruct what should be cut and what should be broken, [do so] with the punishment of the weapon.

But this one, having abandoned even the two kinds of punishment, dwells without punishment.

Asatthenā means those who injure others with a weapon, beginning with a single-edged sword, they rule the kingdom with the weapon.

But this one, without producing even as much blood as the drinking of a small fly with a weapon, having conquered the earth in the manner described by dharma alone – "Come, great king!" – [and] whose arrival is welcomed by rival kings, dwells, having overcome [them], he lives as their lord, this is the meaning.

Parosahassa, sūrā, vīraṅgarūpā, sāgarapariyanta, adaṇḍenā, asatthenā

Thus, having spoken of one accomplishment, in order to speak of the second, it is said, "Sace kho panā, etc."

There, rāgadosamohamānadiṭṭhikilesataṇhāsaṅkhātaṃ chadanaṃ āvaraṇaṃ vivaṭaṃ viddhaṃsitaṃ vivaṭakaṃ etenāti vivaṭacchado means that with which covering, obstruction, openness, destruction, and the state of being uncovered, which are reckoned as passion, hatred, delusion, pride, [wrong] views, defilements, and craving, [are removed].

There is also a reading "vivaṭṭacchadā," this is the very meaning.

Sace kho panā vivaṭacchado.
34
34. ‘Katamehi cāyaṃ, deva, kumāro dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇehi samannāgato, yehi samannāgatassa mahāpurisassa dveva gatiyo bhavanti anaññā. Sace agāraṃ ajjhāvasati, rājā hoti cakkavattī dhammiko dhammarājā cāturanto vijitāpī janapadatthāvariyappatto sattaratanasamannāgato. Tassimāni sattaratanāni bhavanti . Seyyathidaṃ – cakkaratanaṃ hatthiratanaṃ assaratanaṃ maṇiratanaṃ itthiratanaṃ gahapatiratanaṃ pariṇāyakaratanameva sattamaṃ. Parosahassaṃ kho panassa puttā bhavanti sūrā vīraṅgarūpā parasenappamaddanā. So imaṃ pathaviṃ sāgarapariyantaṃ adaṇḍena asatthena dhammena abhivijiya ajjhāvasati. Sace kho pana agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajati, arahaṃ hoti sammāsambuddho loke vivaṭacchado.
‘And what, Sire, are these thirty-two marks with which the prince is endowed, due to which a great man has only two possible destinies?

If he lives the householder's life, he becomes a Wheel-turning Monarch ( cakravartin), a righteous king (dhārmika dharmarājā) who rules by the Dharma, master of the four quarters (cāturanta), all-victorious (vijitāpī), who has stabilised his country (janapadatthāvariyappatto) and possesses the seven treasures (sattaratanasamannāgato).

He has these seven treasures (sattaratnāni):

namely: the Wheel-Treasure (cakkaratanaṃ), the Elephant-Treasure (hatthiratanaṃ), the Horse-Treasure (assaratanaṃ), the Jewel-Treasure (maṇiratanaṃ), the Woman-Treasure (itthiratanaṃ), the Householder-Treasure (gahapatiratanaṃ), and the Counsellor-Treasure (pariṇāyakaratanameva) as the seventh.

His sons, who exceed a thousand (parosahassaṃ), are brave (śūrā), heroic (vīraṅgarūpā), and crush the armies of others (parasenappamaddanā).

He dwells having conquered this sea-girt land (sāgarapariyantaṃ) without stick (adaṇḍena) or sword (asatthena), by the Dharma (dhammena).

But if he goes forth from the householder's life (agārasmā) into homelessness (anagāriyaṃ), then he will become an Arhat (arahaṃ), a Fully Enlightened Buddha (sammāsambuddho), one who draws back the veil (vivaṭacchado) from the world.
35
35. ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro suppatiṭṭhitapādo. Yaṃ pāyaṃ, deva, kumāro suppatiṭṭhitapādo. Idampissa mahāpurisassa mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ bhavati. ‘Imassa, deva imassa hi deva (?), kumārassa heṭṭhā pādatalesu cakkāni jātāni sahassārāni sanemikāni sanābhikāni sabbākāraparipūrāni. Yampi, imassa deva, kumārassa heṭṭhā pādatalesu cakkāni jātāni sahassārāni sanemikāni sanābhikāni sabbākāraparipūrāni, idampissa mahāpurisassa mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ bhavati. imassa hi deva (?) ‘Ayañhi deva, kumāro āyatapaṇhī…pe… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro dīghaṅgulī… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro mudutalunahatthapādo… ‘Ayañhi, deva kumāro jālahatthapādo… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro ussaṅkhapādo… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro eṇijaṅgho… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro ṭhitakova anonamanto ubhohi pāṇitalehi jaṇṇukāni parimasati parāmasati (ka.) parimajjati… parāmasati (ka.) ‘Ayañhi , deva, kumāro kosohitavatthaguyho… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro suvaṇṇavaṇṇo kañcanasannibhattaco… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro sukhumacchavī; sukhumattā chaviyā rajojallaṃ kāye na upalimpati upalippati (syā.) … upalippati (syā.) ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro ekekalomo; ekekāni lomāni lomakūpesu jātāni… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro uddhaggalomo; uddhaggāni lomāni jātāni nīlāni añjanavaṇṇāni kuṇḍalāvaṭṭāni dakkhiṇāvaṭṭakajātāni… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro brahmujugatto… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro sattussado… ‘Ayañhi , deva, kumāro sīhapubbaddhakāyo… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro citantaraṃso pitantaraṃso (syā.) … pitantaraṃso (syā.) ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro nigrodhaparimaṇḍalo yāvatakvassa kāyo tāvatakvassa byāmo, yāvatakvassa byāmo, tāvatakvassa kāyo… ‘Ayañhi , deva, kumāro samavaṭṭakkhandho… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro rasaggasaggī… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro sīhahanu… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro cattālīsadanto… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro samadanto… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro aviraḷadanto… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro susukkadāṭho… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro pahūtajivho… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro brahmassaro karavīkabhāṇī… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro abhinīlanetto… ‘Ayañhi, deva, kumāro gopakhumo… Imassa, deva, kumārassa uṇṇā bhamukantare jātā odātā mudutūlasannibhā. Yampi imassa deva kumārassa uṇṇā bhamukantare jātā odātā mudutūlasannibhā, idampimassa mahāpurisassa mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ bhavati. ‘Ayañhi , deva, kumāro uṇhīsasīso. Yaṃ pāyaṃ, deva, kumāro uṇhīsasīso, idampissa mahāpurisassa mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ bhavati.
35. Indeed, O deva, this prince has feet with level tread ( supratiṣṭhitapāda).

Whatever place he treads, O deva, this prince has feet with level tread ( supratiṣṭhitapāda).

This is, for this great man ( mahāpurisa), a mark of a great man ( mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ).

O deva, beneath this prince's feet, on the soles of his feet, wheels ( cakka ) have arisen, with a thousand spokes ( sahassāra ), with rims ( sanemika ), with hubs ( sanābhika ), complete in every way ( sabbākāraparipūra ).

And again, O deva, beneath this prince's feet, on the soles of his feet, wheels ( cakka ) have arisen, with a thousand spokes ( sahassāra ), with rims ( sanemika ), with hubs ( sanābhika ), complete in every way ( sabbākāraparipūra ), this is, for this great man ( mahāpurisa), a mark of a great man ( mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has long heels ( āyatapaṇhī )...pe…

Indeed, O deva, this prince has long fingers ( dīghaṅgulī).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has soft and tender hands and feet (mudutalunahatthapāda).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has webbed hands and feet (jālahatthapāda).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has ankles that do not protrude ( ussaṅkhapāda).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has legs like a deer (eṇijaṅgha).

Indeed, O deva, this prince, without bending over while standing, strokes his knees with both palms...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has his genitals withdrawn into their sheath ( kosohitavatthaguyha )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has skin the color of gold (suvaṇṇavaṇṇa), like burnished gold (kañcanasannibhattaca).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has delicate skin ( sukhumacchavī); because of the delicacy of his skin, no dust or dirt adheres to his body...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a single hair in each pore (ekekaloma); a single hair has grown in each pore ( lomakūpesu jātāni )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has hair that grows upwards (uddhaggaloma); the hairs have grown upwards, they are blue (nīla), the color of añjana, curling, turning to the right ( dakkhiṇāvaṭṭakajātāni )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a divinely straight body (brahmujugatta).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a voice like Brahmā's (brahmassaro).

Indeed, O deva, the front part of his body is like a lion's ( sīhapubbaddhakāya )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a concealed penis ( citantaraṃsa )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has the proportions of a banyan tree ( nigrodhaparimaṇḍala ): his height is equal to his arm span, and his arm span is equal to his height...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has level shoulders ( samavaṭṭakkhandha )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has excellent taste ( rasaggasaggī )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has jaws like a lion ( sīhahanu )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has forty teeth ( cattālīsadanto )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has even teeth (samadanto).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has teeth without gaps ( aviraḷadanto )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has very white canine teeth ( susukkadāṭho )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a large tongue ( pahūtajivho )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a voice like Brahmā ( brahmassaro ), speaking like a karavīka bird...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has very blue eyes ( abhinīlanetto )...

Indeed, O deva, this prince has eyelashes like a cow’s (gopakhumo).

O deva, this prince has an ūrṇā growing between his eyebrows, white, like soft cotton.

And again, O deva, this prince has an ūrṇā growing between his eyebrows, white, like soft cotton, this is, for this great man ( mahāpurisa ), a mark of a great man ( mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ ).

Indeed, O deva, this prince has a head shaped like a turban ( uṇhīsasīso ).

Whatever place he treads, O deva, this prince has a head shaped like a turban ( uṇhīsasīso ), this is, for this great man ( mahāpurisa), a mark of a great man ( mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ).
35. Evaṃ dutiyaṃ nipphattiṃ kathetvā tāsaṃ nimittabhūtāni lakkhaṇāni dassetuṃ ayañhi, deva, kumārotiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha suppatiṭṭhitapādoti yathā aññesaṃ bhūmiyaṃ pādaṃ ṭhapentānaṃ aggapādatalaṃ vā paṇhi vā passaṃ vā paṭhamaṃ phusati, vemajjhe vā pana chiddaṃ hoti, ukkhipantānaṃ aggatalādīsu ekakoṭṭhāsova paṭhamaṃ uṭṭhahati, na evamassa. Assa pana suvaṇṇapādukatalamiva ekappahāreneva sakalaṃ pādatalaṃ bhūmiṃ phusati, ekappahāreneva bhūmito uṭṭhahati. Tasmā ayaṃ suppatiṭṭhitapādo. Cakkānīti dvīsu pādatalesu dve cakkāni, tesaṃ arā ca nemi ca nābhi ca pāḷiyaṃ vuttāva. Sabbākāraparipūrānīti iminā pana ayaṃ viseso veditabbo, tesaṃ kira cakkānaṃ pādatalassa majjhe nābhi dissati, nābhiparicchinnā vaṭṭalekhā dissati, nābhimukhaparikkhepapaṭṭo dissati, panāḷimukhaṃ dissati, arā dissanti, aresu vaṭṭilekhā dissanti, nemimaṇikā dissanti. Idaṃ tāva pāḷiyaṃ āgatameva. Sambahulavāro pana anāgato, so evaṃ daṭṭhabbo – satti, sirivaccho, nandi, sovattiko, vaṭaṃsako, vaḍḍhamānakaṃ, macchayugaḷaṃ, bhaddapīṭhaṃ, aṅkusako, pāsādo, toraṇaṃ, setacchattaṃ, khaggo, tālavaṇṭaṃ, morahatthako, vāḷabījanī, uṇhīsaṃ, maṇi, patto, sumanadāmaṃ, nīluppalaṃ, rattuppalaṃ, setuppalaṃ, padumaṃ, puṇḍarīkaṃ, puṇṇaghaṭo , puṇṇapāti, samuddo, cakkavāḷo, himavā, sineru, candimasūriyā, nakkhattāni, cattāro mahādīpā, dviparittadīpasahassāni, antamaso cakkavattirañño parisaṃ upādāya sabbo cakkalakkhaṇasseva parivāro. Cakkānī Sabbākāraparipūrānī Āyatapaṇhīti dīghapaṇhi, paripuṇṇapaṇhīti attho. Yathā hi aññesaṃ aggapādo dīgho hoti, paṇhimatthake jaṅghā patiṭṭhāti, paṇhiṃ tacchetvā ṭhapitā viya hoti, na evaṃ mahāpurisassa. Mahāpurisassa pana catūsu koṭṭhāsesu dve koṭṭhāsā aggapādo hoti, tatiye koṭṭhāse jaṅghā patiṭṭhāti, catutthakoṭṭhāse āraggena vaṭṭetvā ṭhapitā viya rattakambalageṇḍukasadisā paṇhi hoti. Āyatapaṇhī Dīghaṅgulīti yathā aññesaṃ kāci aṅguliyo dīghā honti, kāci rassā, na evaṃ mahāpurisassa. Mahāpurisassa pana makkaṭasseva dīghā hatthapādaṅguliyo mūle thūlā, anupubbena gantvā agge tanukā, niyyāsatelena madditvā vaṭṭitaharitālavaṭṭisadisā honti. Tena vuttaṃ – ‘‘dīghaṅgulī’’ti. Dīghaṅgulī Mudutalunahatthapādoti sappimaṇḍe osāretvā ṭhapitaṃ satavāravihatakappāsapaṭalaṃ viya mudu. Yathā ca idāni jātamattassa, evaṃ vuḍḍhakālepi mudutalunāyeva bhavissanti, mudutalunā hatthapādā etassāti mudutalunahatthapādo. Mudutalunahatthapādo Jālahatthapādoti na cammena paṭibaddhaaṅgulantaro. Ediso hi phaṇahatthako purisadosena upahato pabbajjaṃ na paṭilabhati. Mahāpurisassa pana catasso hatthaṅguliyo pañcapi pādaṅguliyo ekappamāṇā honti, tāsaṃ ekappamāṇatāya yavalakkhaṇaṃ aññamaññaṃ paṭivijjhitvā tiṭṭhati. Athassa hatthapādā kusalena vaḍḍhakinā yojitajālavātapānasadisā honti. Tena vuttaṃ – ‘‘jālahatthapādo’’ti. Jālahatthapādo Uddhaṃ patiṭṭhitagopphakattā ussaṅkhā pādā assāti ussaṅkhapādo. Aññesañhi piṭṭhipāde gopphakā honti, tena tesaṃ pādā āṇibaddhā viya baddhā honti, na yathāsukhaṃ parivaṭṭanti, gacchantānaṃ pādatalānipi na dissanti. Mahāpurisassa pana āruhitvā upari gopphakā patiṭṭhahanti, tenassa nābhito paṭṭhāya uparimakāyo nāvāya ṭhapitasuvaṇṇapaṭimā viya niccalo hoti, adhokāyova iñjati, sukhena pādā parivaṭṭanti, puratopi pacchatopi ubhayapassesupi ṭhatvā passantānaṃ pādatalāni paññāyanti, na hatthīnaṃ viya pacchatoyeva. ussaṅkhapādo. Eṇijaṅghoti eṇimigasadisajaṅgho maṃsussadena paripuṇṇajaṅgho, na ekato baddhapiṇḍikamaṃso , samantato samasaṇṭhitena maṃsena parikkhittāhi suvaṭṭitāhi sāligabbhayavagabbhasadisāhi jaṅghāhi samannāgatoti attho. Eṇijaṅgho Anonamantoti anamanto, etenassa akhujjaavāmanabhāvo dīpito. Avasesajanā hi khujjā vā honti vāmanā vā. Khujjānaṃ uparimakāyo aparipuṇṇo hoti, vāmanānaṃ heṭṭhimakāyo. Te aparipuṇṇakāyattā na sakkonti anonamantā jaṇṇukāni parimajjituṃ. Mahāpuriso pana paripuṇṇaubhayakāyattā sakkoti. Anonamanto Kosohitavatthaguyhoti usabhavāraṇādīnaṃ viya suvaṇṇapadumakaṇṇikasadisehi kosehi ohitaṃ paṭicchannaṃ vatthaguyhaṃ assāti kosohitavatthaguyho. Vatthaguyhanti vatthena guhitabbaṃ aṅgajātaṃ vuccati. Kosohitavatthaguyho Vatthaguyha Suvaṇṇavaṇṇoti jātihiṅgulakena majjitvā dīpidāṭhāya ghaṃsitvā gerukaparikammaṃ katvā ṭhapitaghanasuvaṇṇarūpasadisoti attho. Etenassa ghanasiniddhasaṇhasarīrataṃ dassetvā chavivaṇṇadassanatthaṃ kañcanasannibhattacoti vuttaṃ. Purimassa vā vevacanametaṃ. Suvaṇṇavaṇṇo kañcanasannibhattaco Rajojallanti rajo vā malaṃ vā. Na upalimpatīti na laggati padumapalāsato udakabindu viya vivaṭṭati. Hatthadhovanādīni pana utuggahaṇatthāya ceva dāyakānaṃ puññaphalatthāya ca buddhā karonti, vattasīsenāpi ca karontiyeva. Senāsanaṃ pavisantena hi bhikkhunā pāde dhovitvā pavisitabbanti vuttametaṃ. Rajojalla Na upalimpatī Uddhaggalomoti āvaṭṭapariyosāne uddhaggāni hutvā mukhasobhaṃ ullokayamānāni viya ṭhitāni lomāni assāti uddhaggalomo. Uddhaggalomo Brahmujugattoti brahmā viya ujugatto, ujumeva uggatadīghasarīro bhavissati. Yebhuyyena hi sattā khandhe kaṭiyaṃ jāṇūsūti tīsu ṭhānesu namanti, te kaṭiyaṃ namantā pacchato namanti, itaresu dvīsu ṭhānesu purato. Dīghasarīrā pana eke passavaṅkā honti, eke mukhaṃ unnametvā nakkhattāni gaṇayantā viya caranti, eke appamaṃsalohitā sūlasadisā honti, eke purato pabbhārā honti, pavedhamānā gacchanti. Ayaṃ pana ujumeva uggantvā dīghappamāṇo devanagare ussitasuvaṇṇatoraṇaṃ viya bhavissatīti dīpenti. Yathā cetaṃ, evaṃ yaṃ yaṃ jātamattassa sabbaso aparipuṇṇaṃ mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ hoti, taṃ taṃ āyatiṃ tathābhāvitaṃ sandhāya vuttanti veditabbaṃ. Brahmujugatto Sattussadoti dve hatthapiṭṭhiyo dve pādapiṭṭhiyo dve aṃsakūṭāni khandhoti imesu sattasu ṭhānesu paripuṇṇo maṃsussado assāti sattussado. Aññesaṃ pana hatthapādapiṭṭhādīsu sirājālaṃ paññāyati, aṃsakūṭakkhandhesu aṭṭhikoṭiyo. Te manussā petā viya khāyanti, na tathā mahāpuriso, mahāpuriso pana sattasu ṭhānesu paripuṇṇamaṃsussadattā nigūḷhasirājālehi hatthapiṭṭhādīhi vaṭṭetvā suṭṭhapitasuvaṇṇāḷiṅgasadisena khandhena silārūpakaṃ viya khāyati, cittakammarūpakaṃ viya ca khāyati. Sattussado Sīhassa pubbaddhaṃ viya kāyo assāti sīhapubbaddhakāyo. Sīhassa hi puratthimakāyova paripuṇṇo hoti, pacchimakāyo aparipuṇṇo. Mahāpurisassa pana sīhassa pubbaddhakāyo viya sabbo kāyo paripuṇṇo. Sopi sīhasseva tattha tattha vinatunnatādivasena dussaṇṭhitavisaṇṭhito na hoti, dīghayuttaṭṭhāne pana dīgho, rassathūlakisaputhulaanuvaṭṭitayuttaṭṭhānesu tathāvidhova hoti. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā – sīhapubbaddhakāyo. ‘‘Manāpiyeva kho, bhikkhave, kammavipāke paccupaṭṭhite yehi aṅgehi dīghehi sobhati, tāni aṅgāni dīghāni saṇṭhanti. Yehi aṅgehi rassehi sobhati, tāni aṅgāni rassāni saṇṭhanti. Yehi aṅgehi thūlehi sobhati, tāni aṅgāni thūlāni saṇṭhanti. Yehi aṅgehi kisehi sobhati, tāni aṅgāni kisāni saṇṭhanti. Yehi aṅgehi puthulehi sobhati, tāni aṅgāni puthulāni saṇṭhanti. Yehi aṅgehi vaṭṭehi sobhati, tāni aṅgāni vaṭṭāni saṇṭhantī’’ti. Iti nānācittena puññacittena cittito dasahi pāramīhi sajjito mahāpurisassa attabhāvo, loke sabbasippino vā sabbaiddhimanto vā patirūpakampi kātuṃ na sakkonti. Citantaraṃsoti antaraṃsaṃ vuccati dvinnaṃ koṭṭānaṃ antaraṃ, taṃ citaṃ paripuṇṇaṃ antaraṃsaṃ assāti citantaraṃso. Aññesañhi taṃ ṭhānaṃ ninnaṃ hoti, dve piṭṭhikoṭṭā pāṭiyekkā paññāyanti. Mahāpurisassa pana kaṭito paṭṭhāya maṃsapaṭalaṃ yāva khandhā uggamma samussitasuvaṇṇaphalakaṃ viya piṭṭhiṃ chādetvā patiṭṭhitaṃ. Citantaraṃso Nigrodhaparimaṇḍaloti nigrodho viya parimaṇḍalo. Yathā paññāsahatthatāya vā satahatthatāya vā samakkhandhasākho nigrodho dīghatopi vitthāratopi ekappamāṇova hoti, evaṃ kāyatopi byāmatopi ekappamāṇo. Yathā aññesaṃ kāyo dīgho vā hoti byāmo vā, na evaṃ visamappamāṇoti attho. Teneva yāvatakvassa kāyotiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha yāvatako assāti yāvatakvassa. Nigrodhaparimaṇḍalo yāvatakvassa kāyo yāvatakvassa. Samavaṭṭakkhandhoti samavaṭṭitakkhandho. Yathā eke koñcā viya ca bakā viya ca varāhā viya ca dīghagalā vaṅkagalā puthulagalā ca honti , kathanakāle sirājālaṃ paññāyati, mando saro nikkhamati, na evaṃ mahāpurisassa. Mahāpurisassa pana suvaṭṭitasuvaṇṇāḷiṅgasadiso khandho hoti, kathanakāle sirājālaṃ na paññāyati, meghassa viya gajjito saro mahā hoti. Samavaṭṭakkhandho Rasaggasaggīti ettha rasaṃ gasanti harantīti rasaggasā. Rasaharaṇīnametaṃ adhivacanaṃ, tā aggā assāti rasaggasaggī. Mahāpurisassa kira sattarasaharaṇīsahassāni uddhaggāni hutvā gīvāyameva paṭimukkāni. Tilaphalamattopi āhāro jivhagge ṭhapito sabbakāyaṃ anupharati. Teneva mahāpadhānaṃ padahantassa ekataṇḍulādīhipi kaḷāyayūsapasatamattenāpi kāyassa yāpanaṃ ahosi. Aññesaṃ pana tathā abhāvā na sakalaṃ kāyaṃ ojā pharati. Tena te bahvābādhā honti. Rasaggasaggī Sīhasseva hanu assāti sīhahanu. Tattha sīhassa heṭṭhimahanumeva paripuṇṇaṃ hoti, na uparimaṃ. Mahāpurisassa pana sīhassa heṭṭhimaṃ viya dvepi paripuṇṇāni dvādasiyā pakkhassa candasadisāni honti. Atha nemittakā hanukapariyantaṃ olokentāva imesu hanukesu heṭṭhime vīsati uparime vīsatīti cattālīsadantā samā aviraḷā patiṭṭhahissantīti sallakkhetvā ayañhi deva, kumāro cattālīsadanto hotītiādimāhaṃsu. Tatrāyamattho, aññesañhi paripuṇṇadantānampi dvattiṃsa dantā honti. Imassa pana cattālīsaṃ bhavissanti. Aññesañca keci dantā uccā, keci nīcāti visamā honti, imassa pana ayapaṭṭakena chinnasaṅkhapaṭalaṃ viya samā bhavissanti. Aññesaṃ kumbhilānaṃ viya dantā viraḷā honti, macchamaṃsāni khādantānaṃ dantantaraṃ pūrenti. Imassa pana kanakaphalakāyaṃ samussitavajirapanti viya aviraḷā tūlikāya dassitaparicchedā viya dantā bhavissanti. Aññesañca pūtidantā uṭṭhahanti. Tena kāci dāṭhā kāḷāpi vivaṇṇāpi honti. Ayaṃ pana suṭṭhu sukkadāṭho osadhitārakampi atikkamma virocamānāya pabhāya samannāgatadāṭho bhavissati. sīhahanu. ayañhi deva, kumāro cattālīsadanto hotī Pahūtajivhoti puthulajivho. Aññesaṃ jivhā thūlāpi honti kisāpi rassāpi thaddhāpi visamāpi, mahāpurisassa pana jivhā mudu dīghā puthulā vaṇṇasampannā hoti. So hi etaṃ lakkhaṇaṃ pariyesituṃ āgatānaṃ kaṅkhāvinodanatthaṃ mudukattā taṃ jivhaṃ kathinasūciṃ viya vaṭṭetvā ubho nāsikasotāni parāmasati, dīghattā ubho kaṇṇasotāni parāmasati , puthulattā kesantapariyosānaṃ kevalampi nalāṭaṃ paṭicchādeti. Evamassa mududīghaputhulabhāvaṃ pakāsento tesaṃ kaṅkhaṃ vinodeti. Evaṃ tilakkhaṇasampannaṃ jivhaṃ sandhāya ‘‘pahūtajivho’’ti vuttaṃ. Pahūtajivho Brahmassaroti aññe chinnassarāpi bhinnassarāpi kākassarāpi honti, ayaṃ pana mahābrahmuno sarasadisena sarena samannāgato bhavissati, mahābrahmuno hi pittasemhehi apalibuddhattā saro visado hoti. Mahāpurisenāpi katakammaṃ tassa vatthuṃ sodheti. Vatthuno suddhattā nābhito paṭṭhāya samuṭṭhahanto saro visado aṭṭhaṅgasamannāgatova samuṭṭhāti. Karavīko viya bhaṇatīti karavīkabhāṇī, mattakaravīkarutamañjughosoti attho. Brahmassaro karavīkabhāṇī, Abhinīlanettoti na sakalanīlanetto, nīlayuttaṭṭhāne panassa umāpupphasadisena ativisuddhena nīlavaṇṇena samannāgatāni nettāni honti, pītayuttaṭṭhāne kaṇikārapupphasadisena pītavaṇṇena, lohitayuttaṭṭhāne bandhujīvakapupphasadisena lohitavaṇṇena, setayuttaṭṭhāne osadhitārakasadisena setavaṇṇena, kāḷayuttaṭṭhāne addāriṭṭhakasadisena kāḷavaṇṇena samannāgatāni. Suvaṇṇavimāne ugghāṭitamaṇisīhapañjarasadisāni khāyanti. Abhinīlanetto Gopakhumoti ettha pakhumanti sakalacakkhubhaṇḍaṃ adhippetaṃ, taṃ kāḷavacchakassa bahaladhātukaṃ hoti, rattavacchakassa vippasannaṃ, taṃmuhuttajātataruṇarattavacchakasadisacakkhubhaṇḍoti attho. Aññesañhi cakkhubhaṇḍā aparipuṇṇā honti, hatthimūsikādīnaṃ akkhisadisehi viniggatehipi gambhīrehipi akkhīhi samannāgatā honti. Mahāpurisassa pana dhovitvā majjitvā ṭhapitamaṇiguḷikā viya mudusiniddhanīlasukhumapakhumācitāni akkhīni. Gopakhumo Uṇṇāti uṇṇalomaṃ. Bhamukantareti dvinnaṃ bhamukānaṃ vemajjhe nāsikamatthakeyeva jātā, uggantvā pana nalāṭavemajjhe jātā. Odātāti parisuddhā, osadhitārakasamānavaṇṇā. Mudūti sappimaṇḍe osāretvā ṭhapitasatavāravihatakappāsapaṭalasadisā. Tūlasannibhāti simbalitūlalatātūlasamānā, ayamassa odātatāya upamā. Sā panesā koṭiyaṃ gahetvā ākaḍḍhiyamānā upaḍḍhabāhuppamāṇā hoti, vissaṭṭhā dakkhiṇāvaṭṭavasena āvaṭṭitvā uddhaggā hutvā santiṭṭhati. Suvaṇṇaphalakamajjhe ṭhapitarajatapubbuḷakaṃ viya, suvaṇṇaghaṭato nikkhamamānā khīradhārā viya, aruṇappabhārañjite gaganappadese osadhitārakā viya ca atimanoharāya siriyā virocati. Uṇṇā Bhamukantare Odātā Mudū Tūlasannibhā Uṇhīsasīsoti idaṃ paripuṇṇanalāṭatañca paripuṇṇasīsataṃ cāti dve atthavase paṭicca vuttaṃ. Mahāpurisassa hi dakkhiṇakaṇṇacūḷikato paṭṭhāya maṃsapaṭalaṃ uṭṭhahitvā sakalanalāṭaṃ chādayamānaṃ pūrayamānaṃ gantvā vāmakaṇṇacūḷikāyaṃ patiṭṭhitaṃ, taṃ rañño bandhauṇhīsapaṭṭo viya virocati. Mahāpurisassa kira imaṃ lakkhaṇaṃ disvā rājūnaṃ uṇhīsapaṭṭaṃ akaṃsu. Ayaṃ tāva eko attho. Aññe pana janā aparipuṇṇasīsā honti, keci kapisīsā, keci phalasīsā, keci aṭṭhisīsā, keci hatthisīsā, keci tumbasīsā, keci pabbhārasīsā. Mahāpurisassa pana āraggena vaṭṭetvā ṭhapitaṃ viya suparipuṇṇaṃ udakapubbuḷasadisaṃ sīsaṃ hoti. Tattha purimanaye uṇhīsaveṭhitasīso viyāti uṇhīsasīso. Dutiyanaye uṇhīsaṃ viya sabbattha parimaṇḍalasīsoti uṇhīsasīso. Uṇhīsasīso Vipassīsamaññāvaṇṇanā
35. Thus, having spoken of the second accomplishment, the marks that are its causes are shown by saying, "Indeed, this, O deva, is a prince," and so forth.

There, supratiṣṭhitapādo means that, just as when others place their foot on the ground, the tip of the sole, the heel, or the side touches first, or there is a gap in the middle, and when they lift it, only one section of the tip of the sole, etc., rises first, it is not so with him.

But for him, like the sole of a golden slipper, the entire sole touches the ground with a single strike, and with a single strike it rises from the ground.

Therefore, he is supratiṣṭhitapādo.

Cakkānī [means that] on the two soles of the feet there are two wheels, and their spokes, rims, and hubs have already been spoken of in the Pāli.

Sarvākāraparipūrānī means that this distinction should be understood in this way: it seems that in the middle of the sole of the feet of those wheels, a hub is seen, a circular line encircled by the hub is seen, a band surrounding the face of the hub is seen, a lotus-stalk mouth is seen, spokes are seen, circular lines in the spokes are seen, and rim jewels are seen.

This much, indeed, has come down in the Pāli itself.

But many other details have not come down, so they should be understood thus: the śakti, śrīvatsa, nandin, sovattika, vaṭaṃsaka, vaḍḍhamānaka, pair of fish, auspicious seat, goad, palace, archway, white umbrella, sword, palm frond, peacock feather, fan of horse-tail, turban, jewel, bowl, garland of flowers, blue lotus, red lotus, white lotus, padma, puṇḍarīka, full vase, full bowl, ocean, cakravāḷa mountain range, Himavat, Sumeru, sun and moon, constellations, the four great continents, two thousand minor continents—in short, the entire retinue belongs to the characteristics of a cakravartin, beginning with his council.

Cakkānī
Sarvākāraparipūrānī
Āyatapaṇhī means dīghapaṇhi, complete heel; this is the meaning.

Just as for others, the tip of the foot is long, the shank rests on the top of the heel, the heel is as if carved and placed there, it is not so for a great man (mahāpuruṣa).

But for a mahāpuruṣa, in the four sections, two sections are the tip of the foot, in the third section the shank rests, and in the fourth section the heel, rounded with a spoke, as if placed there, is like a ball of red wool.

Āyatapaṇhī
Dīghaṅgulī means that just as for others, some fingers are long and some are short, it is not so for a mahāpuruṣa.

But for a mahāpuruṣa, like a monkey's, the fingers and toes are long, thick at the base, gradually thinning as they go to the tip, like sticks of haritāla that have been kneaded with niyyāsa oil and rolled.

Therefore it was said, "Long fingers."

Dīghaṅgulī
Mudutalunahatthapādo means soft, like a layer of cotton that has been dipped in clarified butter and laid down after being beaten hundreds of times.

And just as they are soft now when he is first born, so they will be soft and tender even in old age; because his hands and feet are soft and tender, he is mudutalunahatthapādo.

Mudutalunahatthapādo
Jālahatthapādo means that the spaces between the fingers are not joined by skin.

For someone with such webbed hands, afflicted by the fault of being a male, does not obtain ordination (pravrajyā).

But for a mahāpuruṣa, the four fingers of the hands and the five toes are of equal length; because of the equal length of those fingers and toes, the barley mark stands piercing one another.

Moreover, his hands and feet are like latticed windows joined by a skilled carpenter.

Therefore it was said, "Jālahatthapādo."

Jālahatthapādo
Because the heels are high, the feet are ussaṅkha; therefore, he is ussaṅkhapādo.

For others, the ankles are at the back of the foot, so their feet are as if bound by pins, they do not turn around easily, and the soles of their feet are not seen by those who are walking.

But for a mahāpuruṣa, the ankles, having risen up, rest on top; therefore, beginning from his navel, his upper body is motionless, like a golden statue placed on a boat, only the lower body moves, and the feet easily turn around; the soles of his feet are seen by those who stand in front, behind, and on both sides, not only from behind, as with elephants.

Ussaṅkhapādo.

Eṇijaṅgho means shanks like those of an antelope, shanks that are full because of the abundance of flesh, not with the muscle of the calf bound together in one place, but surrounded on all sides by evenly arranged flesh, with shanks that are well-rounded like the core of sāli rice or the core of barley; this is the meaning.

Eṇijaṅgho
Anonamanto means not bent down; by this, his state of not being hunchbacked or dwarfish is shown.
Other people are either hunchbacks (khujja) or dwarves (vāmana).
The upper part of the body of hunchbacks is incomplete, while the lower part of the body of dwarves is [incomplete].
Because they have incomplete bodies, they cannot caress their knees without bending down.
But a great man (mahāpurisa) is able to, because he has a completely full body.

"Not bending down (an-onamanto)" [means:] "whose private parts are covered by a sheath (kosohitavatthaguyho)," that is, whose private parts are covered and concealed by sheaths similar to the golden lotus pericarp of bulls, elephants, etc.; therefore, [he is called] "whose private parts are covered by a sheath (kosohitavatthaguyho)."
"Private parts (vatthaguyha)" means the genitals (aṅgajāta) that should be covered by cloth (vatthena guhitabbaṃ).

"Whose private parts are covered by a sheath (kosohitavatthaguyho)," "private parts (vatthaguyha)," "golden-colored (suvaṇṇavaṇṇo)" means that the meaning is like a solid golden statue that has been reddened by being soaked with realgar (jātihiṅgulakena), rubbed with a Dīpi-thorn (dīpidāṭhāya), and treated with red chalk (gerukaparikammaṃ katvā).
Having shown the solidity (ghana), smoothness (siniddha), and tenderness (saṇha) of his body (sarīrataṃ), in order to show the color of his skin (chavivaṇṇadassanatthaṃ), it is said [that he has] "skin like gold (kañcanasannibhattaco)."
Or this is a synonym of the former.

"Golden-colored (suvaṇṇavaṇṇo)," "skin like gold (kañcanasannibhattaco)," "free from the dust and dirt (rajojalla)" [means:] dust or dirt.
"He is not defiled (na upalimpatī)" means it does not stick; it rolls off like a drop of water from a lotus leaf (padumapalāsato udakabindu viya vivaṭṭati).
But the Buddhas perform washing the hands, etc. (hatthadhovanādīni), in order to accept offerings and for the donors to obtain the fruit of merit, and the disciples also do it.
This was said because when entering a dwelling (senāsanaṃ), a monk (bhikkhunā) should wash his feet (pāde dhovitvā) and enter.

"Free from the dust and dirt (rajojalla)," "he is not defiled (na upalimpatī)," "with up-turned hairs (uddhaggalomo)" means he has hairs (lomāni) that, being turned upwards at the end of each whirl, stand as if looking up at the beauty of the face; therefore, [he is called] "with up-turned hairs (uddhaggalomo)."

"With a body that is straight like Brahmā (brahmujugatto)" means his body is straight like Brahmā's; he will have a tall, straight body (ujumeva uggatadīghasarīro).
For the most part (yebhuyyena), beings bend at three places—at the vertebrae (khandhe), at the hips (kaṭiyaṃ), and at the knees (jāṇūsūti); of those, bending at the hips, they bend backwards (pacchato namanti), and at the other two places, they bend forwards (purato).
But some with tall bodies are hunchbacked (passavaṅkā honti), some, raising their faces (mukhaṃ unnametvā), walk as if counting the stars (nakkhattāni gaṇayantā viya caranti), some have little flesh and blood (appamaṃsalohitā), [and] are like stakes (sūlasadisā honti), some have protruding chests (purato pabbhārā honti), [and] they go trembling (pavedhamānā gacchanti).
But this one (ayaṃ), rising straight up (ujumeva uggantvā), will have a tall stature (dīghappamāṇo), like a golden arch (ussitasuvaṇṇatoraṇaṃ) erected in the city of the gods (devanagare), so they say.
As this is, so whatever characteristic of a great man (mahāpurisalakkhaṇaṃ) is completely incomplete (sabbaso aparipuṇṇaṃ) at birth (jātamattassa), it should be understood (veditabbaṃ) that it was said in reference to his future development (tathābhāvitaṃ sandhāya).

"With a body that is straight like Brahmā (brahmujugatto)."

"Having seven convex surfaces (sattussado)" means he has fully rounded (paripuṇṇo) convex surfaces of flesh (maṃsussado) in these seven places (imesu sattasu ṭhānesu): two backs of the hands (dve hatthapiṭṭhiyo), two backs of the feet (dve pādapiṭṭhiyo), and two shoulder blades (dve aṃsakūṭāni), and his vertebrae (khandho); therefore, [he is called] "having seven convex surfaces (sattussado)."
But in others, a network of veins (sirājālaṃ paññāyati) is evident in the backs of the hands and feet, etc. (hatthapādapiṭṭhādīsu), [and there are] bony points (aṭṭhikoṭiyo) in the shoulder blades and vertebrae (aṃsakūṭakkhandhesu).
Those people (te manussā) appear like ghosts (petā viya khāyanti); the great man (mahāpuriso) is not like that (na tathā); but the great man (mahāpuriso), because he has fully rounded convex surfaces of flesh (paripuṇṇamaṃsussadattā) in seven places (sattasu ṭhānesu), and having covered (vaṭṭetvā) the backs of the hands and feet, etc. (hatthapiṭṭhādīhi) with hidden networks of veins (nigūḷhasirājālehi), with vertebrae (khandhena) like a well-erected (suṭṭhapita) golden pillar (suvaṇṇāḷiṅgasadisena), he appears (khāyati) like a stone statue (silārūpakaṃ viya), and he appears (khāyati) like a painted statue (cittakammarūpakaṃ viya ca).

"Having seven convex surfaces (sattussado)," "his body is like the forepart of a lion (sīhassa pubbaddhaṃ viya kāyo assāti)," therefore [he is called] "having the forepart of a lion as his body (sīhapubbaddhakāyo)."
For the forepart of a lion's body is fully rounded (paripuṇṇo hoti), but the hind part of the body is incomplete (aparipuṇṇo).
But the whole body (sabbo kāyo) of the great man (mahāpurisassa) is fully rounded (paripuṇṇo), like the forepart of a lion's body (sīhassa pubbaddhakāyo viya).
Even that one (so pi) is not badly shaped and deformed (dussaṇṭhitavisaṇṭhito), here and there, in dependence on being bent or raised (vinatunnatādivasena), just like a lion (sīhasseva); but in places appropriate to being long (dīghayuttaṭhāne), he is long (dīgho); and in places appropriate to being short (rassa), thick (thūla), thin (kisa), broad (puthula), or rounded (anuvaṭṭita), he is just like that (tathāvidhova hoti).
This was said by the Blessed One (bhagavatā):

"Having the forepart of a lion as his body (sīhapubbaddhakāyo)."

"Indeed, monks (bhikkhave), when the result of karma (kammavipāke) is present, those limbs (aṅgehi) by which [one] is beautiful (sobhati) with long [limbs] (dīghehi), those limbs (aṅgāni) are long (dīghāni saṇṭhanti).
Those limbs (aṅgehi) by which [one] is beautiful (sobhati) with short [limbs] (rassehi), those limbs (aṅgāni) are short (rassāni saṇṭhanti).
Those limbs (aṅgehi) by which [one] is beautiful (sobhati) with thick [limbs] (thūlehi), those limbs (aṅgāni) are thick (thūlāni saṇṭhanti).
Those limbs (aṅgehi) by which [one] is beautiful (sobhati) with thin [limbs] (kisehi), those limbs (aṅgāni) are thin (kisāni saṇṭhanti).
Those limbs (aṅgehi) by which [one] is beautiful (sobhati) with broad [limbs] (puthulehi), those limbs (aṅgāni)0449"/> They are broad and stand firm.
Those parts by which they are beautiful with circles, those parts, the circles, stand firm, it is said.
Thus, the essence of the great person, fashioned with diverse thoughts and meritorious thoughts, equipped with the ten pāramīs, cannot be fashioned even as an image by all the artisans or all the powerful ones in the world.
citantaraṃso means antaraṃsa is said to be the space between two koṭṭas; that which is collected, complete, antaraṃsa, is citantaraṃsa.
For others, that place is low, and two piṭṭhikoṭṭas are perceived as separate.
But for the great person, from the hips onward, a layer of flesh rises up to the shoulders, covering the back like an elevated golden plate, and is established.
citantaraṃso nigrodhaparimaṇḍalo means like a nyagrodha tree, round.
Just as a nyagrodha tree with equally branched limbs, whether fifty or one hundred hands in height, is of the same measure in length and breadth, so it is of the same measure in body and span.
As for others, the body is either long or a span, but it is not of unequal measure, is the meaning.
Therefore, yāvatakvassa kāyo etc., is said.
There, yāvatako assāti means yāvatakvassa.
nigrodhaparimaṇḍalo yāvatakvassa kāyo yāvatakvassa.
samavaṭṭakkhandho means samavaṭṭitakkhandho.
Just as some herons, cranes, boars, have long necks, bent necks, broad necks, and are , at the time of speaking, a network of veins is visible, a soft sound emerges, not so for the great person.
But for the great person, the shoulder is like a well-rounded golden bracelet, at the time of speaking, a network of veins is not visible, the sound is great, like the roar of a cloud.
samavaṭṭakkhandho rasaggasaggī means here, those that seize and carry away taste are rasaggasā.
This is a designation for the vessels of taste; they are foremost, therefore rasaggasaggī.
It is said that for the great person, seventeen thousand vessels of taste, having become upright, are fastened to the neck itself.
Even an amount of food the size of a sesame seed, placed on the tip of the tongue, pervades the entire body.
Therefore, for one maintaining the great foundation, even with one grain of rice etc., or with a hundred measures of kaḷāya soup, there was sustenance of the body.
But for others, due to the absence of that, ojas does not pervade the entire body.
Therefore, they have many diseases.
rasaggasaggī Like a lion, the jaw is his, therefore sīhahanu.
There, only the lower jaw of a lion is complete, not the upper.
But for the great person, like the lower jaw of a lion, both are complete, like the moon of the twelfth day of the pakṣa.
