Then, the nemittakas, looking around the entire jaw, recognizing that in these jaws, twenty in the lower and twenty in the upper, forty teeth, even, not sparse, will be established, said, This indeed, O deva, the youth has forty teeth, etc.
There, this is the meaning: for others, even those with complete teeth have thirty-two teeth.
But for him, there will be forty.
And for others, some teeth are high, some are low, and are uneven; but for him, like a conch shell cut with an iron blade, they will be even.
For others, the teeth are sparse, like those of kumbhilas; for those eating fish meat, they fill the spaces between the teeth.
But for him, like a row of raised vajras on a golden plate, the teeth will be non-sparse, like divisions shown by a brush.
And for others, rotten teeth arise.
Therefore, some canines are black or discolored.
But this one will have very white canines, surpassing even the osadhi star, possessing canines endowed with shining light.
Sīhahanu.

Ayañhi deva, kumāro cattālīsadanto hotī

pahūtajivhoti puthulajivho. Others have tongues that are thick, thin, short, stiff or uneven, but the great man's tongue is soft, long, broad, and beautifully colored. To dispel the doubts of those who have come to examine this mark, because of its softness, he rolls that tongue up like a hard needle and touches both nostrils; because of its length, he touches both ear-holes ; because of its breadth, he covers the entire forehead right up to the hair-line. Thus, by revealing its soft, long and broad nature, he dispels their doubt. Referring to a tongue possessed of these three characteristics, it was said: “He has a large tongue.”

pahūtajivho

Brahmassaroti Others have broken voices, cracked voices, or crow-like voices, but this great man will be endowed with a voice similar to the voice of Mahābrahmā. Mahābrahmā's voice is clear because it is unobstructed by phlegm and bile. The meritorious act performed by the great man purifies its basis. Because of the purity of the basis, a clear voice arises from the navel and arises only endowed with eight qualities. He speaks like a karavīka bird, therefore karavīkabhāṇī, meaning [he has] a voice as sweet as the cry of a drunken karavīka bird.

Brahmassaro karavīkabhāṇī,

Abhinīlanettoti not entirely blue-eyed, but his eyes, where blueness is appropriate, are endowed with an exceedingly pure blue color like an umā flower; where yellowness is appropriate, with a yellow color like a kaṇikāra flower; where redness is appropriate, with a red color like a bandhujīvaka flower; where whiteness is appropriate, with a white color like an Osadhi star; where blackness is appropriate, with a black color like an addāriṭṭhaka. They appear like jeweled lion cages revealed in a golden palace.

Abhinīlanetto

Gopakhumoti Here, pakhuma means the entire eyeball. That of a black calf has a thick texture, that of a red calf is very clear, meaning [he has] an eyeball like that of a young red calf just born. Others' eyeballs are imperfect; the eyes of elephants, mice, etc., are endowed with protruding or sunken eyes. But the great man's eyes are like polished and cleaned jewels, [his eyes are] filled with soft, smooth, blue, subtle eyelashes.

Gopakhumo

Uṇṇāti uṇṇalomaṃ (wool-hair).

Bhamukantareti born right on the nasal bridge between the two eyebrows, but having risen, born in the middle of the forehead.

Odātāti perfectly pure, having a color like an Osadhi star.

Mudūti like a hundred layers of carded cotton placed after being soaked in clarified butter.

Tūlasannibhāti similar to simbali cotton, latā cotton or tree cotton. This is a simile for its whiteness.

But that, when grasped at the tip and pulled, is about half a cubit long; when released it remains turning in a clockwise direction, its tip raised . It shines with an exceedingly lovely radiance, like a silver stud placed in the middle of a golden plate, like a stream of milk flowing out of a golden pitcher, and like an Osadhi star in a sky tinged with the light of dawn.

Uṇṇā Bhamukantare Odātā Mudū Tūlasannibhā

Uṇhīsasīsoti This was said with reference to two aspects: having a perfect forehead and having a perfect head. For in the great man, a layer of flesh rises up starting from the right ear-lobe, covering and filling the entire forehead, and is established at the left ear-lobe; it shines like a king's diadem-cloth. It is said that kings made diadem-cloths after seeing this mark of the great man. This, firstly, is one meaning. Others have imperfect heads: some have monkey-heads, some have fruit-heads, some have bone-heads, some have elephant-heads, some have gourd-heads, some have cliff-heads. But the great man has a very perfect head like a water-bubble, as if it had been rounded off with a lathe.
In that [first] method, uṇhīsa-sīso [means] as if (viya) the head (sīso) were wrapped (veṭhita) with a turban (uṇhīsa).

In the second method, uṇhīsa-sīso [means] the head (sīso) is perfectly round (parimaṇḍala) everywhere (sabbattha) like (viya) a turban (uṇhīsa).

Uṇhīsa-sīso. Commentary on the designation Vipassī.
36
36. ‘Imehi kho ayaṃ, deva, kumāro dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇehi samannāgato, yehi samannāgatassa mahāpurisassa dveva gatiyo bhavanti anaññā. Sace agāraṃ ajjhāvasati, rājā hoti cakkavattī dhammiko dhammarājā cāturanto vijitāvī janapadatthāvariyappatto sattaratanasamannāgato. Tassimāni sattaratanāni bhavanti. Seyyathidaṃ – cakkaratanaṃ hatthiratanaṃ assaratanaṃ maṇiratanaṃ itthiratanaṃ gahapatiratanaṃ pariṇāyakaratanameva sattamaṃ. Parosahassaṃ kho panassa puttā bhavanti sūrā vīraṅgarūpā parasenappamaddanā. So imaṃ pathaviṃ sāgarapariyantaṃ adaṇḍena asatthena dhammena dhammena samena (syā.) abhivijiya ajjhāvasati. Sace kho pana agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajati, arahaṃ hoti sammāsambuddho loke vivaṭacchado’ti. Vipassīsamaññā
“Sire, this prince is endowed with the thirty-two marks of a Great Man (dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇehi samannāgato). To such, only two courses are open.

If he lives the household life (agāraṃ ajjhāvasati), he will become a ruler, a wheel-turning righteous monarch of the law (rājā hoti cakkavattī dhammiko dhammarājā), conqueror of the four quarters (cāturanto vijitāvī), who has established the security of his realm (janapadatthāvariyappatto) and is possessed of the seven treasures (sattaratanasamannāgato).

He has these seven treasures (tassimāni sattaratanāni bhavanti): the wheel-treasure (cakkaratanaṃ), the elephant-treasure (hatthiratanaṃ), the horse-treasure (assaratanaṃ), the jewel-treasure (maṇiratanaṃ), the woman-treasure (itthiratanaṃ), the householder-treasure (gahapatiratanaṃ), and, as seventh, the counselor-treasure (pariṇāyakaratanameva sattamaṃ).

His children, who exceed a thousand (parosahassaṃ kho panassa puttā bhavanti), are brave (sūrā) and heroic, and crush the armies of others (vīraṅgarūpā parasenappamaddanā);

over this earth bounded by the ocean (imaṃ pathaviṃ sāgarapariyantaṃ), he rules without stick or sword (adaṇḍena asatthena), by means of the Dharma (dhammena) .

But if he goes forth from the household life into homelessness (agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajati), then he will become an Arhat (arahaṃ hoti), a fully-enlightened Buddha (sammāsambuddho), one who draws back the veil from the world (loke vivaṭacchado’ti).”

Vipassī’s designation.
37
37. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā nemitte brāhmaṇe ahatehi vatthehi acchādāpetvā acchādetvā (syā.) sabbakāmehi santappesi. Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā vipassissa kumārassa dhātiyo upaṭṭhāpesi. Aññā khīraṃ pāyenti, aññā nhāpenti, aññā dhārenti, aññā aṅkena pariharanti. Jātassa kho pana, bhikkhave, vipassissa kumārassa setacchattaṃ dhārayittha divā ceva rattiñca – ‘mā naṃ sītaṃ vā uṇhaṃ vā tiṇaṃ vā rajo vā ussāvo vā bādhayitthā’ti. Jāto kho pana, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bahuno janassa piyo ahosi manāpo. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, uppalaṃ vā padumaṃ vā puṇḍarīkaṃ vā bahuno janassa piyaṃ manāpaṃ; evameva kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bahuno janassa piyo ahosi manāpo. Svāssudaṃ aṅkeneva aṅkaṃ parihariyati.
37. Then, monks, King Bandhuma, having clothed those Brahmins learned in omens in fresh clothes, satisfied all their desires. Then, monks, King Bandhuma appointed nurses (dhātiyo) for Prince Vipassi. Some suckled him, some bathed him, some carried him, some dandled him in their laps (aṅkena pariharanti). When Prince Vipassi was born, monks, a white umbrella (setacchattaṃ) was held over him both day and night, thinking, 'May neither cold nor heat, grass, dust, urine, or anything else trouble him.' And Prince Vipassi was much beloved and liked by the people. Just as a blue lotus (uppalaṃ), a red lotus (padumaṃ) or a white lotus (puṇḍarīkaṃ) is loved and liked by many people, so too, monks, Prince Vipassi was loved and liked by many people. Thus he was borne from lap to lap.
37.Sabbakāmehīti idaṃ lakkhaṇāni pariggaṇhāpetvā pacchā kataṃ viya vuttaṃ, na panevaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Paṭhamañhi te nemittake santappetvā pacchā lakkhaṇapariggaṇhanaṃ katanti veditabbaṃ. Tassa vitthāro gabbhokkantiyaṃ vuttoyeva. Pāyentīti thaññaṃ pāyenti. Tassa kira niddosena madhurena khīrena samannāgatā saṭṭhi dhātiyo upaṭṭhāpesi, tathā sesāpi tesu tesu kammesu kusalā saṭṭhisaṭṭhiyeva. Tāsaṃ pesanakārake saṭṭhi purise, tassa tassa katākatabhāvaṃ sallakkhaṇe saṭṭhi amacce upaṭṭhāpesi. Evaṃ cattāri saṭṭhiyo itthīnaṃ, dve saṭṭhiyo purisānanti cha saṭṭhiyo upaṭṭhakānaṃyeva ahesuṃ. Setacchattanti dibbasetacchattaṃ. Kuladattiyaṃ pana sirigabbheyeva tiṭṭhati. Mā naṃ sītaṃ vātiādīsu mā abhibhavīti attho veditabbo. Svāssudanti so assudaṃ. Aṅkeneva aṅkanti aññassa bāhunāva aññassa bāhuṃ. Aññassa ca aṃsakūṭeneva aññassa aṃsakūṭaṃ. Parihariyatīti nīyati, sampāpiyatīti attho.
37. Sabbakāmehīti this was said as if the auspicious marks had been determined, and [only] then was the action performed, but it should not be regarded thus.

It should be understood that first he satisfied the omen readers, and only then did he determine the auspicious marks. Its detailed account has already been given in the chapter on conception.

Pāyentīti they cause [him] to drink breast milk. It seems that he had sixty nurses attending to him, who were endowed with faultless, sweet milk; likewise, there were sixty each who were skilled in those various tasks. He had sixty men to send them on errands, and sixty ministers to observe whether each task was done or not done. Thus, there were four sixties of women, and two sixties of men, so there were six sixties of attendants.

Setacchatta means a divine white umbrella. But the family umbrella remains in the auspicious chamber itself.

In mā naṃ sītaṃ vā etc., the meaning should be understood as "may it not overcome him."

Svāssuda means so assudaṃ.

Aṅkeneva aṅka means one person’s arm [supporting] another person’s arm. And one person’s shoulder blade [supporting] another person’s shoulder blade.

Parihariyatīti means it is led, it is brought near.
38
38. ‘‘Jāto kho pana, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro mañjussaro ca kumāro brahmassaro mañjussaro ca (sī. ka.) ahosi vaggussaro ca madhurassaro ca pemaniyassaro ca. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, himavante pabbate karavīkā nāma sakuṇajāti mañjussarā ca vaggussarā ca madhurassarā ca pemaniyassarā ca; evameva kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro mañjussaro ca ahosi vaggussaro ca madhurassaro ca pemaniyassaro ca.
‘And when he was born, monks, Prince Vipassī had a delightful voice (mañjussaro), and he had a beautiful voice (vaggussaro), and he had a sweet voice (madhurassaro), and he had an endearing voice (pemaniyassaro).

Just as, monks, in the Himālaya mountains, the karavīka bird, a species of bird, has a delightful voice (mañjussarā), and a beautiful voice (vaggussarā), and a sweet voice (madhurassarā), and an endearing voice (pemaniyassarā), even so, monks, Prince Vipassī had a delightful voice (mañjussaro), and he had a beautiful voice (vaggussaro), and he had a sweet voice (madhurassaro), and he had an endearing voice (pemaniyassaro).’
38.Mañjussaroti akharassaro. Vaggussaroti chekanipuṇassaro. Madhurassaroti sātassaro. Pemaniyassaroti pemajanakassaro. Tatridaṃ karavīkānaṃ madhurassaratāya – karavīkasakuṇe kira madhurarasaṃ ambapakkaṃ mukhatuṇḍakena paharitvā paggharitarasaṃ pivitvā pakkhena tālaṃ datvā vikūjamāne catuppadā mattā viya laḷituṃ ārabhanti. Gocarapasutāpi catuppadā mukhagatāni tiṇāni chaḍḍetvā taṃ saddaṃ suṇanti. Vāḷamigā khuddakamige anubandhamānā ukkhittaṃ pādaṃ anikkhipitvāva tiṭṭhanti. Anubaddhamigā ca maraṇabhayaṃ jahitvā tiṭṭhanti. Ākāse pakkhandā pakkhinopi pakkhe pasāretvā taṃ saddaṃ suṇamānāva tiṭṭhanti. Udake macchāpi kaṇṇapaṭalaṃ papphoṭetvā taṃ saddaṃ suṇamānāva tiṭṭhanti. Evaṃ madhurassarā karavīkā. Asandhimittāpi dhammāsokassa devī – ‘‘atthi nu kho, bhante, buddhassarena sadiso kassaci saro’’ti saṅghaṃ pucchi. Atthi karavīkasakuṇassāti. Kuhiṃ, bhante, te sakuṇāti? Himavanteti. Sā rājānaṃ āha – ‘‘deva, ahaṃ karavīkasakuṇaṃ passitukāmāmhī’’ti. Rājā – ‘‘imasmiṃ pañjare nisīditvā karavīko āgacchatū’’ti suvaṇṇapañjaraṃ vissajjesi. Pañjaro gantvā ekassa karavīkassa purato aṭṭhāsi. So – ‘‘rājāṇāya āgato pañjaro, na sakkā na gantu’’nti tattha nisīdi. Pañjaro āgantvā rañño purato aṭṭhāsi. Na karavīkasaddaṃ kārāpetuṃ sakkonti. Atha rājā – ‘‘kathaṃ, bhaṇe, ime saddaṃ na karontī’’ti āha. Ñātake adisvā devāti. Atha naṃ rājā ādāsehi parikkhipāpesi. So attano chāyaṃ disvā – ‘‘ñātakā me āgatā’’ti maññamāno pakkhena tālaṃ datvā madhurassarena maṇivaṃsaṃ dhamamāno viya viravi. Sakalanagare manussā mattā viya laḷiṃsu. Asandhimittā cintesi – ‘‘imassa tāva tiracchānagatassa evaṃ madhuro saddo, kīdiso nu kho sabbaññutaññāṇasiripattassa bhagavato saddo ahosī’’ti pītiṃ uppādetvā taṃ pītiṃ avijahitvā sattahi jaṅghasatehi saddhiṃ sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhāsi. Evaṃ madhuro kira karavīkasaddoti. Tato pana satabhāgena sahassabhāgena ca madhurataro vipassissa kumārassa saddo ahosīti veditabbo.
38. Mañjussaro means akharassaro.
Vaggussaro means chekanipuṇassaro.
Madhurassaro means sātassaro.
Pemaniyassaro means pemajanakassaro.
Here, regarding the sweetness of the karavīka birds: When the karavīka bird, having struck a ripe mango with its beak, and having drunk the dripping juice, claps its wings, four-footed creatures, as if intoxicated, begin to play.

Even four-footed creatures who are grazing, discarding the grass in their mouths, listen to that sound. Wild animals, following small animals, stand without putting down their raised feet. Anubaddha deer, abandoning the fear of death, stand still. In the sky, soaring birds, spreading their wings, stand listening to that sound. In the water, even fish, flapping their fins, stand listening to that sound. Thus are the karavīka birds sweet-sounding.

Asandhimittā, the queen of Dharmāśoka, asked the Saṅgha, "Is there any sound similar to the voice of the Buddha, O venerable ones?"

"There is the sound of the karavīka bird."

"Where, venerable ones, are those birds?"

"In the Himalayas."

She said to the king, "O King, I wish to see the karavīka bird."

The king released a golden cage, saying, "Having sat in this cage, may the karavīka bird come!"

The cage went and stood in front of one of the karavīka birds.

It thought, "The cage has come by royal command, it is not possible not to go," and sat down there.

The cage, having come, stood in front of the king.

They were not able to make it produce the karavīka sound.

Then the king said, "Why, friends, do they not make a sound?"

"O Queen, they do not see their relatives."

Then the king had mirrors placed all around it.

Seeing its own reflection, thinking "My relatives have come," it flapped its wings, clapped them in rhythm, and sang out in a sweet voice, like playing a jeweled flute.

In the entire city, people frolicked as if drunk.

Asandhimittā thought, "Since even this animal has such a sweet sound, what must the sound of the Blessed One, who is endowed with all-knowing wisdom and glorious virtues, have been like!" Having generated joy, without abandoning that joy, along with seven hundred jaṅghasatas, she was established in the fruit of stream-entry ( sotāpattiphala).

Thus, the karavīka sound is sweet indeed.

Moreover, the sound of Vipassī's son was a hundred times, a thousand times sweeter, it should be known.
39
39. ‘‘Jātassa kho pana, bhikkhave, vipassissa kumārassa kammavipākajaṃ dibbacakkhu pāturahosi yena sudaṃ yena dūraṃ (syā.) samantā yojanaṃ passati divā ceva rattiñca.
"And when Prince Vipaśyi was born, monks, due to the ripening of his karma, he manifested a divine eye (divyacakṣu), with which he saw a league (yojana) all around, both day (divā) and night (rātriñca)."
39.Kammavipākajanti na bhāvanāmayaṃ, kammavipākavasena pana devatānaṃ cakkhusadisameva maṃsacakkhu ahosi , yena nimittaṃ katvā tilavāhe pakkhittaṃ ekatilampi ayaṃ soti uddharitvā dātuṃ sakkoti.
kammavipākaja nti [is] not bhāvanāmaya. But due to the power of the ripening of karma (karmavipāka), the fleshy eye (maṃsacakkhu) of the gods (devatā) became just like the eye (cakkhu). Having made that an occasion (nimitta), even a single sesame seed (ekatila) thrown into a heap of sesame seeds (tilavāha), this soti is able to take out and give.
40
40. ‘‘Jāto kho pana, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro animisanto pekkhati seyyathāpi devā tāvatiṃsā. ‘Animisanto kumāro pekkhatī’ti kho, bhikkhave animisanto pekkhati, jātassa kho pana bhikkhave (ka.), vipassissa kumārassa ‘vipassī vipassī’ tveva samaññā udapādi.
‘And when Prince Vipassī was born, monks, he was unblinkingly watchful, like the Thirty-Three Gods (devā tāvatiṃsā). And because it was said that he was unblinkingly watchful, the prince came to be called “Vipassī”.’
40.Vipassīti ettha ayaṃ vacanattho, antarantarā nimīlajanitandhakāravirahena visuddhaṃ passati, vivaṭehi ca akkhīhi passatīti vipassī; dutiyavāre viceyya viceyya passatīti vipassī; vicinitvā vicinitvā passatīti attho. Atthe panāyatīti atthe jānāti passati, nayati vā pavattetīti attho. Ekadivasaṃ kira vinicchayaṭṭhāne nisīditvā atthe anusāsantassa rañño alaṅkatapaṭiyattaṃ mahāpurisaṃ ānetvā hatthe ṭhapayiṃsu. Tassa taṃ aṅkekatvā upalāḷayamānasseva amaccā sāmikaṃ assāmikaṃ akaṃsu. Bodhisatto anattamanasaddaṃ nicchāresi. Rājā – ‘‘kimetaṃ, upadhārethā’’ti āha. Upadhāriyamānā aññaṃ adisvā – ‘‘aḍḍassa dubbinicchitattā evaṃ kataṃ bhavissatī’’ti puna sāmikaṃyeva sāmikaṃ katvā ‘‘ñatvā nu kho kumāro evaṃ karotī’’ti vīmaṃsantā puna sāmikaṃ assāmikaṃ akaṃsu. Punapi bodhisatto tatheva saddaṃ nicchāresi. Atha rājā – ‘‘jānāti mahāpuriso’’ti tato paṭṭhāya appamatto ahosi. Idaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ – ‘‘viceyya viceyya kumāro atthe panāyatī’’ti. Atthe panāyatī
40.
Vipassī: Here, the meaning of the word is: He sees purely without the darkness caused by the intermittent closing [of the eyes], thus he is vipassī. In the second instance, he sees by distinguishing, thus he is vipassī; the meaning is he sees by discriminating.
atthe panāyatī: The meaning is, he knows and sees the meaning, he leads or establishes the meaning.
One day, it seems, having sat in the judgment seat, the ministers brought a well-adorned great man to the king who was instructing about the meaning and placed him in his hand.
Having put him on his lap and while caressing him, the ministers made him the owner and not the owner.
The bodhisattva emitted an unpleasant sound.
The king said, "What is this, consider it!"
While they were considering, not seeing anything else, [they thought,] "Due to the stupidity of the half-wit, this has been done." Again, having made the owner the owner, while pondering, "Having known, does the boy do this?", again they made the owner not the owner.
Again, the bodhisattva emitted the same sound.
Then the king, "The great man knows," from then on, he was diligent.
Referring to this it was said, "The boy discerns and causes the meaning to appear."
atthe panāyatī
41
41. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā atthakaraṇe aṭṭa karaṇe (syā.) nisinno vipassiṃ kumāraṃ aṅke nisīdāpetvā atthe anusāsati . Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro pituaṅke nisinno viceyya viceyya atthe panāyati ñāyena aṭṭe panāyati ñāṇena (syā.). Viceyya viceyya kumāro atthe panāyati ñāyenāti kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa kumārassa bhiyyosomattāya ‘vipassī vipassī’ tveva samaññā udapādi.
Then, monks, King Bandhumā, seated in the judgment hall, would seat Prince Vipassī on his lap and instruct him in matters of law. There, monks, Prince Vipassī, seated on his father's lap, would examine and discern the meaning of the cases with reason. Because the prince Vipassī, examining and discerning, would decide cases with reason, monks, the designation 'Vipassī, Vipassī' arose for Prince Vipassī all the more.
42
42. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā vipassissa kumārassa tayo pāsāde kārāpesi, ekaṃ vassikaṃ ekaṃ hemantikaṃ ekaṃ gimhikaṃ; pañca kāmaguṇāni upaṭṭhāpesi. Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro vassike pāsāde cattāro māse vassike pāsāde vassike nippurisehi tūriyehi paricārayamāno na heṭṭhāpāsādaṃ orohatī’’ti. Paṭhamabhāṇavāro. Jiṇṇapuriso
"Then, monks, King Bandhuma had three palaces built for Prince Vipassi: one for the rainy season, one for the cold season, and one for the hot season; he provided all five sense-pleasures.

There, monks, Prince Vipassi stayed in the rainy-season palace for the four months of the rainy season, attended by female musicians and without male attendants, and he never left that palace."

The First Chapter of Recitation.

Old Man.
42.Vassikantiādīsu yattha sukhaṃ hoti vassakāle vasituṃ, ayaṃ vassiko. Itaresupi eseva nayo. Ayaṃ panettha vacanattho vassāvāso vassaṃ, vassaṃ arahatīti vassiko. Itaresupi eseva nayo. Tattha vassiko pāsādo nātiucco hoti, nātinīco, dvāravātapānānipissa nātibahūni nātitanūni, bhūmattharaṇapaccattharaṇakhajjabhojjānipettha missakāneva vaṭṭanti. Hemantike thambhāpi bhittiyopi nīcā honti, dvāravātapānāni tanukāni sukhumacchiddāni, uṇhappavesanatthāya bhittiniyūhāni nīhariyanti. Bhūmattharaṇapaccattharaṇanivāsanapārupanāni panettha uṇhaviriyāni kambalādīni vaṭṭanti. Khajjabhojjaṃ siniddhaṃ kaṭukasannissitaṃ nirudakasannissitañca. Gimhike thambhāpi bhittiyopi uccā honti, dvāravātapānāni panettha bahūni vipulajātāni honti, bhūmattharaṇādīni dukūlamayāni vaṭṭanti. Khajjabhojjāni madhurasasannissitabharitāni. Vātapānasamīpesu cettha nava cāṭiyo ṭhapetvā udakassa pūretvā nīluppalādīhi sañchādenti. Tesu tesu padesesu udakayantāni karonti, yehi deve vassante viya udakadhārā nikkhamanti. Hemantike Gimhike Nippurisehīti purisavirahitehi. Na kevalañcettha tūriyāneva nippurisāni, sabbaṭṭhānānipi nippurisāneva, dovārikāpi itthiyova, nahāpanādiparikammakarāpi itthiyova. Rājā kira – ‘‘tathārūpaṃ issariyasukhasampattiṃ anubhavamānassa purisaṃ disvā purisāsaṅkā uppajjati, sā me puttassa mā ahosī’’ti sabbakiccesu itthiyova ṭhapesīti. Nippurisehī Paṭhamabhāṇavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Jiṇṇapurisavaṇṇanā
42. Where there is happiness in dwelling during the rainy season ( vassakāle), that is vassika.

And so with the other three.

Here, the meaning of the word is: vassāvāso vassaṃ, vassaṃ arahatīti vassiko "The rains-residence is the rains; he is worthy of the rains, therefore vassiko."

And so with the other three.

There, the vassiko pāsāda (rainy season dwelling) is not too high, not too low; its door-windows are neither too many nor too thin; the ground coverings, the coverlets, the foods and provisions are mixed.

In the cold season dwelling (hemantike), the pillars and walls are low, the door-windows are thin with fine holes, and wall projections are made to let in warmth. The ground coverings, the coverlets, the bedding and cloths are warm and include blankets, etc. The foods and provisions are oily, mixed with pungent flavors, and mixed with non-watery things.

In the hot season dwelling (gimhike), the pillars and walls are high, the door-windows are many and of ample design, the ground coverings, etc., are made of dukūla cloth. The foods and provisions are filled with sweet-tasting mixtures.

Near the air-holes, nine new bowls are placed, filled with water, and covered with blue lotuses, etc. In those various places, water contraptions are made, from which streams of water emerge as if a god were raining.

In the cold season dwelling.

In the hot season dwelling.

"Nippurisehī means: without men (purisavirahitehi)."

Not only are the musical instruments here without men, but all the places are without men; even the doorkeeper is a woman, and the bath attendants and those who prepare things are women.

The king, it is said, [thought]: "Seeing a man enjoying such sovereignty, happiness, and prosperity, the thought of a man arises; may that not happen to my son!" Thus, in all affairs, he employed only women.

Here ends the explanation of the first recitation section.

Explanation of the Old Man.
43
43. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bahūnaṃ vassānaṃ bahūnaṃ vassasatānaṃ bahūnaṃ vassasahassānaṃ accayena sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘yojehi, samma sārathi, bhaddāni bhaddāni yānāni uyyānabhūmiṃ gacchāma subhūmidassanāyā’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā bhaddāni bhaddāni yānāni yojetvā vipassissa kumārassa paṭivedesi – ‘yuttāni kho te, deva, bhaddāni bhaddāni yānāni, yassa dāni kālaṃ maññasī’ti . Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bhaddaṃ bhaddaṃ yānaṃ bhadraṃ yānaṃ (syā.), bhaddaṃ yānaṃ (pī.) cattāro māse (sī. pī.) abhiruhitvā bhaddehi bhaddehi yānehi uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyāsi.
43. Then, monks, after many years, many hundreds of years, many thousands of years had passed, Prince Vipassī addressed the charioteer: ‘Harness fine, auspicious carriages, good charioteer, let us go to the pleasure-park to see the beautiful grounds.’

‘As you say, your highness,’ monks, the charioteer replied to Prince Vipassī, and having harnessed fine, auspicious carriages, he reported to Prince Vipassī: ‘The fine, auspicious carriages are ready for you, your highness; now is the time to do as you wish.’

Then, monks, Prince Vipassī, mounting a fine, auspicious vehicle, and accompanied by fine, auspicious vehicles, set out for the pleasure-park.
43. Dutiyabhāṇavāre gopānasivaṅkanti gopānasī viya vaṅkaṃ. Bhogganti khandhe, kaṭiyaṃ, jāṇūsūti tīsu ṭhānesu bhoggavaṅkaṃ. Daṇḍaparāyananti daṇḍagatikaṃ daṇḍapaṭisaraṇaṃ. Āturanti jarāturaṃ. Gatayobbananti atikkantayobbanaṃ pacchimavaye ṭhitaṃ. Disvāti aḍḍhayojanappamāṇena balakāyena parivuto susaṃvihitārakkhopi gacchanto yadā ratho purato hoti, pacchā balakāyo, tādise okāse suddhāvāsakhīṇāsavabrahmehi attano ānubhāvena rathassa puratova dassitaṃ, taṃ purisaṃ passitvā. Suddhāvāsā kira – ‘‘mahāpuriso paṅke gajo viya pañcasu kāmaguṇesu laggo, satimassa uppādessāmā’’ti taṃ dassesuṃ. Evaṃ dassitañca taṃ bodhisatto ceva passati sārathi ca. Brahmāno hi bodhisattassa appamādatthaṃ sārathissa ca kathāsallāpatthaṃ taṃ dassesuṃ. Kiṃ panesoti ‘‘eso jiṇṇoti kiṃ vuttaṃ hoti, nāhaṃ, bho ito pubbe evarūpaṃ addasa’’nti pucchi. Tenahīti yadi mayhampi evarūpehi kesehi evarūpena ca kāyena bhavitabbaṃ, tena hi samma sārathi. Alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyāti – ‘‘ajja uyyānabhūmiṃ passissāmā’’ti gacchāma, alaṃ tāya uyyānabhūmiyāti saṃviggahadayo saṃvegānurūpamāha. Antepuraṃ gatoti itthijanaṃ vissajjetvā sirigabbhe ekakova nisinno. Yatra hi nāmāti yāya jātiyā sati jarā paññāyati, sā jāti dhiratthu dhikkatā atthu, jigucchāmetaṃ jātinti, jātiyā mūlaṃ khaṇanto nisīdi, paṭhamena sallena hadaye viddho viya. Tena hī Alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā Antepuraṃ gato Yatra hi nāmā dhiratthu
43. In the second round of recitation:

" Gopānasivaṅka " means bent like the eaves of a roof.

"Bhogga" means bent at the shoulders (skandha), waist (kaṭi), and knees (jāṇu), in these three places (ṭhāna).

"Daṇḍaparāyana" means using a staff for walking, using a staff for support.

"Ātura" means afflicted by old age (jarā).

"Gatayobbana" means having passed youth, abiding in the final stage of life.

"Disvā" means having seen: When the chariot goes forward, surrounded by a troop with a half-yojana measure, even with well-arranged protection, and the troop is behind, in such an opportunity, the Pure Abodes (Śuddhāvāsa) and those whose defilements are destroyed (khīṇāsava) showed that man in front of the chariot through their own power (ānubhāva), having seen that man.

The Pure Abodes [thought]: “This great man is stuck in the mud, like an elephant caught in the five sensual qualities (kāmaguṇa). Let us arouse mindfulness in him,” and they showed that.

Thus, the Bodhisattva and the charioteer both see what was shown.

The Brahmās showed that to the Bodhisattva for the sake of diligence (appamāda) and to the charioteer for the sake of a conversation.

"Kiṃ paneso?" [he asked], "What does 'eso jiṇṇoti' mean? Sir, I have never seen such a thing before."

"Hī," [he said], "If I also must become like this with such hair and such a body, then enough, dear charioteer.

"Alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā" [he said] – "Today, we were going to see the park, enough with that park!" He spoke in accordance with his agitation, his heart being agitated (saṃviggahadaya).

"Antepuraṃ gato" means having dismissed the women, he sat alone in the glory chamber.

"Yatra hi nāmā" means wherever there is birth (jāti) and old age is perceived, may that birth be destroyed.

"Dhiratthu! Shame! I despise this birth!" Cutting off the root (mūla) of birth, he sat down, as if pierced in the heart by the first arrow.
44
44. ‘‘Addasā kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto purisaṃ jiṇṇaṃ gopānasivaṅkaṃ bhoggaṃ bhaggaṃ (syā.) daṇḍaparāyanaṃ pavedhamānaṃ gacchantaṃ āturaṃ gatayobbanaṃ. Disvā sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘ayaṃ pana, samma sārathi, puriso kiṃkato? Kesāpissa na yathā aññesaṃ, kāyopissa na yathā aññesa’nti. ‘Eso kho, deva, jiṇṇo nāmā’ti. ‘Kiṃ paneso, samma sārathi, jiṇṇo nāmā’ti? ‘Eso kho, deva, jiṇṇo nāma. Na dāni tena ciraṃ jīvitabbaṃ bhavissatī’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, ahampi jarādhammo, jaraṃ anatīto’ti? ‘Tvañca, deva, mayañcamha sabbe jarādhammā, jaraṃ anatītā’ti. ‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā. Itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhī’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsi. Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro antepuraṃ gato dukkhī dummano pajjhāyati – ‘dhiratthu kira, bho, jāti nāma, yatra hi nāma jātassa jarā paññāyissatī’ti!
44. Monks, Vipassī, the prince, saw, as he was going to the pleasure garden, a man who was old (jīrṇa), with a bent body like a roof gable (gopānasivaṅka), decrepit (bhogga), leaning on a staff (daṇḍaparāyana), trembling (pavedhamāna), and sick (ātura), his youth gone (gatayobbana).

Having seen him, he said to the charioteer (sārathi): “Well then, good charioteer, what is the matter with this man (purisa)? His hairs (kesā) are not like other men’s (aññesaṃ), nor is his body (kāya) like other men’s.”

“Prince (deva), he is called old (jīrṇa).”

“But what is this being old (jīrṇa), good charioteer?” “Prince, he is called old. He will not live (jīvitabbaṃ) much longer (ciraṃ).”

“Well then, good charioteer, am I also subject to old age (jarādhammo), not exempt from old age (jaraṃ anatīto)?” “You too, Prince, and we too, are all subject to old age, not exempt from old age.”

“Well then, good charioteer, enough (alaṃ) of the pleasure garden for today (uyyānabhūmiyā). Return now to the palace (antepuraṃ).”

“Very good, Prince,” said the charioteer, and he brought Prince Vipassī back to the palace.

There, monks, Prince Vipassī, having gone to the palace, afflicted (dukkhī) and dejected (dummano), reflected (pajjhāyati): “Shame on birth (jāti), since old age (jarā) will appear (paññāyissati) in one who is born (jātassa)!”
45
45. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā sārathiṃ āmantāpetvā etadavoca – ‘kacci, samma sārathi, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā abhiramittha? Kacci, samma sārathi, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā attamano ahosī’ti? ‘Na kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā abhiramittha, na kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā attamano ahosī’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, addasa kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto’ti? ‘Addasā kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto purisaṃ jiṇṇaṃ gopānasivaṅkaṃ bhoggaṃ daṇḍaparāyanaṃ pavedhamānaṃ gacchantaṃ āturaṃ gatayobbanaṃ. Disvā maṃ etadavoca – ‘‘ayaṃ pana, samma sārathi, puriso kiṃkato, kesāpissa na yathā aññesaṃ, kāyopissa na yathā aññesa’’nti? ‘‘Eso kho, deva, jiṇṇo nāmā’’ti. ‘‘Kiṃ paneso, samma sārathi, jiṇṇo nāmā’’ti? ‘‘Eso kho, deva, jiṇṇo nāma na dāni tena ciraṃ jīvitabbaṃ bhavissatī’’ti. ‘‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, ahampi jarādhammo, jaraṃ anatīto’’ti? ‘‘Tvañca, deva, mayañcamha sabbe jarādhammā, jaraṃ anatītā’’ti. ‘‘‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā, itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhī’’’ti. ‘‘Evaṃ, devā’’ti kho ahaṃ, deva, vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsiṃ. So kho, deva, kumāro antepuraṃ gato dukkhī dummano pajjhāyati – ‘‘dhiratthu kira bho jāti nāma, yatra hi nāma jātassa jarā paññāyissatī’’’ti. Byādhitapuriso
‘Then, O monks, King Bandhuma summoned his charioteer and said to him: “Did the prince enjoy the park? Was the prince pleased in the park?” “No, my lord, the prince did not enjoy the park, nor was he pleased in the park.”

‘But what did the prince see when he went to the park?” “There, my lord, the prince saw a man who was old, bent like a roof-gutter, decrepit, leaning on a stick, trembling as he walked, sick and past his youth.

Having seen me, he said to me: “What has happened to this man, good charioteer? His hairs are not like those of others, nor is his body like that of others?” “That, my lord, is called old age.”

“But what is this ‘old age,’ good charioteer?” “That, my lord, is called old age. Now he will not live long.”

“But am I also subject to old age, good charioteer, not exempt from aging?” “You too, my lord, and we too, are all subject to old age and not exempt from aging.”

“‘Then, good charioteer, enough for today with the pleasure park; return now to the inner palace.’”

“‘Even so, my lord,” I replied to Vipassi, the prince, and from there I returned to the inner palace.

There, O monks, the prince went to the inner palace, grieving and depressed, pondering: “Alas, indeed, is birth! For old age will appear in one who is born!”’

The Sick Man
45.Sārathiṃ āmantāpetvāti rājā kira nemittakehi kathitakālato paṭṭhāya ohitasoto vicarati, so ‘‘kumāro uyyānaṃ gacchanto antarāmagge nivatto’’ti sutvā sārathiṃ āmantāpesi. Mā heva khotiādīsu rajjaṃ kāretu, mā pabbajatu, brāhmaṇānaṃ vacanaṃ mā saccaṃ hotūti evaṃ cintesīti attho. Byādhipurisavaṇṇanā
"Having ordered the charioteer to be summoned": The king, from the time of the soothsayers' prediction, was living attentively. Having heard that "the prince, while going to the park, has turned back on the way," he ordered the charioteer to be summoned.

"May it not be thus": He thought in this way, "Let him rule the kingdom, let him not renounce the world, may the word of the brāhmaṇas (brāhmaṇānaṃ), the priests, not be true." This is the meaning.

Description of the diseased man.
46
46. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumassa rañño etadahosi – ‘Mā heva kho vipassī kumāro na rajjaṃ kāresi, mā heva vipassī kumāro agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbaji, mā heva nemittānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ saccaṃ assa vacana’nti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā vipassissa kumārassa bhiyyosomattāya pañca kāmaguṇāni upaṭṭhāpesi – ‘yathā vipassī kumāro rajjaṃ kareyya, yathā vipassī kumāro na agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajeyya, yathā nemittānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ micchā assa vacana’nti. ‘‘Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro pañcahi kāmaguṇehi samappito samaṅgībhūto paricāreti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bahūnaṃ vassānaṃ…pe…
“Then, monks, it occurred to King Bandhuma:

‘May Prince Vipassī not fail to rule the kingdom, may Prince Vipassī not go forth from the household life into homelessness, may the words of the soothsaying Brahmins not prove true.’

Then, monks, King Bandhuma provided Prince Vipassī with even more of the five sense-pleasures, so that Prince Vipassī would rule the kingdom, so that Prince Vipassī would not go forth from the household life into homelessness, so that the words of the soothsaying Brahmins would be false."

“There, monks, Prince Vipassī lived indulging in, and addicted to the five sense-pleasures (pañca kāmaguṇāni).

Then, monks, after many years, Prince Vipassī…"
47
47. ‘‘Addasā kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto purisaṃ ābādhikaṃ dukkhitaṃ bāḷhagilānaṃ sake muttakarīse palipannaṃ semānaṃ sayamānaṃ (syā. ka.) aññehi vuṭṭhāpiyamānaṃ aññehi saṃvesiyamānaṃ. Disvā sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘ayaṃ pana, samma sārathi, puriso kiṃkato? Akkhīnipissa na yathā aññesaṃ, saropissa siropissa (syā.) na yathā aññesa’nti? ‘Eso kho, deva, byādhito nāmā’ti. ‘Kiṃ paneso, samma sārathi, byādhito nāmā’ti? ‘Eso kho, deva, byādhito nāma appeva nāma tamhā ābādhā vuṭṭhaheyyā’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, ahampi byādhidhammo, byādhiṃ anatīto’ti? ‘Tvañca, deva, mayañcamha sabbe byādhidhammā, byādhiṃ anatītā’ti. ‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā, itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhī’ti. ‘Evaṃ devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsi. Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro antepuraṃ gato dukkhī dummano pajjhāyati – ‘dhiratthu kira bho jāti nāma, yatra hi nāma jātassa jarā paññāyissati, byādhi paññāyissatī’ti.
47.
Then, monks, Prince Vipassī, while going to the pleasure-park, saw a man afflicted, suffering, very ill, lying in his own urine and excrement, being raised up by others, being put to bed by others.

Having seen [him], he addressed the charioteer: "Charioteer, what is the matter with this man? His eyes are not like those of others, nor is his voice like that of others?" "This one, Prince, is called sick."

"But what, charioteer, is this 'sick'?" "This one, Prince, who is called sick, may not recover from that sickness."

"But what, charioteer, am I also subject to sickness, not beyond sickness?" "You too, Prince, and we all are subject to sickness, not beyond sickness."

"Well then, charioteer, that will do for today with the pleasure-park; return now to the inner palace."

"Very good, Prince," said the charioteer, and having promised Prince Vipassī, he returned to the inner palace.

There, monks, Prince Vipassī, having gone to the inner palace, afflicted, depressed, reflected: "Shame on this thing birth ( jāti), since he who is born must experience old age (jarā) and sickness (vyādhi)!"
47.Addasa khoti pubbe vuttanayeneva suddhāvāsehi dassitaṃ addasa. Ābādhikanti iriyāpathabhañjanakena visabhāgabādhena ābādhikaṃ. Dukkhitanti rogadukkhena dukkhitaṃ. Bāḷhagilānanti adhimattagilānaṃ. Palipannanti nimuggaṃ. Jarā paññāyissati byādhi paññāyissatīti idhāpi yāya jātiyā sati idaṃ dvayaṃ paññāyati, dhikkatā sā jāti, ajātaṃ khemanti jātiyā mūlaṃ khaṇanto nisīdi, dutiyena sallena viddho viya. Kālaṅkatapurisavaṇṇanā
47. Addasa kho means he saw what was shown by the Śuddhāvāsas, in the manner previously stated.
Ābādhika means afflicted by an affliction that destroys proper conduct and is of a different kind.
Dukkhita means afflicted with the suffering of disease.
Bāḷhagilāna means excessively ill.
Palipanna means immersed.
Jarā paññāyissati vyādhi paññāyissatī means even here, because of whatever birth this pair is known, cursed is that birth, unborn is safety, he sat digging up the root of birth, as if struck by a second dart.

Description of a deceased man.
48
48. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā sārathiṃ āmantāpetvā etadavoca – ‘kacci, samma sārathi, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā abhiramittha, kacci, samma sārathi, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā attamano ahosī’ti? ‘Na kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā abhiramittha, na kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā attamano ahosī’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, addasa kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto’ti? ‘Addasā kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto purisaṃ ābādhikaṃ dukkhitaṃ bāḷhagilānaṃ sake muttakarīse palipannaṃ semānaṃ aññehi vuṭṭhāpiyamānaṃ aññehi saṃvesiyamānaṃ. Disvā maṃ etadavoca – ‘‘ayaṃ pana, samma sārathi, puriso kiṃkato, akkhīnipissa na yathā aññesaṃ, saropissa na yathā aññesa’’nti? ‘‘Eso kho, deva, byādhito nāmā’’ti. ‘‘Kiṃ paneso, samma sārathi, byādhito nāmā’’ti? ‘‘Eso kho, deva, byādhito nāma appeva nāma tamhā ābādhā vuṭṭhaheyyā’’ti. ‘‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, ahampi byādhidhammo, byādhiṃ anatīto’’ti? ‘‘Tvañca, deva, mayañcamha sabbe byādhidhammā, byādhiṃ anatītā’’ti. ‘‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā, itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhī’’ti. ‘‘Evaṃ, devā’’ti kho ahaṃ, deva, vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsiṃ. So kho, deva, kumāro antepuraṃ gato dukkhī dummano pajjhāyati – ‘‘‘dhiratthu kira bho jāti nāma, yatra hi nāma jātassa jarā paññāyissati, byādhi paññāyissatī’’’ti. Kālaṅkatapuriso
48. Then, mendicants, King Bandhuma summoned his charioteer and said this: ‘Well, friend charioteer, did the prince enjoy himself in the pleasure park? Well, friend charioteer, was the prince pleased in the pleasure park?’ ‘No, your majesty, the prince did not enjoy himself in the pleasure park; no, your majesty, the prince was not pleased in the pleasure park.’

‘Then, friend charioteer, what did the prince see going to the pleasure park?’ ‘Going to the pleasure park, your majesty, the prince saw a man who was afflicted, miserable, seriously ill, lying in his own urine and excrement, being lifted up by some, being put to bed by others.

Having seen me, he said this: “Now, friend charioteer, what has happened to this man? His eyes are not like those of others, his voice is not like that of others?” “That, your majesty, is called a sick person.”

“Well then, friend charioteer, what is this sickness?” “That, your majesty, is called sickness. It is possible that he might recover from that disease.”

“Well then, friend charioteer, am I also subject to sickness, not exempt from sickness?” “You too, your majesty, and we too are all subject to sickness, not exempt from sickness.”

“In that case, friend charioteer, that will do for today with the pleasure park. Return now to the inner chambers.”

‘Yes, your majesty,’ I replied to Prince Vipassi, and then and there I returned to the inner chambers.

Then, your majesty, the prince, having gone to the inner chambers, dejected and miserable, reflected: “Damn this thing called birth, since for one who is born, old age will appear, sickness will appear!”

The man who had passed away.
49
49. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumassa rañño etadahosi – ‘mā heva kho vipassī kumāro na rajjaṃ kāresi, mā heva vipassī kumāro agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbaji, mā heva nemittānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ saccaṃ assa vacana’nti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā vipassissa kumārassa bhiyyosomattāya pañca kāmaguṇāni upaṭṭhāpesi – ‘yathā vipassī kumāro rajjaṃ kareyya, yathā vipassī kumāro na agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajeyya, yathā nemittānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ micchā assa vacana’nti. ‘‘Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro pañcahi kāmaguṇehi samappito samaṅgībhūto paricāreti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bahūnaṃ vassānaṃ…pe…
49. Then, monks, it occurred to King Bandhuma: "Prince Vipassī must not fail to rule the kingdom, Prince Vipassī must not go forth from the household life into homelessness—the words of the brāhmaṇas learned in signs must not come true!"

Then, monks, King Bandhuma provided Prince Vipassī with even more enjoyment of the five kāmaguṇas (objects of desire) – "so that Prince Vipassī might rule the kingdom, so that Prince Vipassī might not go forth from the household life into homelessness, so that the words of the brāhmaṇas learned in signs might be false."

There, monks, Prince Vipassī lived indulging in, and addicted to the five kāmaguṇas (objects of desire).

Then, monks, after many years, Prince Vipassī...
50
50. ‘‘Addasā kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto mahājanakāyaṃ sannipatitaṃ nānārattānañca dussānaṃ vilātaṃ kayiramānaṃ. Disvā sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘kiṃ nu kho, so, samma sārathi, mahājanakāyo sannipatito nānārattānañca dussānaṃ vilātaṃ kayiratī’ti? ‘Eso kho, deva, kālaṅkato nāmā’ti. ‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, yena so kālaṅkato tena rathaṃ pesehī’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā yena so kālaṅkato tena rathaṃ pesesi. Addasā kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro petaṃ kālaṅkataṃ, disvā sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘kiṃ panāyaṃ, samma sārathi, kālaṅkato nāmā’ti? ‘Eso kho, deva, kālaṅkato nāma. Na dāni taṃ dakkhanti mātā vā pitā vā aññe vā ñātisālohitā, sopi na dakkhissati mātaraṃ vā pitaraṃ vā aññe vā ñātisālohite’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, ahampi maraṇadhammo maraṇaṃ anatīto; mampi na dakkhanti devo vā devī vā aññe vā ñātisālohitā; ahampi na dakkhissāmi devaṃ vā deviṃ vā aññe vā ñātisālohite’ti? ‘Tvañca, deva, mayañcamha sabbe maraṇadhammā maraṇaṃ anatītā; tampi na dakkhanti devo vā devī vā aññe vā ñātisālohitā; tvampi na dakkhissasi devaṃ vā deviṃ vā aññe vā ñātisālohite’ti. ‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā, itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhī’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsi. Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro antepuraṃ gato dukkhī dummano pajjhāyati – ‘dhiratthu kira, bho, jāti nāma, yatra hi nāma jātassa jarā paññāyissati, byādhi paññāyissati, maraṇaṃ paññāyissatī’ti.
50.
Then, monks, Vipassī, the prince, going to the pleasure park, saw a great crowd of people gathered and cloths of various colors being displayed. Seeing this, he addressed the charioteer: “What is this, dear charioteer? Why is this great crowd of people gathered and cloths of various colors being displayed?” “That, prince, is called a dead man.” “Well then, dear charioteer, drive the chariot to where that dead man is.” “Very good, Prince,” said the charioteer, and drove the chariot to where the dead man was. Vipassī, the prince, gazed at the corpse of the dead man. Then he said to the charioteer: “Why is this called a dead man?” “That, Prince, is called a dead man. Now, his mother, father, or other relatives will not see him, nor will he see his mother, father, or other relatives.” “But, dear charioteer, am I also subject to death, not exempt from dying? Will gods, goddesses, or other relatives not see me? Will I also not see gods, goddesses, or other relatives?” “You too, Prince, and we all are subject to death, not exempt from dying. Gods, goddesses, or other relatives will not see you, nor will you see gods, goddesses, or other relatives.” “Well then, dear charioteer, that will do for today with the pleasure-park. Return now to the palace.” “Very good, Prince,” said the charioteer, and brought Prince Vipassī back to the palace. Arrived there, Prince Vipassī was overcome with grief and dejection, crying: “Shame on this thing birth, since to him who is born old age will manifest itself, sickness will manifest itself, and death will manifest itself!”
50.Vilātanti sivikaṃ. Petanti ito paṭigataṃ. Kālaṅkatanti katakālaṃ, yattakaṃ tena kālaṃ jīvitabbaṃ, taṃ sabbaṃ katvā niṭṭhapetvā matanti attho. Imampissa purimanayeneva brahmāno dassesuṃ. Yatra hi nāmāti idhāpi yāya jātiyā sati idaṃ tayaṃ paññāyati, dhikkatā sā jāti, ajātaṃ khemanti jātiyā mūlaṃ khaṇanto nisīdi, tatiyena sallena viddho viya. Pabbajitavaṇṇanā
"vilātaṃ" means a palanquin (śivikā).
"petaṃ" means returned from here (ito paṭigataṃ).
"kālaṅkataṃ" means having made the time, how much time one should live by that time, having done all of that, having completed it, it means dead.
To him also, the Brahmins showed [it] in the same way as before.
"yatra hi nāmā" means even here, when there is any birth by which this triad is perceived, cursed is that birth, unborn is safe, [he] sat digging up the root of birth, as if pierced by a third dart.

Explanation of going forth.
51
51. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā sārathiṃ āmantāpetvā etadavoca – ‘kacci, samma sārathi, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā abhiramittha, kacci, samma sārathi, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā attamano ahosī’ti? ‘Na kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā abhiramittha, na kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiyā attamano ahosī’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, addasa kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto’ti? ‘Addasā kho, deva, kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto mahājanakāyaṃ sannipatitaṃ nānārattānañca dussānaṃ vilātaṃ kayiramānaṃ. Disvā maṃ etadavoca – ‘‘kiṃ nu kho, so , samma sārathi, mahājanakāyo sannipatito nānārattānañca dussānaṃ vilātaṃ kayiratī’’ti? ‘‘Eso kho, deva, kālaṅkato nāmā’’ti. ‘‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, yena so kālaṅkato tena rathaṃ pesehī’’ti. ‘‘Evaṃ devā’’ti kho ahaṃ, deva, vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā yena so kālaṅkato tena rathaṃ pesesiṃ. Addasā kho, deva, kumāro petaṃ kālaṅkataṃ, disvā maṃ etadavoca – ‘‘kiṃ panāyaṃ, samma sārathi, kālaṅkato nāmā’’ti ? ‘‘Eso kho, deva, kālaṅkato nāma. Na dāni taṃ dakkhanti mātā vā pitā vā aññe vā ñātisālohitā, sopi na dakkhissati mātaraṃ vā pitaraṃ vā aññe vā ñātisālohite’’ti. ‘‘Kiṃ pana, samma sārathi, ahampi maraṇadhammo maraṇaṃ anatīto; mampi na dakkhanti devo vā devī vā aññe vā ñātisālohitā; ahampi na dakkhissāmi devaṃ vā deviṃ vā aññe vā ñātisālohite’’ti? ‘‘Tvañca, deva, mayañcamha sabbe maraṇadhammā maraṇaṃ anatītā; tampi na dakkhanti devo vā devī vā aññe vā ñātisālohitā, tvampi na dakkhissasi devaṃ vā deviṃ vā aññe vā ñātisālohite’’ti. ‘‘Tena hi, samma sārathi, alaṃ dānajja uyyānabhūmiyā, itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhī’ti. ‘‘‘Evaṃ, devā’’ti kho ahaṃ, deva, vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsiṃ. So kho, deva, kumāro antepuraṃ gato dukkhī dummano pajjhāyati – ‘‘dhiratthu kira bho jāti nāma, yatra hi nāma jātassa jarā paññāyissati, byādhi paññāyissati, maraṇaṃ paññāyissatī’’’ti. Pabbajito
51. ‘Then, mendicants, King Bandhuma sent for the charioteer and said to him: “Friend charioteer, did the prince enjoy the park; friend charioteer, was the prince happy in the park?” “No, your majesty, the prince did not enjoy the park; no, your majesty, the prince was not happy in the park.”

‘But what did the prince see, friend charioteer, when he went to the park?’ ‘Your majesty, when the prince went to the pleasure-park, he saw a great crowd gathered together, and cloths of various colours were being torn up.

Having seen me, he said this: “What is this, friend charioteer, this great crowd gathered together, and cloths of various colours being torn up?” “Prince, that is what is called a dead man (kālaṅkata).”

‘Then, friend charioteer, drive the chariot over to where the dead man (kālaṅkata) is.”

“Very good, Prince,” I said, and I drove the chariot to where the dead man (kālaṅkata) was.

Your majesty, the prince saw the dead man (kālaṅkata), and having seen me, he said this: “But what is this, friend charioteer, called a dead man (kālaṅkata)?” ‘Prince, that is what is called a dead man (kālaṅkata).

Now his mother, father, or other blood relatives will not see him again, nor will he see his mother, father, or other blood relatives.”

‘But am I, friend charioteer, also subject to death, not exempt from dying? Will my parents and other relatives not see me again, nor will I see my parents and other relatives?” “Yes, your majesty, we are all subject to death, not exempt from dying; your parents and other relatives will not see you again, nor will you see your parents and other relatives.”

‘Then, friend charioteer, that will do for today with the pleasure-park; return now to the inner apartments (antepura).’

“Very good, Prince,” I said, and I returned to the inner apartments (antepura).

Then, mendicants, the prince, having gone to the inner apartments (antepura), was overcome with grief and dejection, crying: “Alas! This thing called birth, since to him who is born old age, sickness and death will manifest themselves!”

Renunciation
52
52. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumassa rañño etadahosi – ‘mā heva kho vipassī kumāro na rajjaṃ kāresi, mā heva vipassī kumāro agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbaji, mā heva nemittānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ saccaṃ assa vacana’nti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, bandhumā rājā vipassissa kumārassa bhiyyosomattāya pañca kāmaguṇāni upaṭṭhāpesi – ‘yathā vipassī kumāro rajjaṃ kareyya, yathā vipassī kumāro na agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajeyya, yathā nemittānaṃ brāhmaṇānaṃ micchā assa vacana’nti. ‘‘Tatra sudaṃ, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro pañcahi kāmaguṇehi samappito samaṅgībhūto paricāreti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bahūnaṃ vassānaṃ bahūnaṃ vassasatānaṃ bahūnaṃ vassasahassānaṃ accayena sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘yojehi, samma sārathi, bhaddāni bhaddāni yānāni, uyyānabhūmiṃ gacchāma subhūmidassanāyā’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā bhaddāni bhaddāni yānāni yojetvā vipassissa kumārassa paṭivedesi – ‘yuttāni kho te, deva, bhaddāni bhaddāni yānāni, yassa dāni kālaṃ maññasī’ti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro bhaddaṃ bhaddaṃ yānaṃ abhiruhitvā bhaddehi bhaddehi yānehi uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyāsi.
52. Then, monks, it occurred to King Bandhuma: 'Lest Prince Vipassī should not rule the kingdom, lest Prince Vipassī should go forth from the household life into homelessness, lest the words of the soothsaying brāhmaṇas prove true.'

Then, monks, King Bandhuma provided Prince Vipassī with even more of the five sense-pleasures (pañca kāmaguṇāni), so that Prince Vipassī would rule the kingdom, and would not go forth from the household life into homelessness, and that the words of the soothsaying brāhmaṇas would be false.

There, monks, Prince Vipassī lived indulging in, and addicted to the five sense-pleasures (pañcahi kāmaguṇehi),

Then, monks, after many years, many hundreds of years, many thousands of years had passed, Prince Vipassī addressed his charioteer: 'Harness fine carriages, dear charioteer. We will go to the pleasure-park (uyyānabhūmiṃ) to inspect it."

'Even so, your majesty,' the charioteer replied to Prince Vipassī. He harnessed the fine carriages, and informed Prince Vipassī: 'Your Majesty, the fine carriages are harnessed. Now is the time for you to do as you wish.'

Then, monks, Prince Vipassī mounted a fine carriage and drove out to the pleasure-park (uyyānabhūmiṃ) in procession with fine carriages.
52.Bhaṇḍunti muṇḍaṃ. Imampissa purimanayeneva brahmāno dassesuṃ. Sādhu dhammacariyātiādīsu ayaṃ deva dhammacaraṇabhāvo sādhūti cintetvā pabbajitoti evaṃ ekamekassa padassa yojanā veditabbā. Sabbāni cetāni dasakusalakammapathavevacanāneva. Avasāne pana avihiṃsāti karuṇāya pubbabhāgo. Anukampāti mettāya pubbabhāgo. Tenahīti uyyojanatthe nipāto. Pabbajitaṃ hissa disvā cittaṃ pabbajjāya ninnaṃ jātaṃ. Atha tena saddhiṃ kathetukāmo hutvā sārathiṃ uyyojento tena hītiādimāha. Bodhisattapabbajjāvaṇṇanā
52. Bhaṇḍu means muṇḍaṃ (shaven-headed).

To those Brahmins, he showed it just as before.

In the expressions beginning with sādhu dhammacariyā (good is the practice of the Dharma), the connection of each and every word should be understood thus: "Thinking, 'Good is this state of practicing the Dharma, O god!', he went forth."

All of these are indeed synonyms for the ten wholesome courses of action.

Finally, avihiṃsā (non-harming) is the preliminary part of compassion (karuṇā).

Anukampā (sympathy) is the preliminary part of loving-kindness (mettā).

Tenahī is a particle in the sense of encouragement.

Having seen him gone forth, his mind was inclined to going forth.

Then, wishing to speak with him, while encouraging the charioteer, he said the words beginning with tena hī.

Exposition on the Going Forth of the Bodhisattva.
53
53. ‘‘Addasā kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro uyyānabhūmiṃ niyyanto purisaṃ bhaṇḍuṃ pabbajitaṃ kāsāyavasanaṃ. Disvā sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘ayaṃ pana, samma sārathi, puriso kiṃkato? Sīsaṃpissa na yathā aññesaṃ, vatthānipissa na yathā aññesa’nti? ‘Eso kho, deva, pabbajito nāmā’ti. ‘Kiṃ paneso, samma sārathi, pabbajito nāmā’ti? ‘Eso kho, deva, pabbajito nāma sādhu dhammacariyā sādhu samacariyā sammacariyā (ka.) sādhu kusalakiriyā kusalacariyā (syā.) sādhu puññakiriyā sādhu avihiṃsā sādhu bhūtānukampā’ti. ‘Sādhu kho so, samma sārathi, pabbajito nāma, sādhu dhammacariyā sādhu samacariyā sādhu kusalakiriyā sādhu puññakiriyā sādhu avihiṃsā sādhu bhūtānukampā. Tena hi, samma sārathi, yena so pabbajito tena rathaṃ pesehī’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā yena so pabbajito tena rathaṃ pesesi. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro taṃ pabbajitaṃ etadavoca – ‘tvaṃ pana, samma, kiṃkato, sīsampi te na yathā aññesaṃ, vatthānipi te na yathā aññesa’nti? ‘Ahaṃ kho, deva, pabbajito nāmā’ti. ‘Kiṃ pana tvaṃ, samma, pabbajito nāmā’ti? ‘Ahaṃ kho, deva, pabbajito nāma, sādhu dhammacariyā sādhu samacariyā sādhu kusalakiriyā sādhu puññakiriyā sādhu avihiṃsā sādhu bhūtānukampā’ti. ‘Sādhu kho tvaṃ, samma, pabbajito nāma sādhu dhammacariyā sādhu samacariyā sādhu kusalakiriyā sādhu puññakiriyā sādhu avihiṃsā sādhu bhūtānukampā’ti. Bodhisattapabbajjā
‘And as he was being driven to the pleasure-park, Prince Vipassi saw a shaven-headed man, one who had gone forth, wearing a yellow robe.

At the sight he said to the charioteer: “Charioteer, what is the matter with this man? Is his head unlike that of others, and are his clothes unlike those of others?” “Prince, he is called one who has gone forth.”

“Charioteer, what is one who has gone forth?” “Prince, by one who has gone forth is meant one who truly follows the dharma (Dhamma), who truly lives in serenity, does good actions (kuśalakriyā), performs meritorious deeds (puṇyakriyā), is harmless (avihiṃsā) and truly has compassion for living beings (bhūtānukampā).”

“Charioteer, he is well called one who has gone forth! He truly follows the dharma, he truly lives in serenity, he does good actions (kuśalakriyā), he performs meritorious deeds (puṇyakriyā), he is harmless (avihiṃsā) and he truly has compassion for living beings (bhūtānukampā). Drive the carriage over to where he is.”

“Very good, Prince”, said the charioteer, and did so.

Then Prince Vipassi said to the renunciate: “Friend, what is the matter with you? Is your head unlike that of others, and are your clothes unlike those of others?” “Prince, I am called one who has gone forth.”

“But what is it to be one who has gone forth?” “Prince, by one who has gone forth is meant one who truly follows the dharma, who truly lives in serenity, does good actions (kuśalakriyā), performs meritorious deeds (puṇyakriyā), is harmless (avihiṃsā) and truly has compassion for living beings (bhūtānukampā).”

“You are well called one who has gone forth! You truly follow the dharma, you truly live in serenity, you do good actions (kuśalakriyā), you perform meritorious deeds (puṇyakriyā), you are harmless (avihiṃsā) and you truly have compassion for living beings (bhūtānukampā).”

Bodhisattva Renunciation
54
54. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro sārathiṃ āmantesi – ‘tena hi, samma sārathi, rathaṃ ādāya itova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāhi. Ahaṃ pana idheva kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajissāmī’ti. ‘Evaṃ, devā’ti kho, bhikkhave, sārathi vipassissa kumārassa paṭissutvā rathaṃ ādāya tatova antepuraṃ paccaniyyāsi. Vipassī pana kumāro tattheva kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbaji. Mahājanakāyaanupabbajjā
“Then, monks, Prince Vipassī addressed the charioteer: ‘Then, good charioteer, take the chariot and return to the inner palace from here. But I shall stay here, shave off my hair and beard, put on yellow robes, and go forth from the household life into homelessness.’

‘Very good, Prince,’ said the charioteer, and taking the chariot, he returned to the inner palace. And Prince Vipassī, right there, shaving off his hair and beard, putting on yellow robes, went forth from the household life into homelessness.

The great crowd's going forth."
54.Athakho, bhikkhaveti – ‘‘pabbajitassa sādhu dhammacariyā’’tiādīni ca aññañca bahuṃ mahājanakāyena rakkhiyamānassa puttadārasambādhe ghare vasato ādīnavapaṭisaṃyuttañceva migabhūtena cetasā yathāsukhaṃ vane vasato pabbajitassa vivekānisaṃsapaṭisaṃyuttañca dhammiṃ kathaṃ sutvā pabbajitukāmo hutvā – atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī kumāro sārathiṃ āmantesi. Imāni cattāri disvā pabbajitaṃ nāma sabbabodhisattānaṃ vaṃsova tantiyeva paveṇīyeva. Aññepi ca bodhisattā yathā ayaṃ vipassī kumāro, evaṃ cirassaṃ cirassaṃ passanti. Amhākaṃ pana bodhisatto cattāripi ekadivasaṃyeva disvā mahābhinikkhamanaṃ nikkhamitvā anomānadītīre pabbajito. Teneva rājagahaṃ patvā tattha raññā bimbisārena – ‘‘kimatthaṃ, paṇḍita, pabbajitosīti’’ puṭṭho āha – ‘‘Jiṇṇañca disvā dukhitañca byādhitaṃ, Matañca disvā gatamāyusaṅkhayaṃ; Kāsāyavatthaṃ pabbajitañca disvā, Tasmā ahaṃ pabbajitomhi rājā’’ti. Mahājanakāyaanupabbajjāvaṇṇanā
54. Now then, monks—having heard the righteous talk connected with the disadvantage of living in a household crowded with children and wives, protected by a large group of people, and the advantages of seclusion for one who has gone forth, who lives in the forest at his pleasure, with a mind like a deer, and the proper practice of the Dharma for one who has gone forth—having desired to go forth, now then, monks, Vipassī Kumāra addressed the charioteer.

Having seen these four things, going forth is indeed the lineage, the continuity, the custom of all Bodhisattvas. Other Bodhisattvas also see [them] for a long, long time, just as this Vipassī Kumāra [did]. But our Bodhisattva, having seen the four sights in one day, went forth in the great going forth and became a renunciate on the bank of the Anomā River. Having gone to Rājagaha by that very means, he was asked by King Bimbisāra, "Why, wise man, have you gone forth?" He said:

"Having seen the old, the suffering, and the sick,
Having seen the dead, the end of the life span;
Having seen a renunciate wearing ochre robes,
Therefore, O king, I have gone forth."

Description of the going forth of the great multitude of people.
55
55. ‘‘Assosi kho, bhikkhave, bandhumatiyā rājadhāniyā mahājanakāyo caturāsīti pāṇasahassāni – ‘vipassī kira kumāro kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajito’ti. Sutvāna tesaṃ etadahosi – ‘na hi nūna so orako dhammavinayo, na sā orakā orikā (sī. syā.) pabbajjā, yattha vipassī kumāro kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajito. Vipassīpi nāma kumāro kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajissati, kimaṅgaṃ kimaṅga (sī.) pana maya’nti. ‘‘Atha kho, so bhikkhave, mahājanakāyo mahājanakāyo (syā.) caturāsīti pāṇasahassāni kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā vipassiṃ bodhisattaṃ agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajitaṃ anupabbajiṃsu. Tāya sudaṃ, bhikkhave, parisāya parivuto vipassī bodhisatto gāmanigamajanapadarājadhānīsu cārikaṃ carati. mahājanakāyo (syā.)
"Monks, in the city of Bandhumatī, a great crowd of eighty-four thousand people heard that ‘Prince Vipassī has shaved off his hair and beard, donned yellow robes, and gone forth from the householder’s life into homelessness.’ Having heard that, the thought occurred to them: “This is certainly no common teaching and discipline, no common going-forth, for which Prince Vipassī has shaved off hair and beard, donned yellow robes and gone forth into homelessness. If Prince Vipassī can shave off his hair and beard, don yellow robes, and go forth from the household life into homelessness, why shouldn’t we do so too?”

"Then, monks, that great crowd of eighty-four thousand people, having shaved off their hair and beards and donned yellow robes, followed the Bodhisattva Vipassī into homelessness. And with this following, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassī went on his rounds through villages, towns, districts, and royal cities.
55.Sutvāna tesanti tesaṃ caturāsītiyā pāṇasahassānaṃ sutvā etadahosi. Orakoti ūnako lāmako. Anupabbajiṃsūti anupabbajitāni . Kasmā panettha yathā parato khaṇḍatissānaṃ anupabbajjāya – ‘‘bandhumatiyā rājadhāniyā nikkhamitvā’’ti vuttaṃ, evaṃ na vuttanti? Nikkhamitvā sutattā. Ete kira sabbepi vipassissa kumārassa upaṭṭhākaparisāva, te pātova upaṭṭhānaṃ āgantvā kumāraṃ adisvā pātarāsatthāya gantvā bhuttapātarāsā āgamma ‘‘kuhiṃ kumāro’’ti pucchitvā ‘‘uyyānabhūmiṃ gato’’ti sutvā ‘‘tattheva naṃ dakkhissāmā’’ti nikkhamantā nivattamānaṃ sārathiṃ disvā – ‘‘kumāro pabbajito’’ti cassa vacanaṃ sutvā sutaṭṭhāneyeva sabbābharaṇāni omuñcitvā antarāpaṇato kāsāvapītāni vatthāni āharāpetvā kesamassuṃ ohāretvā pabbajiṃsu. Iti nagarato nikkhamitvā bahinagare sutattā ettha – ‘‘bandhumatiyā rājadhāniyā nikkhamitvā’’ti na vuttaṃ. Cārikaṃcaratīti gatagataṭṭhāne mahāmaṇḍapaṃ katvā dānaṃ sajjetvā āgamma svātanāya nimantito janassa āyācitabhikkhameva paṭiggaṇhanto cattāro māse cārikaṃ cari. Cārikaṃ caratī Ākiṇṇoti iminā gaṇena parivuto. Ayaṃ pana vitakko bodhisattassa kadā uppannoti? Sve visākhapuṇṇamā bhavissatīti cātuddasīdivase. Tadā kira so – ‘‘yatheva maṃ ime pubbe gihibhūtaṃ parivāretvā caranti, idānipi tatheva, kiṃ iminā gaṇenā’’ti gaṇasaṅgaṇikāya ukkaṇṭhitvā ‘‘ajjeva gacchāmī’’ti cintetvā puna ‘‘ajja avelā, sace idāni gamissāmi, sabbeva ime jānissanti, sveva gamissāmī’’ti cintesi. Taṃ divasañca uruvelagāmasadise gāme gāmavāsino svātanāya nimantayiṃsu. Te caturāsītisahassānampi tesaṃ pabbajitānaṃ mahāpurisassa ca pāyāsameva paṭiyādayiṃsu. Atha mahāpuriso punadivase tasmiṃyeva gāme tehi pabbajitehi saddhiṃ bhattakiccaṃ katvā vasanaṭṭhānameva agamāsi. Tattha te pabbajitā mahāpurisassa vattaṃ dassetvā attano attano rattiṭṭhānadivāṭṭhānāni paviṭṭhā. Bodhisattopi paṇṇasālaṃ pavisitvā nisinno. Ākiṇṇo ‘‘Ṭhite majjhanhike kāle, sannisīvesu pakkhisu; Saṇateva brahāraññaṃ, taṃ bhayaṃ paṭibhāti ma’’nti. (saṃ. ni. 1.15); Evarūpe avivekārāmānaṃ bhayakāle sabbasattānaṃ sadarathakāleyeva – ‘‘ayaṃ kālo’’ti nikkhamitvā paṇṇasālāya dvāraṃ pidahitvā bodhimaṇḍābhimukho pāyāsi. Aññadāpi ca tasmiṃ ṭhāne vicaranto bodhimaṇḍaṃ passati, nisīdituṃ panassa cittaṃ na namitapubbaṃ. Taṃ divasaṃ panassa ñāṇaṃ paripākagataṃ, tasmā alaṅkataṃ bodhimaṇḍaṃ disvā ārohanatthāya cittaṃ uppannaṃ. So dakkhiṇadisābhāgena upagamma padakkhiṇaṃ katvā puratthimadisābhāge cuddasahatthaṃ pallaṅkaṃ paññapetvā caturaṅgavīriyaṃ adhiṭṭhahitvā – ‘‘yāva buddho na homi, na tāva ito vuṭṭhahāmī’’ti paṭiññaṃ katvā nisīdi. Idamassa vūpakāsaṃ sandhāya – ‘‘ekova gaṇamhā vūpakaṭṭho vihāsī’’ti vuttaṃ. Aññenevatānīti te kira sāyaṃ bodhisattassa upaṭṭhānaṃ āgantvā paṇṇasālaṃ parivāretvā nisinnā ‘‘ativikālo jāto, upadhārethā’’ti vatvā paṇṇasālaṃ vivaritvā taṃ apassantāpi ‘‘kuhiṃ gato’’ti nānubandhiṃsu, ‘‘gaṇavāse nibbinno eko viharitukāmo maññe mahāpuriso, buddhabhūtaṃyeva naṃ passissāmā’’ti vatvā antojambudīpābhimukhā cārikaṃ pakkantā. Aññeneva tānī Bodhisattaabhivesavaṇṇanā
55. Having heard “sutvāna tesa”, this occurred, having heard the eighty-four thousand pāṇasahassāna.
“orako” means insufficient, inferior.
“anupabbajiṃsū” means anupabbajitāni.
Why is it not said here, as it is said elsewhere regarding the going forth of Khaṇḍatissa, “Having departed from the capital city of Bandhumatī”? Because it is well-known that they departed.
These were all the attendants of Prince Vipassī; having come to the place of attendance in the morning, not seeing the prince, having gone for breakfast, and having returned after eating breakfast, asking “Where is the prince?” and hearing “He has gone to the pleasure garden,” thinking “We will see him right there,” while going out, seeing the charioteer returning – having heard his words “The prince has gone forth,” right there in that very place, having taken off all their ornaments, having obtained ochre-colored robes from the marketplace, having shaved their heads and beards, they went forth.
Thus, it is not said here – “Having departed from the capital city of Bandhumatī” – because it is well-known that they departed from the city.

cārikaṃ caratī means, having made a large pavilion in a place suitable for going and coming, having prepared a donation, and having come, accepting only the alms requested from the people invited for the next day, he wandered on cārikaṃ for four months.

cārikaṃ caratī ākiṇṇo means surrounded by this group.
But when did this thought arise in the Bodhisattva? On the fourteenth day, [thinking,] “Tomorrow will be the Visākha full moon.”
Then he thought, “Just as these [people] formerly surrounded me and attended [to me] when I was a householder, so they do now; what is the use of this group?” Having become disgusted with the association of a group, thinking “I will go today,” then again, “Today is the wrong time; if I go now, they will all know; I will go tomorrow,” he thought.
And on that day, the villagers in a village similar to Uruvela village invited [him] for the next day.
They prepared only milk-rice for the eighty-four thousand who had gone forth, as well as for the Great Being.
Then the Great Being, on the next day, having performed the meal duties together with those who had gone forth in that very village, went to his own dwelling place.
There, those who had gone forth, having shown their duties to the Great Being, entered their own respective night-quarters and day-quarters.
The Bodhisattva also entered the leaf-hut and sat down.

ākiṇṇo “When the time is midday, with the birds having settled down;
just like a dense forest, that fear appears to me.”
(Saṃ.
Ni.
1.
15);
In such a form, at the time of fear for all beings in a place devoid of discrimination, just at the time of sorrow – “This is the time,” having departed, having closed the door of the leaf-hut, he set out towards the Bodhi-tree.
And at another time, while wandering in that place, he sees the Bodhi-tree, but his mind has never bent down to sit [there].
On that day, however, his knowledge had reached maturity; therefore, having seen the adorned Bodhi-tree, a desire arose in his mind to ascend [it].
He approached the southern side, made a circumambulation, and in the eastern part, having spread out a fourteen-cubit couch, having resolved upon fourfold effort – “Until I become a Buddha, I will not rise from here,” having made a vow, he sat down.
Referring to this seclusion of his, it is said – “Alone, secluded from the group, he dwelt.”

aññeneva tānī means, those [attendants], it is said, having come to the Bodhisattva's place of attendance in the evening, having surrounded the leaf-hut, and having sat down, [saying,] "It has become very late, reflect on it," and having opened the leaf-hut, even though they did not see him, they did not pursue [him, saying,] "Where has he gone?" [Thinking,] "The Great Being, disgusted with living in a group, I think, desires to dwell alone; we will see him when he has become a Buddha," they departed on cārikaṃ towards Jambudīpa.

aññeneva tānī Description of the Bodhisattva's Abode.
56
56. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa rahogatassa paṭisallīnassa evaṃ cetaso parivitakko udapādi – ‘na kho metaṃ na kho panetaṃ (syā.) patirūpaṃ yohaṃ ākiṇṇo viharāmi, yaṃnūnāhaṃ eko gaṇamhā vūpakaṭṭho vihareyya’nti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī bodhisatto aparena samayena eko gaṇamhā vūpakaṭṭho vihāsi , aññeneva tāni caturāsīti pabbajitasahassāni agamaṃsu, aññena maggena vipassī bodhisatto. Bodhisattaabhiniveso
"Then, monks, a thought arose in the mind of Vipassī, the bodhisattva, when he was alone and in seclusion: ‘It is not fitting for me to live crowded, may I live alone, separated from the group.’ Then, monks, after a while, Vipassī, the bodhisattva, lived alone, separated from the group, the eighty-four thousand renunciates went one way, Vipassī, the bodhisattva, went another way."
Bodhisattva's determination.
57
57. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa vāsūpagatassa rahogatassa paṭisallīnassa evaṃ cetaso parivitakko udapādi – ‘kicchaṃ vatāyaṃ loko āpanno, jāyati ca jīyati ca mīyati ca jiyyati ca miyyati ca (ka.) cavati ca upapajjati ca, atha ca panimassa dukkhassa nissaraṇaṃ nappajānāti jarāmaraṇassa, kudāssu nāma imassa dukkhassa nissaraṇaṃ paññāyissati jarāmaraṇassā’ti? ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati jarāmaraṇaṃ hoti, kiṃpaccayā jarāmaraṇa’nti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘jātiyā kho sati jarāmaraṇaṃ hoti, jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇa’nti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati jāti hoti, kiṃpaccayā jātī’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘bhave kho sati jāti hoti, bhavapaccayā jātī’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati bhavo hoti, kiṃpaccayā bhavo’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘upādāne kho sati bhavo hoti, upādānapaccayā bhavo’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati upādānaṃ hoti, kiṃpaccayā upādāna’nti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘taṇhāya kho sati upādānaṃ hoti, taṇhāpaccayā upādāna’nti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati taṇhā hoti, kiṃpaccayā taṇhā’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘vedanāya kho sati taṇhā hoti, vedanāpaccayā taṇhā’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati vedanā hoti, kiṃpaccayā vedanā’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘phasse kho sati vedanā hoti, phassapaccayā vedanā’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati phasso hoti, kiṃpaccayā phasso’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘saḷāyatane kho sati phasso hoti, saḷāyatanapaccayā phasso’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati saḷāyatanaṃ hoti, kiṃpaccayā saḷāyatana’nti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘nāmarūpe kho sati saḷāyatanaṃ hoti, nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatana’nti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati nāmarūpaṃ hoti, kiṃpaccayā nāmarūpa’nti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘viññāṇe kho sati nāmarūpaṃ hoti, viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpa’nti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho sati viññāṇaṃ hoti, kiṃpaccayā viññāṇa’nti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘nāmarūpe kho sati viññāṇaṃ hoti, nāmarūpapaccayā viññāṇa’nti.
57. Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, when he had gone to a dwelling, when he was in a secluded place, while meditating, the following thought arose in his mind: "Alas, this world is in distress! It is born, it decays, it dies, it passes away and is reborn. Yet it does not understand an escape from this suffering, from aging and death. When will an escape from this suffering, from aging and death be discerned?"

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does aging and death occur? What is the condition for aging and death?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection ( yoniso manasikārā ), gained a breakthrough of wisdom ( paññāya abhisamayo ): "When there is birth ( jāti ), aging and death occur; birth is the condition for aging and death."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does birth ( jāti ) occur? What is the condition for birth?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is becoming ( bhava ), birth occurs; becoming is the condition for birth."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does becoming ( bhava ) occur? What is the condition for becoming?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is grasping ( upādāna ), becoming occurs; grasping is the condition for becoming."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does grasping ( upādāna ) occur? What is the condition for grasping?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is craving ( taṇhā ), grasping occurs; craving is the condition for grasping."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does craving ( taṇhā ) occur? What is the condition for craving?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is feeling ( vedanā ), craving occurs; feeling is the condition for craving."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does feeling ( vedanā ) occur? What is the condition for feeling?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is contact ( phassa ), feeling occurs; contact is the condition for feeling."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does contact ( phassa ) occur? What is the condition for contact?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there are the six sense bases ( saḷāyatana ), contact occurs; the six sense bases are the condition for contact."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists do the six sense bases ( saḷāyatana ) occur? What is the condition for the six sense bases?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is name and form ( nāmarūpa ), the six sense bases occur; name and form are the condition for the six sense bases."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does name and form ( nāmarūpa ) occur? What is the condition for name and form?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is consciousness ( viññāṇa ), name and form occur; consciousness is the condition for name and form."

Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: "When what exists does consciousness ( viññāṇa ) occur? What is the condition for consciousness?" Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi, through thorough reflection, gained a breakthrough of wisdom: "When there is name and form ( nāmarūpa ), consciousness occurs; name and form are the condition for consciousness."
57.Vāsūpagatassāti bodhimaṇḍe ekarattivāsaṃ upagatassa. Rahogatassāti rahasi gatassa. Paṭisallīnassāti ekībhāvavasena nilīnassa. Kicchanti dukkhaṃ. Cavati ca upapajjati cāti idaṃ dvayaṃ pana aparāparaṃ cutipaṭisandhiṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Jarāmaraṇassāti ettha yasmā pabbajanto jiṇṇabyādhimatteyeva disvā pabbajito, tasmāssa jarāmaraṇameva upaṭṭhāti. Tenevāha – ‘‘jarāmaraṇassā’’ti. Iti jarāmaraṇaṃ mūlaṃ katvā abhiniviṭṭhassa bhavaggato otarantassa viya – atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi. Yonisomanasikārāti upāyamanasikārā pathamanasikārā. Aniccādīni hi aniccāditova manasikaroto yonisomanasikāro nāma hoti. Ayañca – ‘‘kismiṃ nu kho satijātiādīni honti, kismiṃ asati na hontī’’ti udayabbayānupassanāvasena pavattattā tesaṃ aññataro . Tasmāssa ito yonisomanasikārā iminā upāyamanasikārena ahu paññāya abhisamayo, bodhisattassa paññāya yasmiṃ sati jarāmaraṇaṃ hoti, tena jarāmaraṇakāraṇena saddhiṃ samāgamo ahosi. Kiṃ pana tanti? Jāti. Tenāha – ‘‘jātiyā kho sati jarāmaraṇaṃ hotī’’ti. Yā cāyaṃ jarāmaraṇassa kāraṇapariggāhikā paññā, tāya saddhiṃ bodhisattassa samāgamo ahosīti ayamettha attho. Etenupāyena sabbapadāni veditabbāni. Yonisomanasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo, Nāmarūpe kho sati viññāṇanti ettha pana saṅkhāresu sati viññāṇanti ca, avijjāya sati saṅkhārāti ca vattabbaṃ bhaveyya, tadubhayampi na gahitaṃ. Kasmā? Avijjāsaṅkhārā hi atīto bhavo tehi saddhiṃ ayaṃ vipassanā na ghaṭiyati. Mahāpuriso hi paccuppannavasena abhiniviṭṭhoti. Nanu ca avijjāsaṅkhārehi adiṭṭhehi na sakkā buddhena bhavitunti. Saccaṃ na sakkā, iminā pana te bhavaupādānataṇhāvaseneva diṭṭhāti. Imasmiṃ ṭhāne vitthārato paṭiccasamuppādakathā kathetabbā. Sā panesā visuddhimagge kathitāva. Nāmarūpe kho sati viññāṇa
57. "Because he approached a dwelling," meaning he approached dwelling at the seat of enlightenment for a single night.
