"Because he went to a secluded place," meaning he went to a secluded place.

"Because he was reclusive," meaning he was hidden due to a state of oneness.

"Kiccha" means suffering (duḥkha).

"Cavati ca upapajjati cā," this pair is said with reference to the immediate sequence of dissolution and rebirth-linking (cutipaṭisandhiṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ).

"Of aging and death," here, because one who goes forth goes forth only after seeing old age, sickness, and death, therefore for him, only aging and death arise.

Therefore he said, "Of aging and death."

Thus, having made aging and death the root, for one who is entirely devoted and descending from the peak of existence – then, monks, it occurred to Vipassī, the bodhisattva:

"Systematic attention," meaning skillful attention, initial attention.

Indeed, systematic attention (yonisomanasikāro) exists when one gives attention to impermanence, etc., precisely as impermanent, etc.

And this—"When what exists do birth and the like exist? When what does not exist do they not exist?"— because it proceeds by way of contemplating arising and passing away, one of these.

Therefore, from this systematic attention of his, through this skillful attention, there arose a clear understanding in wisdom (ahu paññāya abhisamayo), because aging and death exist when there is birth, for the bodhisattva's wisdom, therefore there was a convergence with the cause of aging and death.

But what is that? Birth (jāti).

Therefore he said, "When there is birth, aging and death exist."

That wisdom, moreover, which is the ascertainment of the cause of aging and death, the convergence of the bodhisattva with that—this is the meaning here.

Through this method, all words should be understood.

Systematic attention, there arose a clear understanding in wisdom (ahu paññāya abhisamayo),

"When name and form exist, there is consciousness (nāmarūpe kho sati viññāṇa)," here, however, it should be said, "When formations exist, there is consciousness," and "When ignorance exists, there are formations," but neither of these is taken up.

Why? Because ignorance and formations are past existences; this insight (vipaśyanā) is not in accordance with them.

The great person, however, is entirely devoted by way of the present.

Surely it is not possible to become a Buddha without seeing ignorance and formations?

Truly, it is not possible; however, these are seen only by way of the power of craving which is appropriation for existence.

In this place, the discourse on dependent origination (pratītyasamutpāda) should be spoken extensively.

That, however, has already been spoken in the Path of Purification (viśuddhimārga).

When name and form exist, there is consciousness (nāmarūpe kho sati viññāṇa)
58
58. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘paccudāvattati kho idaṃ viññāṇaṃ nāmarūpamhā, nāparaṃ gacchati. Ettāvatā jāyetha vā jiyyetha vā miyyetha vā cavetha vā upapajjetha vā, yadidaṃ nāmarūpapaccayā viññāṇaṃ, viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpaṃ, nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatanaṃ, saḷāyatanapaccayā phasso, phassapaccayā vedanā, vedanāpaccayā taṇhā , taṇhāpaccayā upādānaṃ, upādānapaccayā bhavo, bhavapaccayā jāti, jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṃ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti. Evametassa kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa samudayo hoti’.
58. Then, monks, the Bodhisattva Vipassi thought: “This consciousness (vijñāṇa) turns back at name-and-form (nāmarūpa), it does not go any further."

To this extent there is birth (jāti) and decay, or death, or passing away, or arising; namely: with name-and-form (nāmarūpa) as condition, there is consciousness (vijñāṇa); with consciousness (vijñāṇa) as condition, there is name-and-form (nāmarūpa); with name-and-form (nāmarūpa) as condition, there are the six sense-bases (ṣaḷāyatana); with the six sense-bases (ṣaḷāyatana) as condition, there is contact (sparśa); with contact (sparśa) as condition, there is feeling (vedanā); with feeling (vedanā) as condition, there is craving (tṛṣṇā); with craving (tṛṣṇā) as condition, there is clinging (upādāna); with clinging (upādāna) as condition, there is becoming (bhava); with becoming (bhava) as condition, there is birth (jāti); with birth (jāti) as condition, there are aging-and-death (jarāmaraṇa), sorrow (śoka), lamentation (parideva), pain (duḥkha), displeasure (daurmanasya), and despair (upāyāsa) come to be.

Thus there is the arising (samudaya) of this whole mass of suffering (duḥkhakkhandha).
58.Paccudāvattatīti paṭinivattati. Katamaṃ panettha viññāṇaṃ paccudāvattatīti? Paṭisandhiviññāṇampi vipassanāñāṇampi. Tattha paṭisandhiviññāṇaṃ paccayato paṭinivattati, vipassanāñāṇaṃ ārammaṇato. Ubhayampi nāmarūpaṃ nātikkamati, nāmarūpato paraṃ na gacchati. Ettāvatā jāyetha vātiādīsu viññāṇe nāmarūpassa paccaye honte, nāmarūpe ca viññāṇassa paccaye honte, dvīsupi aññamaññapaccayesu hontesu ettakena jāyetha vā…pe… upapajjetha vā, ito hi paraṃ kiṃ aññaṃ jāyeyya vā…pe… upapajjeyya vā. Nanu etadeva jāyati ca…pe… upapajjati cāti? Evaṃ saddhiṃ aparāparacutipaṭisandhīhi pañca padāni dassetvā puna taṃ ettāvatāti vuttamatthaṃ niyyātento – ‘‘yadidaṃ nāmarūpapaccayā viññāṇaṃ, viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpa’’nti vatvā tato paraṃ anulomapaccayākāravasena viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpamūlaṃ āyatimpi jātijarāmaraṇaṃ dassetuṃ nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatanantiādimāha. Tattha kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa samudayo hotīti sakalassa jātijarāmaraṇasokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsādibhedassa dukkharāsissa nibbatti hoti. Iti mahāpuriso sakalassa vaṭṭadukkhassa nibbattiṃ addasa.
58. "Paccudāvattatī" means paṭinivattati (returning).

What consciousness (viññāṇaṃ) returns here? Rebirth-consciousness (paṭisandhiviññāṇaṃ) and insight-wisdom (vipassanāñāṇaṃ).

There, rebirth-consciousness returns due to condition (paccayato), insight-wisdom, from its object (ārammaṇato).

Both do not go beyond name and form (nāmarūpaṃ), they do not go further than name and form.

In the statement, "By so much might one be born (jāyetha vā)," etc., when consciousness exists as the condition for name and form (nāmarūpassa paccaye honte), and name and form exists as the condition for consciousness (viññāṇassa paccaye honte), in both of these mutually conditional states (aññamaññapaccayesu hontesu), by so much might one be born (jāyetha vā)…pe… come into existence (upapajjetha vā). For what else beyond this might be born (jāyeyya vā)…pe… come into existence (upapajjeya vā)?

Surely, is it not this itself that is born (jāyati ca)…pe… comes into existence (upapajjati cāti)? Thus, showing the five factors (pañca padāni) together with successive dying and rebirth (aparāparacutipaṭisandhīhi), and then explaining the stated meaning, "By so much," he says: "Because of name and form (nāmarūpa), there is consciousness (viññāṇaṃ), because of consciousness (viññāṇa), there is name and form (nāmarūpa)." Then, further on, in the manner of direct conditionality (anulomapaccayākāravasena), consciousness is the root of name and form (nāmarūpamūlaṃ).

To show future birth, old age, and death (jātijarāmaraṇaṃ), he says, "Because of name and form (nāmarūpa), there are the six sense bases (saḷāyatana)," etc.

There, "There is the arising of the whole mass of suffering (kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa samudayo hotī)," means there is the origination (nibbatti hoti) of the entire heap of suffering (dukkharāsissa), which is differentiated (bhedassa) into all kinds of birth (jāti), old age (jarā), death (maraṇa), sorrow (śoka), lamentation (parideva), pain (dukkha), dejection (domanassa), and despair (upāyāsa).

Thus, the great person (mahāpuriso) saw the origination (nibbattiṃ addasa) of the entirety of cyclic suffering (sakalassa vaṭṭadukkhassa).
59
59. ‘‘‘Samudayo samudayo’ti kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṃ udapādi, ñāṇaṃ udapādi, paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.
And at the thought, "Origin, origin," there arose in the Bodhisattva Vipassi, with insight into things never realized before, vision (cakṣuṃ), knowledge (jñāṇaṃ), wisdom (prajñā), true knowledge (vidyā), and light (āloko).
59.Samudayosamudayoti khoti nibbatti nibbattīti kho. Pubbe ananussutesūti na anussutesu assutapubbesu. Cakkhuṃ udapādītiādīsu udayadassanapaññāvesā. Dassanaṭṭhena cakkhu, ñātakaraṇaṭṭhena ñāṇaṃ, pajānanaṭṭhena paññā, nibbijjhitvā paṭivijjhitvā uppannaṭṭhena vijjā, obhāsaṭṭhena ca ālokoti vuttā. Yathāha – ‘‘cakkhuṃ udapādīti dassanaṭṭhena. Ñāṇaṃ udapādīti ñātaṭṭhena. Paññā udapādīti pajānanaṭṭhena. Vijjā udapādīti paṭivedhaṭṭhena. Āloko udapādīti obhāsaṭṭhena. Cakkhudhammo dassanaṭṭho attho. Ñāṇadhammo ñātaṭṭho attho. Paññādhammo pajānanaṭṭho attho. Vijjādhammo paṭivedhaṭṭho attho. Āloko dhammo obhāsaṭṭho attho’’ti (paṭi. ma. 2.39). Ettakehi padehi kiṃ kathitanti? Imasmiṃ sati idaṃ hotīti paccayasañjānanamattaṃ kathitaṃ. Athavā vīthipaṭipannā taruṇavipassanā kathitāti.
59. Samudayo, samudayoti kho means arising, it means arising. Pubbe ananussutesu means in what was not previously heard, in what was not heard before. In cakkhuṃ udapādī etc., [these terms are used with] the intention of showing the wisdom of arising.

Cakkhu (eye) is stated in the sense of seeing, ñāṇaṃ (knowledge) in the sense of making known, paññā (wisdom) in the sense of understanding, vijjā (clear knowledge) in the sense of having arisen through penetrating, āloko (light) in the sense of illuminating.

As it was said: " Cakkhuṃ udapādī means in the sense of seeing. Ñāṇaṃ udapādī means in the sense of that being known. Paññā udapādī means in the sense of understanding. Vijjā udapādī means in the sense of penetration. Āloko udapādī means in the sense of illuminating. The nature of cakkhu is the meaning of seeing. The nature of ñāṇa is the meaning of that being known. The nature of paññā is the meaning of understanding. The nature of vijjā is the meaning of penetration. The nature of āloko is the meaning of illuminating" (Paṭi. Ma. 2.39).

What is said by so many terms? It is said that with this, that is, just the recognition of conditions [is said]. Or else, youthful insight that has entered the path is said.
60
60. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati jarāmaraṇaṃ na hoti, kissa nirodhā jarāmaraṇanirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘jātiyā kho asati jarāmaraṇaṃ na hoti, jātinirodhā jarāmaraṇanirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati jāti na hoti, kissa nirodhā jātinirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘bhave kho asati jāti na hoti, bhavanirodhā jātinirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati bhavo na hoti, kissa nirodhā bhavanirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘upādāne kho asati bhavo na hoti, upādānanirodhā bhavanirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati upādānaṃ na hoti, kissa nirodhā upādānanirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘taṇhāya kho asati upādānaṃ na hoti, taṇhānirodhā upādānanirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati taṇhā na hoti, kissa nirodhā taṇhānirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘vedanāya kho asati taṇhā na hoti, vedanānirodhā taṇhānirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati vedanā na hoti, kissa nirodhā vedanānirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘phasse kho asati vedanā na hoti, phassanirodhā vedanānirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati phasso na hoti, kissa nirodhā phassanirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘saḷāyatane kho asati phasso na hoti, saḷāyatananirodhā phassanirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati saḷāyatanaṃ na hoti, kissa nirodhā saḷāyatananirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘nāmarūpe kho asati saḷāyatanaṃ na hoti, nāmarūpanirodhā saḷāyatananirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati nāmarūpaṃ na hoti, kissa nirodhā nāmarūpanirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘viññāṇe kho asati nāmarūpaṃ na hoti, viññāṇanirodhā nāmarūpanirodho’ti. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘kimhi nu kho asati viññāṇaṃ na hoti, kissa nirodhā viññāṇanirodho’ti? Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa yoniso manasikārā ahu paññāya abhisamayo – ‘nāmarūpe kho asati viññāṇaṃ na hoti, nāmarūpanirodhā viññāṇanirodho’ti.
Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does ageing-and-death not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of ageing-and-death?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Birth being absent, ageing-and-death does not occur. With the cessation of birth comes the cessation of ageing-and-death.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does birth not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of birth?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Becoming being absent, birth does not occur. With the cessation of becoming comes the cessation of birth.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does becoming not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of becoming?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Clinging being absent, becoming does not occur. With the cessation of clinging comes the cessation of becoming.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does clinging not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of clinging?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Craving being absent, clinging does not occur. With the cessation of craving comes the cessation of clinging.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does craving not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of craving?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Feeling being absent, craving does not occur. With the cessation of feeling comes the cessation of craving.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does feeling not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of feeling?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Contact being absent, feeling does not occur. With the cessation of contact comes the cessation of feeling.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does contact not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of contact?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “The six sense bases being absent, contact does not occur. With the cessation of the six sense bases comes the cessation of contact.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, do the six sense bases not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of the six sense bases?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Name and form being absent, the six sense bases do not occur. With the cessation of name and form comes the cessation of the six sense bases.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, do name and form not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of name and form?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Consciousness being absent, name and form do not occur. With the cessation of consciousness comes the cessation of name and form.”

Then, mendicants, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassī: “What being absent, does consciousness not occur? With the cessation of what comes the cessation of consciousness?” Then, mendicants, as a result of the profound consideration of the Bodhisattva Vipassī, the realization dawned on him through wisdom: “Name and form being absent, consciousness does not occur. With the cessation of name and form comes the cessation of consciousness.”
61
61. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa etadahosi – ‘adhigato kho myāyaṃ maggo sambodhāya yadidaṃ – nāmarūpanirodhā viññāṇanirodho, viññāṇanirodhā nāmarūpanirodho, nāmarūpanirodhā saḷāyatananirodho, saḷāyatananirodhā phassanirodho, phassanirodhā vedanānirodho, vedanānirodhā taṇhānirodho, taṇhānirodhā upādānanirodho, upādānanirodhā bhavanirodho, bhavanirodhā jātinirodho, jātinirodhā jarāmaraṇaṃ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā nirujjhanti. Evametassa kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa nirodho hoti’.
61. Then, monks, it occurred to the Bodhisattva Vipassi: 'I have attained the path to perfect enlightenment, namely: with the cessation of name and form (nāmarūpa), consciousness (vijñāṇa) ceases; with the cessation of consciousness, name and form ceases; with the cessation of name and form, the six sense spheres (ṣaḷāyatana) cease; with the cessation of the six sense spheres, contact (sparśa) ceases; with the cessation of contact, feeling (vedanā) ceases; with the cessation of feeling, craving (tṛṣṇā) ceases; with the cessation of craving, grasping (upādāna) ceases; with the cessation of grasping, becoming (bhava) ceases; with the cessation of becoming, birth (jāti) ceases; with the cessation of birth, old age and death (jarāmaraṇa), sorrow (śoka), lamentation (parideva), pain (duḥkha), grief (daurmanasya), and despair (upāyāsa) cease. Thus, there is cessation of this entire mass of suffering (duḥkhakkhandha)'.
61.Adhigato kho myāyanti adhigato kho me ayaṃ. Maggoti vipassanāmaggo. Bodhāyāti catusaccabujjhanatthāya, nibbānabujjhanatthāya eva vā. Api ca bujjhatīti bodhi, ariyamaggassetaṃ nāmaṃ, tadatthāyātipi vuttaṃ hoti. Vipassanāmaggamūlako hi ariyamaggoti. Idāni taṃ maggaṃ niyyātento – ‘‘yadidaṃ nāmarūpanirodhātiādimāha. Ettha ca viññāṇanirodhotiādīhi paccattapadehi nibbānameva kathitaṃ. Iti mahāpuriso sakalassa vaṭṭadukkhassa anibbattinirodhaṃ addasa.
"Adhigato kho myāyanti" means "I have attained this."
"Maggoti" means "the path of insight (vipaśyanā-mārga)."
"Bodhāyati" means "for the purpose of understanding the four noble truths (catusatya), or even for the purpose of understanding nirvāṇa."
Moreover, bodhi means "awakening," this is a name for the noble path (ārya-mārga), it is also said to be for that purpose.
Indeed, the noble path has its root in the path of insight.
Now, pointing out that path, he says, "‘‘yadidaṃ nāmarūpanirodhāti," and so on.
And here, with words referring to individual experience such as viññāṇanirodhoti, only nirvāṇa is spoken of.
Thus, the great person saw the cessation of the arising of all the suffering of the cycle [of rebirth].
62
62. ‘‘‘Nirodho nirodho’ti kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bodhisattassa pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṃ udapādi, ñāṇaṃ udapādi, paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.
62. And at the thought: “Nirodha, nirodha (cessation, cessation),” there arose in the Bodhisatta Vipassi, with insight into things never realised before, the eye (cakṣu), knowledge (jñāna), wisdom (prajñā), true knowledge (vidyā), and light (āloka).
62.Nirodho nirodhoti khoti anibbatti anibbattiti kho. Cakkhuntiādīni vuttatthāneva. Idha pana sabbeheva etehi padehi – ‘‘imasmiṃ asati idaṃ na hotī’’ti nirodhasañjānanamattameva kathitaṃ, athavā vuṭṭhānagāminī balavavipassanā kathitāti.
62. Nirodho nirodhoti kho means non-arising, non-arising.
Cakkhuntiādīni [means] in the same way as previously stated.
Here, however, by all these terms, it is said that, "When this is not, this does not occur (imasmiṃ asati idaṃ na hotī)," only the understanding of cessation (nirodha-sañjānana-mātra) is described, or strong insight (balava-vipassanā) which leads to emergence (vyutthāna-gāminī) is described.
63
63. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī bodhisatto aparena samayena pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu udayabbayānupassī vihāsi – ‘iti rūpaṃ, iti rūpassa samudayo, iti rūpassa atthaṅgamo; iti vedanā, iti vedanāya samudayo, iti vedanāya atthaṅgamo; iti saññā, iti saññāya samudayo, iti saññāya atthaṅgamo; iti saṅkhārā, iti saṅkhārānaṃ samudayo, iti saṅkhārānaṃ atthaṅgamo; iti viññāṇaṃ, iti viññāṇassa samudayo, iti viññāṇassa atthaṅgamo’ti, tassa pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu udayabbayānupassino viharato na cirasseva anupādāya āsavehi cittaṃ vimuccī’’ti. Dutiyabhāṇavāro. Brahmayācanakathā
‘Then, monks, at another time Vipassī, the Bodhisattva, dwelt contemplating the rise and fall of the five aggregates of clinging (pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu): "Such is form (rūpa), such its arising (samudaya), such its passing away (atthaṅgama); such is feeling (vedanā)...; such is perception (saññā)...; such are the mental formations (saṅkhārā)...; such is consciousness (vijñāṇa), such its arising, such its passing away." And as he remained contemplating the rise and fall of the five aggregates of clinging, before long, his mind (cittaṃ) was freed from the corruptions (āsavā) without remainder (anupādāya).’

The Second Chapter.

The account of the Request to Brahmā.
63.Aparena samayenāti evaṃ paccayañca paccayanirodhañca viditvā tato aparabhāge. Upādānakkhandhesūti upādānassa paccayabhūtesu khandhesu. Udayabbayānupassīti tameva paṭhamaṃ diṭṭhaṃ udayañca vayañca anupassamāno. Vihāsīti sikhāpattaṃ vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanaṃ vahanto vihari. Idaṃ kasmā vuttaṃ? Sabbeyeva hi pūritapāramino bodhisattā pacchimabhave puttassa jātadivase mahābhinikkhamanaṃ nikkhamitvā pabbajitvā padhānamanuyuñjitvā bodhipallaṅkamāruyha mārabalaṃ vidhamitvā paṭhamayāme pubbenivāsaṃ anussaranti, dutiyayāme dibbacakkhuṃ visodhenti, tatiyayāme paccayākāraṃ sammasitvā ānāpānacatutthajjhānato uṭṭhāya pañcasu khandhesu abhinivisitvā udayabbayavasena samapaññāsa lakkhaṇāni disvā yāva gotrabhuñāṇā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā ariyamaggena sakale buddhaguṇe paṭivijjhanti. Ayampi mahāpuriso pūritapāramī. So yathāvuttaṃ sabbaṃ anukkamaṃ katvā pacchimayāme ānāpānacatutthajjhānato uṭṭhāya pañcasu khandhesu abhinivisitvā vuttappakāraṃ udayabbayavipassanaṃ ārabhi. Taṃ dassetuṃ idaṃ vuttaṃ. Tattha iti rūpanti idaṃ rūpaṃ, ettakaṃ rūpaṃ, ito uddhaṃ rūpaṃ natthīti ruppanasabhāvañceva bhūtupādāyabhedañca ādiṃ katvā lakkhaṇarasapaccupaṭṭhānapadaṭṭhānavasena anavasesarūpapariggaho vutto. Iti rūpassa samudayoti iminā evaṃ pariggahitassa rūpassa samudayadassanaṃ vuttaṃ. Tattha itīti evaṃ samudayo hotīti attho. Tassa vitthāro – ‘‘avijjāsamudayā rūpasamudayo, taṇhāsamudayā rūpasamudayo, kammasamudayā rūpasamudayo, āhārasamudayā rūpasamudayoti, nibbattilakkhaṇaṃ passantopi rūpakkhandhassa udayaṃ passatī’’ti evaṃ veditabbo. Atthaṅgamepi ‘‘avijjānirodhā rūpanirodho…pe… vipariṇāmalakkhaṇaṃ passantopi rūpakkhandhassa nirodhaṃ passatī’’ti (paṭi. ma. 1.50) ayamassa vitthāro. iti rūpa Iti rūpassa samudayo itī Iti vedanātiādīsupi ayaṃ vedanā, ettakā vedanā, ito uddhaṃ vedanā natthi. Ayaṃ saññā, ime saṅkhārā, idaṃ viññāṇaṃ, ettakaṃ viññāṇaṃ, ito uddhaṃ viññāṇaṃ natthīti vedayitasañjānanaabhisaṅkharaṇavijānanasabhāvañceva sukhādirūpasaññādi phassādi cakkhuviññāṇādi bhedañca ādiṃ katvā lakkhaṇarasapaccupaṭṭhānapadaṭṭhānavasena anavasesavedanāsaññāsaṅkhāraviññāṇapariggaho vutto. Iti vedanāya samudayotiādīhi pana evaṃ pariggahitānaṃ vedanāsaññāsaṅkhāraviññāṇānaṃ samudayadassanaṃ vuttaṃ. Tatrāpi itīti evaṃ samudayo hotīti attho. Tesampi vitthāro – ‘‘avijjāsamudayā vedanāsamudayo’’ti (paṭi. ma. 1.50) rūpe vuttanayeneva veditabbo. Ayaṃ pana viseso – tīsu khandhesu ‘‘āhārasamudayā’’ti avatvā ‘‘phassasamudayā’’ti vattabbaṃ. Viññāṇakkhandhe ‘‘nāmarūpasamudayā’’ti atthaṅgamapadampi tesaṃyeva vasena yojetabbaṃ. Ayamettha saṅkhepo, vitthāro pana udayabbayavinicchayo sabbākāraparipūro visuddhimagge vutto. Tassa pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu udayabbayānupassino viharatoti tassa vipassissa bodhisattassa imesu rūpādīsu pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu samapaññāsalakkhaṇavasena udayabbayānupassino viharato yathānukkamena vaḍḍhite vipassanāñāṇe anuppādanirodhena nirujjhamānehi āsavasaṅkhātehi kilesehi anupādāya aggahetvāva cittaṃ vimuccati, tadetaṃ maggakkhaṇe vimuccati nāma, phalakkhaṇe vimuttaṃ nāma; maggakkhaṇe vā vimuttañceva vimuccati ca, phalakkhaṇe vimuttameva. Iti vedanā Iti vedanāya samudayo itī Tassa pañcasu upādānakkhandhesu udayabbayānupassino viharato Ettāvatā ca mahāpuriso sabbabandhanā vippamutto sūriyarasmisamphuṭṭhamiva padumaṃ suvikasitacittasantāno cattāri maggañāṇāni, cattāri phalañāṇāni, catasso paṭisambhidā, catuyoniparicchedakañāṇaṃ, pañcagatiparicchedakañāṇaṃ, cha asādhāraṇañāṇāni, sakale ca buddhaguṇe hatthagate katvā paripuṇṇasaṅkappo bodhipallaṅke nisinnova – ‘‘Anekajātisaṃsāraṃ, sandhāvissaṃ anibbisaṃ; Gahakāraṃ gavesanto, dukkhā jāti punappunaṃ. Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi, puna gehaṃ na kāhasi; Sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, gahakūṭaṃ visaṅkhataṃ; Visaṅkhāragataṃ cittaṃ, taṇhānaṃ khayamajjhagā’’ti. (dha. pa. 153, 154); ‘‘Ayoghanahatasseva, jalato jātavedaso; Anupubbūpasantassa, yathā na ñāyate gati. Evaṃ sammāvimuttānaṃ, kāmabandhoghatārinaṃ; Paññāpetuṃ gati natthi, pattānaṃ acalaṃ sukha’’nti. (udā. 80); Evaṃ manasi karonto sarade sūriyo viya, puṇṇacando viya ca virocitthāti. Dutiyabhāṇavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Brahmayācanakathāvaṇṇanā
63.
"With another occasion," thus, having known the condition and the cessation of conditions, then in the other part.
"In the clinging aggregates," in the aggregates that are the condition for clinging.
"Observing rise and fall," thus, that very first seen, observing both rise and fall.
"Abiding," carrying the crest-jewel, the insight leading to emergence, he abides.
Why is this said? For all those bodhisattvas who have fulfilled the pāramitās, in the final existence, on the day of the son's birth, having renounced the great renunciation, having gone forth, having practiced exertion, having ascended the bodhi-seat, having shattered Māra's forces, in the first watch, they recollect their former abodes, in the second watch, they purify the divine eye, in the third watch, having reflected on the causal mode, having arisen from the fourth jhāna of mindfulness of breathing, having applied themselves to the five aggregates, having seen the fifty-four characteristics by way of rise and fall, having developed insight as far as the gotrabhu knowledge, they penetrate all the Buddha qualities with the noble path.
This great being also has fulfilled the pāramīs.
He, having done all that was said in order, in the final watch, having arisen from the fourth jhāna of mindfulness of breathing, having applied himself to the five aggregates, began the insight of rise and fall in the manner that was said.
To show that, this was said.

There, "Thus form," this is form, so much is form, there is no form higher than this, having made the nature of disintegration, the division of the elements and the derived [elements], etc., the beginning, the complete grasp of form without remainder is said by way of the characteristics, taste, manifestation, and proximate cause.
"Thus the arising of form," by this, the seeing of the arising of form that was thus grasped is said.
There, "Thus," thus the arising is, is the meaning.
Its expansion: "From the arising of ignorance, the arising of form; from the arising of craving, the arising of form; from the arising of karma, the arising of form; from the arising of nutriment, the arising of form," thus, even seeing the characteristic of production, one sees the arising of the aggregate of form, thus it should be understood.
In cessation also, "From the cessation of ignorance, the cessation of form…pe… even seeing the characteristic of change, one sees the cessation of the aggregate of form" (Paṭi.
Ma.
1.
50) this is its expansion.

Thus form, thus the arising of form, thus.
"Thus feeling," etc., in these also, this is feeling, so much is feeling, there is no feeling higher than this.
This is perception, these are formations, this is consciousness, so much is consciousness, there is no consciousness higher than this, having made the nature of experiencing, perceiving, constructing, cognizing, the beginning, and also the division of pleasant form perception, etc., contact, etc., eye-consciousness, etc., the complete grasp of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness without remainder is said by way of the characteristics, taste, manifestation, and proximate cause.
"Thus the arising of feeling," etc., by these, the seeing of the arising of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness that were thus grasped is said.
There also, "Thus," thus the arising is, is the meaning.
For those also, the expansion: "From the arising of ignorance, the arising of feeling" (Paṭi.
Ma.
1.
50) should be understood just as it was said in form.
This, however, is the distinction: in the three aggregates, without saying "from the arising of nutriment," one should say "from the arising of contact."
In the aggregate of consciousness, "from the arising of name and form," the word for cessation also should be connected in dependence on those very [aggregates].
This is the condensation here, the expansion, however, the determination of rise and fall, complete in all aspects, is said in the Visuddhimagga.
"Of him, abiding observing rise and fall in the five clinging aggregates," of that discerning bodhisattva, in these five clinging aggregates beginning with form, abiding observing rise and fall by way of the fifty-four characteristics, in the insight knowledge increased according to order, the mind is liberated without taking up, without grasping, because of the defilements called influxes ceasing with the cessation of non-arising, that is, at the moment of the path, it is called liberated, at the moment of the fruit, it is called released; or, at the moment of the path, it is both liberated and being liberated, at the moment of the fruit, it is only released.

Thus feeling, thus the arising of feeling, thus.
"Of him, abiding observing rise and fall in the five clinging aggregates"

To this extent, the great being, completely released from all bonds, with a mind-stream like a lotus touched by the sun's rays, well-blossomed, having made the four path knowledges, the four fruit knowledges, the four discriminations, the knowledge that distinguishes the four births, the knowledge that distinguishes the five destinies, the six uncommon knowledges, and all the Buddha qualities attainable, with perfected intention, is seated on the bodhi seat—

"Through many births in saṃsāra, I ran without finding rest;

seeking the house-builder, painful is birth again and again."
O builder, I have seen you; you shall not build this house again. All your rafters are broken, your ridgepole is destroyed. My mind has reached the Unconditioned; it has attained the destruction of craving." (Dha. Pa. 153, 154);

‘‘Just as the path of fire that has consumed fuel, struck by a hammer of iron, gradually subsides, is not known;

Even so, there is no way to trace those who are rightly liberated, who have crossed over the flood of sensual bonds, who have attained unwavering bliss.’’ (Udā. 80);

Reflecting thus, he shone like the autumn sun and like the full moon.

The explanation of the Second Bhāṇavāra is finished.

Explanation of the story of the Brahma's Request
64
64. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa etadahosi – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ dhammaṃ deseyya’nti. Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa etadahosi – ‘adhigato kho myāyaṃ dhammo gambhīro duddaso duranubodho santo paṇīto atakkāvacaro nipuṇo paṇḍitavedanīyo. Ālayarāmā kho panāyaṃ pajā ālayaratā ālayasammuditā. Ālayarāmāya kho pana pajāya ālayaratāya ālayasammuditāya duddasaṃ idaṃ ṭhānaṃ yadidaṃ idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppādo. Idampi kho ṭhānaṃ duddasaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbānaṃ. Ahañceva kho pana dhammaṃ deseyyaṃ, pare ca me na ājāneyyuṃ; so mamassa kilamatho, sā mamassa vihesā’ti.
‘Then, monks, it occurred to Vipassi, the Blessed One, the worthy one, the perfectly enlightened Buddha: “Suppose now I were to teach the Dhamma (dharma)?”

‘Then, monks, it occurred to Vipassi, the Blessed One, the worthy one, the perfectly enlightened Buddha: “This Dhamma (dharma) that I have attained is profound, hard to see, hard to understand, peaceful, excellent, beyond reasoning, subtle, to be apprehended by the wise.

But this generation delights in attachment (ālaya), is excited by attachment (ālaya), enjoys attachment (ālaya).

For a generation delighting in attachment (ālaya), excited by attachment (ālaya), enjoying attachment (ālaya), this state is hard to see, namely, this/that conditionality and dependent co-arising (paṭiccasamuppāda).

And it is hard to see this truth, namely, the stilling of all formations (saṅkhāra), the relinquishing of all acquisitions (upadhi), the destruction of craving (taṇhā), dispassion (virāga), cessation (nirodha), Nibbāna (nibbāna).

And if I were to teach the Dhamma (dharma), others would not understand me, and that would be tiresome for me, troublesome for me.”’
64. Tatiyabhāṇavāre yaṃnūnāhaṃ dhammaṃ deseyyanti yadi panāhaṃ dhammaṃ deseyyaṃ. Ayaṃ pana vitakko kadā uppannoti? Buddhabhūtassa aṭṭhame sattāhe. So kira buddho hutvā sattāhaṃ bodhipallaṅke nisīdi, sattāhaṃ bodhipallaṅkaṃ olokento aṭṭhāsi, sattāhaṃ ratanacaṅkame caṅkami, sattāhaṃ ratanagabbhe dhammaṃ vicinanto nisīdi, sattāhaṃ ajapālanigrodhe nisīdi, sattāhaṃ mucalinde nisīdi, sattāhaṃ rājāyatane nisīdi. Tato uṭṭhāya aṭṭhame sattāhe puna āgantvā ajapālanigrodhe nisinnamattasseva sabbabuddhānaṃ āciṇṇasamāciṇṇo ayañceva ito anantaro ca vitakko uppannoti. Tattha adhigatoti paṭividdho. Dhammoti catusaccadhammo. Gambhīroti uttānabhāvapaṭikkhepavacanametaṃ. Duddasoti gambhīrattāva duddaso dukkhena daṭṭhabbo, na sakkā sukhena daṭṭhuṃ. Duddasattāva duranubodho dukkhena avabujjhitabbo, na sakkā sukhena avabujjhituṃ. Santoti nibbuto. Paṇītoti atappako. Idaṃ dvayaṃ lokuttarameva sandhāya vuttaṃ. Atakkāvacaroti takkena avacaritabbo ogāhitabbo na hoti, ñāṇeneva avacaritabbo. Nipuṇoti saṇho. Paṇḍitavedanīyoti sammāpaṭipadaṃ paṭipannehi paṇḍitehi veditabbo. Ālayarāmāti sattā pañcasu kāmaguṇesu allīyanti, tasmā te ālayāti vuccanti. Aṭṭhasatataṇhāvicaritāni ālayanti, tasmā ālayāti vuccanti. Tehi ālayehi ramantīti ālayarāmā. Ālayesu ratāti ālayaratā. Ālayesu suṭṭhu muditāti ālayasammuditā. Yatheva hi susajjitaṃ pupphaphalabharitarukkhādisampannaṃ uyyānaṃ paviṭṭho rājā tāya tāya sampattiyā ramati, pamudito āmodito hoti, na ukkaṇṭhati, sāyaṃ nikkhamituṃ na icchati, evamimehipi kāmālayataṇhālayehi sattā ramanti, saṃsāravaṭṭe pamuditā anukkaṇṭhitā vasanti. Tena nesaṃ bhagavā duvidhampi ālayaṃ uyyānabhūmiṃ viya dassento – ‘‘ālayarāmā’’tiādimāha. adhigato Dhammo Gambhīro Duddaso duranubodho Santo Paṇīto Atakkāvacaro Nipuṇo Paṇḍitavedanīyo Ālayarāmā ālayarāmā. ālayaratā. ālayasammuditā. Yadidanti nipāto, tassa ṭhānaṃ sandhāya – ‘‘yaṃ ida’’nti, paṭiccasamuppādaṃ sandhāya – ‘‘yo aya’’nti evamattho daṭṭhabbo. Idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppādoti imesaṃ paccayā idappaccayā, idappaccayā eva idappaccayatā, idappaccayatā ca sā paṭiccasamuppādo cāti idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppādo. Saṅkhārādipaccayānaṃ avijjādīnaṃ etaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Sabbasaṅkhārasamathotiādi sabbaṃ nibbānameva. Yasmā hi taṃ āgamma sabbasaṅkhāravipphanditāni sammanti vūpasammanti tasmā – ‘‘sabbasaṅkhārasamatho’’ti vuccati. Yasmā ca taṃ āgamma sabbe upadhayo paṭinissaṭṭhā honti, sabbā taṇhā khīyanti , sabbe kilesarāgā virajjanti, sabbaṃ dukkhaṃ nirujjhati, tasmā ‘‘sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho’’ti vuccati. Sā panesā taṇhā bhavena bhavaṃ, phalena vā saddhiṃ kammaṃ vinati saṃsibbatīti katvā vānanti vuccati. Tato vānato nikkhantanti nibbānaṃ. So mamassa kilamathoti yā ajānantānaṃ desanā nāma, so mama kilamatho assa, sā mama vihesā assāti attho. Kāyakilamatho ceva kāyavihesā ca assāti vuttaṃ hoti, citte pana ubhayampetaṃ buddhānaṃ natthi. Yadida Idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppādo Sabbasaṅkhārasamatho nibbānaṃ. So mamassa kilamatho
64. In the third recitation section, on page V2.0049 and M2.0055: "Should I now teach the Dharma?" If I were to teach the Dharma...

This thought, when did it arise? In the eighth week of the one who had become Buddha.

It is said that the Buddha, having become Buddha, sat for seven weeks on the bodhi platform; for seven weeks he stood gazing at the bodhi platform; for seven weeks he walked in the jeweled cloister; for seven weeks he sat in the jeweled chamber contemplating the Dharma; for seven weeks he sat under the ajapāla nigrodha tree; for seven weeks he sat in mucalinda; for seven weeks he sat in rājāyatana.

Then, rising up, in the eighth week, coming again to the ajapāla nigrodha tree, just as he was sitting, on page P2.0464, this thought, usual and customary for all Buddhas, the immediately preceding one, arose.

There, "attained" means penetrated.

"Dharma" means the Four Noble Truths (catusatya-dharma).

"Profound" means a statement rejecting the state of being shallow.

"Difficult to see" means because of being profound, it is difficult to see; it must be seen with difficulty; it is not possible to see easily.

Because it is difficult to see, "difficult to understand" means it must be understood with difficulty; it is not possible to understand easily.

"Serene" means extinguished (nirvuto).

"Excellent" means without heat (atappako).

This duality is said with reference only to the supramundane.

"Beyond the sphere of reasoning" means it cannot be traversed or fathomed by reasoning; it can only be traversed by wisdom (jñānena).

"Subtle" means refined (saṇho).

"To be experienced by the wise" means it should be known by the wise who have embarked on the right path (samyak-pratipāda).

"Ālayarāmā" means beings cling to the qualities of the five senses; therefore, they are called "dwellings" (ālaya).

The cravings consisting of eighty-one hundred thousand defilements cling, therefore, they are called "dwellings" (ālaya).

Because they delight in those dwellings (ālayehi ramantīti), they are "ālayarāmā."

Because they are attached to dwellings (ālayesu ratāti), they are "ālayaratā."

Because they are very pleased in dwellings (ālayesu suṭṭhu muditāti), they are "ālayasammuditā."

Just as a king, having entered a well-arranged garden, complete with trees laden with flowers and fruits, delights in that attainment, is delighted and rejoices, does not feel weary, and does not wish to leave in the evening, so beings delight in these dwellings of craving and attachment to sense objects, and reside in the round of saṃsāra, delighted and without weariness.

Therefore, the Blessed One, showing both kinds of dwelling as like a garden, said, beginning with "ālayarāmā."

Attained, Dharma, profound, difficult to see, difficult to understand, serene, excellent, beyond the sphere of reasoning, subtle, to be experienced by the wise, ālayarāmā, ālayarāmā, ālayaratā, ālayasammuditā.

"yad ida" is an indeclinable. With reference to its place, "what is this?" With reference to dependent origination (pratītyasamutpāda), "who is this?" Thus, the meaning should be seen.

"idappaccayatā pratītyasamutpāda" means because of these conditions (pratyaya), there are these conditions; these conditions are just this conditionality (idappaccayatā), and that conditionality is also dependent origination (pratītyasamutpāda); therefore, it is "idappaccayatā pratītyasamutpāda."

This is a designation of ignorance (avidyā) and so forth, the conditions (pratyaya) beginning with saṃskāra.

"sarvasaṃkhārasamatha" and so forth is just all nirvāṇa.

Because, indeed, having come to that, all the manifestations of saṃskāra are calmed, subside, therefore, it is called "sarvasaṃkhārasamatha."

And because, having come to that, all attachments (upadhi) are relinquished, all craving (tṛṣṇā) is destroyed, all defilements (kleśa) and passions (rāga) are extinguished, all suffering (duḥkha) ceases, therefore, it is called "the relinquishing of all attachments, the destruction of craving, dispassion (virāga), cessation (nirodha)."

But that craving, having made existence (bhava) into existence, or together with the fruit, weaves karma, sews it together; therefore, it is called desire (vāna).

From that desire having gone out, it is nirvāṇa.
"That would be tiring for me" – that is instruction for those who do not know. That would be tiring for me, that would be troublesome to me, is the meaning.

Bodily fatigue and bodily trouble there would be, it is said; but in the mind, both of these things, for the Buddhas, do not exist.

What is this? This is the arising of dependent origination conditioned by this (idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppādo).

The calming of all formations (sabbasaṅkhārasamatho)

Nirvāṇa.

That would be tiring for me.
65
65. ‘‘Apissu, bhikkhave, vipassiṃ bhagavantaṃ arahantaṃ sammāsambuddhaṃ imā anacchariyā gāthāyo paṭibhaṃsu pubbe assutapubbā – ‘Kicchena me adhigataṃ, halaṃ dāni pakāsituṃ; Rāgadosaparetehi, nāyaṃ dhammo susambudho. ‘Paṭisotagāmiṃ nipuṇaṃ, gambhīraṃ duddasaṃ aṇuṃ; Rāgarattā na dakkhanti, tamokhandhena āvuṭā’ti. ‘‘Itiha , bhikkhave, vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa paṭisañcikkhato appossukkatāya cittaṃ nami, no dhammadesanāya.
"Furthermore, monks, these amazing verses, never heard before, occurred to Vipassī, the Blessed One, the Worthy One, the perfectly enlightened one:

'With difficulty I have attained (this), enough of proclaiming (it) now;
This Dhamma is not easily understood by those overcome with lust and hatred.

'Going against the stream, subtle, deep, hard to see, minute;
Those dyed in lust will not see (it), shrouded in a mass of darkness.'

"Thus, monks, as the Lord Buddha Vipassī pondered, his mind was inclined to inaction rather than to teaching the Dhamma.
65.Apissūti anubrūhanatthe nipāto. So – ‘‘na kevalaṃ etadahosi, imāpi gāthā paṭibhaṃsū’’ti dīpeti. Vipassintiādīsu vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassāti attho. Anacchariyāti anuacchariyā. Paṭibhaṃsūti paṭibhānasaṅkhātassa ñāṇassa gocarā ahesuṃ, parivitakkayitabbataṃ pāpuṇiṃsu. Kicchenāti dukkhena, na dukkhāya paṭipadāya. Buddhānañhi cattāropi maggā sukhapaṭipadāva honti. Pāramīpūraṇakāle pana sarāgasadosasamohasseva sato āgatāgatānaṃ yācakānaṃ alaṅkatapaṭiyattaṃ sīsaṃ chinditvā galalohitaṃ nīharitvā suañjitāni akkhīni uppāṭetvā kulavaṃsapadīpakaṃ puttaṃ manāpacāriniṃ bhariyanti evamādīni dentassa aññāni ca khantivādisadisesu attabhāvesu chejjabhejjādīni pāpuṇantassa āgamanīyapaṭipadaṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Halanti ettha hakāro nipātamatto, alanti attho. Pakāsitunti desetuṃ; evaṃ kicchena adhigatassa dhammassa alaṃ desetuṃ; ko attho desitenāti vuttaṃ hoti. Rāgadosaparetehīti rāgadosaphuṭṭhehi rāgadosānugatehi vā. Kicchenā Hala Pakāsitu Rāgadosaparetehī Paṭisotagāminti niccādīnaṃ paṭisotaṃ aniccaṃ dukkhamanattāsubhanti evaṃ gataṃ catusaccadhammaṃ. Rāgarattāti kāmarāgena bhavarāgena diṭṭhirāgena ca rattā. Na dakkhantīti aniccaṃ dukkhamanattā asubhanti iminā sabhāvena na passissanti , te apassante ko sakkhissati evaṃ gāhāpetuṃ? Tamokhandhena āvuṭāti avijjārāsinā ajjhotthaṭā. Paṭisotagāmi Rāgarattā Na dakkhantī Tamokhandhena āvuṭā Appossukkatāyāti nirussukkabhāvena, adesetukāmatāyāti attho. Kasmā panassa evaṃ cittaṃ nami? Nanu esa – ‘‘mutto mocessāmī, tiṇṇo tāressāmi’’, Appossukkatāyā ‘‘Kiṃ me aññātavesena, dhammaṃ sacchikatenidha; Sabbaññutaṃ pāpuṇitvā, santāressaṃ sadevaka’’nti. Patthanaṃ katvā pāramiyo pūretvā sabbaññutaṃ pattoti. Saccametaṃ, paccavekkhaṇānubhāvena panassa evaṃ cittaṃ nami. Tassa hi sabbaññutaṃ patvā sattānaṃ kilesagahanataṃ dhammassa ca gambhīrataṃ paccavekkhantassa sattānaṃ kilesagahanatā ca dhammagambhīratā ca sabbākārena pākaṭā jātā. Athassa – ‘‘ime sattā kañjikapuṇṇalābu viya takkabharitacāṭi viya vasātelapītapilotikā viya añjanamakkhitahatthā viya kilesabharitā atisaṃkiliṭṭhā rāgarattā dosaduṭṭhā mohamūḷhā, te kiṃ nāma paṭivijjhissantī’’ti cintayato kilesagahanapaccavekkhaṇānubhāvenāpi evaṃ cittaṃ nami. ‘‘Ayañca dhammo pathavīsandhārakaudakakkhandho viya gambhīro, pabbatena paṭicchādetvā ṭhapito sāsapo viya duddaso, satadhā bhinnassa vālassa koṭiyā koṭiṃ paṭipādanaṃ viya duranubodho, nanu mayā hi imaṃ dhammaṃ paṭivijjhituṃ vāyamantena adinnaṃ dānaṃ nāma natthi, arakkhitaṃ sīlaṃ nāma natthi, aparipūritā kāci pāramī nāma natthi. Tassa me nirussāhaṃ viya mārabalaṃ vidhamantassāpi pathavī na kampittha, paṭhamayāme pubbenivāsaṃ anussarantassāpi na kampittha, majjhimayāme dibbacakkhuṃ visodhentassāpi na kampittha, pacchimayāme pana paṭiccasamuppādaṃ paṭivijjhantasseva me dasasahassilokadhātu kampittha. Iti mādisenāpi tikkhañāṇena kicchenevāyaṃ dhammo paṭividdho taṃ lokiyamahājanā kathaṃ paṭivijjhissantī’’ti dhammagambhīratāpaccavekkhaṇānubhāvenāpi evaṃ cittaṃ namīti veditabbaṃ. Apica brahmunā yācite desetukāmatāyapissa evaṃ cittaṃ nami. Jānāti hi bhagavā – ‘‘mama appossukkatāya citte namamāne maṃ mahābrahmā dhammadesanaṃ yācissati, ime ca sattā brahmagarukā, te ‘satthā kira dhammaṃ na desetukāmo ahosi, atha naṃ mahābrahmā yācitvā desāpesi, santo vata bho dhammo, paṇīto vata bho dhammo’ti maññamānā sussūsissantī’’ti. Imampissa kāraṇaṃ paṭicca appossukkatāya cittaṃ nami, no dhammadesanāyāti veditabbaṃ.
65. Apissū is a particle [nipāta] in the sense of strengthening [anubrūhana].

So, he illuminates, "Not only was this so, these verses arose as well."

Vipassi etc., means the meaning of Vipassi, the Blessed One, the Arhat, the Perfectly Enlightened Buddha.

Anacchariyā means not wonderful [anuacchariyā].

Paṭibhaṃsū means they were the range of knowledge [ñāṇa] known as insight [paṭibhāna], they attained the state of being subject to reflection [parivitakkayitabbataṃ pāpuṇiṃsu].

Kicchenā means with difficulty [dukkhena], not by the difficult path [dukkhāya paṭipadāya].

For the Buddhas, all four paths are paths of ease [sukhapaṭipadāva] .

However, at the time of fulfilling the perfections [pāramī], even while being full of lust, aversion, and delusion [sarāgasadosasamohasseva sato], he gave away his ornamented and prepared head to the beggars who came and came, having cut it off, and having drawn out the blood of the throat, and having plucked out his well-anointed eyes, and a son who illuminates the family lineage, and a wife who is pleasing in her conduct; this was said with reference to the practice that is to be followed [āgamanīyapaṭipadaṃ sandhāya etaṃ vuttaṃ] by one who attains [pāpuṇantassa] other existences similar to the Khantivādi, where cutting and piercing etc. [chejjabhejjādīni] are present.

Hala means here the letter ha is merely a particle [nipātamatto], ala is the meaning.

Pakāsitu means to teach [desetuṃ]; thus it is said [vuttaṃ hoti], "Enough to teach the Dharma that has been attained with difficulty [kicchena adhigatassa dhammassa alaṃ desetuṃ]; what is the use of teaching it [ko attho desitenāti]?"

Rāgadosaparetehī means touched by lust and aversion [rāgadosaphuṭṭhehi] or accompanied by lust and aversion [rāgadosānugatehi vā].

Kicchenā hala pakāsitu rāgadosaparetehī

Paṭisotagāmi means going against the stream [paṭisotaṃ] of the permanent etc. [niccādīnaṃ], the impermanent [aniccaṃ], suffering [dukkhaṃ], non-self [anattā], and impure [asubhaṃ]; thus the Four Noble Truths [catusaccadhammaṃ] are attained [gataṃ].

Rāgarattā means stained [rattā] by sensual desire [kāmarāgena], desire for existence [bhavarāgena], and desire for views [diṭṭhirāgena].

Na dakkhantī means they will not see [na passissanti] the impermanent [aniccaṃ], suffering [dukkhaṃ], non-self [anattā], and impure [asubhaṃ] with this nature [sabhāvena]; who will be able to make them grasp [evaṃ gāhāpetuṃ], when they do not see [te apassante ko sakkhissati]?

Tamokhandhena āvuṭā means covered [āvuṭā] by a mass of ignorance [avijjārāsinā ajjhotthaṭā].

Paṭisotagāmi rāgarattā na dakkhantī tamokhandhena āvuṭā

Appossukkatāyā means by lack of enthusiasm [nirussukkabhāvena], the meaning is by lack of desire to teach [adesetukāmatāyāti attho].

Why did his mind turn thus [evaṃ cittaṃ nami]? Surely he thought, "Having been freed, I will free [mutto mocessāmī], having crossed over, I will carry over [tiṇṇo tāressāmi],"

Appossukkatāyā

"What [kiṃ] use is it for me with an unknown appearance [aññātavesena], the Dharma having been realized here [dhammaṃ sacchikatenidha];

Having attained omniscience [sabbaññutaṃ pāpuṇitvā], I will deliver [santāressaṃ] those with the gods [sadevaka]?"

Having made the aspiration [patthanaṃ katvā], having fulfilled the perfections [pāramiyo pūretvā], he attained omniscience [sabbaññutaṃ pattoti].

This is true [saccametaṃ], but his mind turned thus [evaṃ cittaṃ nami] due to the power of reflection [paccavekkhaṇānubhāvena].

For when he had attained omniscience [sabbaññutaṃ patvā] and was reflecting on the depth of the defilements of beings [sattānaṃ kilesagahanataṃ] and the profundity of the Dharma [dhammassa ca gambhīrataṃ paccavekkhantassa], both the depth of the defilements of beings [sattānaṃ kilesagahanatā ca] and the profundity of the Dharma [dhammagambhīratā ca] became manifest in every way [sabbākārena pākaṭā jātā].

Then the thought occurred to him [athassa] – “These beings [ime sattā] are like gourds filled with kañjika [kañjikapuṇṇalābu viya], like bowls filled with buttermilk [takkabharitacāṭi viya], like wicks soaked in sesame oil [vasātelapītapilotikā viya], like hands smeared with lampblack [añjanamakkhitahatthā viya], full of defilements [kilesabharitā], extremely stained [atisaṃkiliṭṭhā], stained with desire [rāgarattā], corrupted by aversion [dosaduṭṭhā], deluded by delusion [mohamūḷhā], what indeed will they penetrate [te kiṃ nāma paṭivijjhissantī]?” – his mind turned thus [evaṃ cittaṃ nami] due to the power of reflecting on the depth of the defilements [kilesagahanapaccavekkhaṇānubhāvenāpi].

“And this Dharma [ayañca dhammo] is profound [gambhīro] like a foundation of earth and a mass of water [pathavīsandhārakaudakakkhandho viya], difficult to see [duddaso] like mustard seed placed having covered it with a mountain [pabbatena paṭicchādetvā ṭhapito sāsapo viya], difficult to understand [duranubodho] like analyzing the tip of a hair that has been split into a hundred parts [satadhā bhinnassa vālassa koṭiyā koṭiṃ paṭipādanaṃ viya], indeed, while I was striving to penetrate this Dharma [imaṃ dhammaṃ paṭivijjhituṃ vāyamantena], there is no gift that was not given [adinnaṃ dānaṃ nāma natthi], there is no morality that was not guarded [arakkhitaṃ sīlaṃ nāma natthi], there is no perfection that was not fulfilled [aparipūritā kāci pāramī nāma natthi].

Even when I was dispelling the army of Māra [mārabalaṃ vidhamantassāpi] as if without enthusiasm [nirussāhaṃ viya], the earth did not tremble [pathavī na kampittha], even when I was recollecting my former abodes [pubbenivāsaṃ anussarantassāpi] in the first watch [paṭhamayāme], it did not tremble [na kampittha], even when I was purifying the divine eye [dibbacakkhuṃ visodhentassāpi] in the middle watch [majjhimayāme], it did not tremble [na kampittha], but in the last watch [pacchimayāme], just as I was penetrating dependent origination [paṭiccasamuppādaṃ paṭivijjhantasseva], the ten-thousand world-system trembled [dasasahassilokadhātu kampittha].”

Thus, even with sharp wisdom [tikkhañāṇena] like mine, this Dharma has been penetrated with difficulty [kicchenevāyaṃ dhammo paṭividdho], how will those ordinary people [taṃ lokiyamahājanā] penetrate [kathaṃ paṭivijjhissantī]?” It should be understood that his mind turned thus [evaṃ cittaṃ namīti veditabbaṃ] due to the power of reflecting on the profundity of the Dharma [dhammagambhīratāpaccavekkhaṇānubhāvenāpi].

Moreover [apica], when he was requested by Brahmā [brahmunā yācite], his mind turned thus [evaṃ cittaṃ nami] out of a desire to teach [desetukāmatāyapi],

For the Blessed One knows [jānāti hi bhagavā] – “When my mind turns due to lack of enthusiasm [mama appossukkatāya citte namamāne], Mahābrahmā will request me to teach the Dharma [maṃ mahābrahmā dhammadesanaṃ yācissati], and these beings [ime ca sattā] are respectful of Brahmā [brahmagarukā], thinking, ‘The Teacher was not willing to teach the Dharma [satthā kira dhammaṃ na desetukāmo ahosi], then Mahābrahmā requested him and made him teach [atha naṃ mahābrahmā yācitvā desāpesi], truly, the Dharma is peaceful [santo vata bho dhammo], truly, the Dharma is excellent [paṇīto vata bho dhammo]’, they will be eager to listen [maññamānā sussūsissantī]”.

Having rejected this reason too [imampissa kāraṇaṃ paṭicca], his mind turned due to lack of enthusiasm [appossukkatāya cittaṃ nami], not due to teaching the Dharma [no dhammadesanāyāti veditabbaṃ].
66
66. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, aññatarassa mahābrahmuno vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa cetasā cetoparivitakkamaññāya etadahosi – ‘nassati vata bho loko, vinassati vata bho loko, yatra hi nāma vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa appossukkatāya cittaṃ namati nami (syā. ka.), namissati (?), no dhammadesanāyā’ti. Atha kho so, bhikkhave, mahābrahmā seyyathāpi nāma balavā puriso samiñjitaṃ vā bāhaṃ pasāreyya, pasāritaṃ vā bāhaṃ samiñjeyya; evameva brahmaloke antarahito vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa purato pāturahosi. Atha kho so, bhikkhave, mahābrahmā ekaṃsaṃ uttarāsaṅgaṃ karitvā dakkhiṇaṃ jāṇumaṇḍalaṃ pathaviyaṃ nihantvā nidahanto (syā.) yena vipassī bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho tenañjaliṃ paṇāmetvā vipassiṃ bhagavantaṃ arahantaṃ sammāsambuddhaṃ etadavoca – ‘desetu, bhante, bhagavā dhammaṃ, desetu sugato dhammaṃ, santi santī (syā.) sattā apparajakkhajātikā; assavanatā dhammassa parihāyanti, bhavissanti dhammassa aññātāro’ti.
66. Then, monks, the reasoning of a certain Great Brahmā, Vipassi, the Blessed One, the Arhat, the perfectly enlightened Buddha, became mentally known through the awareness of the thoughts in [Vipassi's] mind, and he thought: "Alas, the world is perishing, the world is being destroyed! Indeed, the mind of Vipassi, the Blessed One, the Arhat, the perfectly enlightened Buddha, inclines to inaction, not to teaching the Dharma!"

Then, monks, that Great Brahmā, just as quickly as a strong man might stretch out his flexed arm or flex his extended arm, vanished from the Brahmā-world and appeared before Vipassi, the Blessed One, the Arhat, the perfectly enlightened Buddha.

Then, monks, that Great Brahmā, arranging his upper robe over one shoulder, kneeling on his right knee on the ground, saluted Vipassi, the Blessed One, the Arhat, the perfectly enlightened Buddha, with joined hands and said this to Vipassi, the Blessed One, the Arhat, the perfectly enlightened Buddha: "May the Blessed One teach the Dharma, venerable sir! May the Sugata teach the Dharma! There are beings with little dust in their eyes who are declining through not hearing the Dharma; they will be those who understand the Dharma."
66.Aññatarassāti ettha kiñcāpi ‘‘aññataro’’ti vuttaṃ, atha kho imasmiṃ cakkavāḷe jeṭṭhakamahābrahmā esoti veditabbo. Nassati vata bho lokoti so kira imaṃ saddaṃ tathā nicchāresi, yathā dasasahassilokadhātubrahmāno sutvā sabbe sannipatiṃsu. Yatra hi nāmāti yasmiṃ nāma loke. Purato pāturahosīti tehi dasahi brahmasahassehi saddhiṃ pāturahosi. Apparajakkhajātikāti paññāmaye akkhimhi appaṃ parittaṃ rāgadosamoharajaṃ etesaṃ, evaṃ sabhāvāti apparajakkhajātikā. Assavanatāti assavanatāya. Bhavissantīti purimabuddhesu dasapuññakiriyavatthuvasena katādhikārā paripākagatā padumāni viya sūriyarasmisamphassaṃ, dhammadesanaṃyeva ākaṅkhamānā catuppadikagāthāvasāne ariyabhūmiṃ okkamanārahā na eko, na dve, anekasatasahassā dhammassa aññātāro bhavissantīti dasseti.
66. "Aññatarassā" means here, although it is said "aññataro", but in this cakkavāḷa (world-system) he is to be understood as the jeṭṭhakamahābrahmā (eldest great Brahma).

"Nassati vata bho loko" means he uttered this sound in such a way that the dasasahassilokadhātubrahmāno (Brahmas of the ten thousand world-systems), having heard, all gathered together.

"Yatra hi nāmā" means in which world indeed.

"Purato pāturahosī" means he appeared with those ten thousand Brahma-s.

"Apparajakkhajātikā" means in the paññāmaye akkhimhi (wisdom-eye), there is little rāgadosamoharajaṃ (dust of passion, hatred, and delusion) for them; thus, their nature is apparajakkhajātikā (of little dust nature).

"Assavanatā" means by assavanatāya (not listening).

"Bhavissantī" means in the former Buddhas, those who have done deeds of merit based on the ten objects, having reached maturity, like lotuses to the touch of the sun's rays, expecting only the teaching of the Dharma, at the end of the four-lined verse, not one, not two, but many hundreds of thousands will be worthy to descend into the noble ground, this he shows.
67
67. ‘‘Evaṃ vutte atha kho (ka.), bhikkhave, vipassī bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho taṃ mahābrahmānaṃ etadavoca – ‘mayhampi kho, brahme, etadahosi – ‘‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ dhammaṃ deseyya’’nti. Tassa mayhaṃ, brahme, etadahosi – ‘‘adhigato kho myāyaṃ dhammo gambhīro duddaso duranubodho santo paṇīto atakkāvacaro nipuṇo paṇḍitavedanīyo. Ālayarāmā kho panāyaṃ pajā ālayaratā ālayasammuditā. Ālayarāmāya kho pana pajāya ālayaratāya ālayasammuditāya duddasaṃ idaṃ ṭhānaṃ yadidaṃ idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppādo. Idampi kho ṭhānaṃ duddasaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbānaṃ. Ahañceva kho pana dhammaṃ deseyyaṃ, pare ca me na ājāneyyuṃ; so mamassa kilamatho, sā mamassa vihesā’’ti. Apissu maṃ, brahme , imā anacchariyā gāthāyo paṭibhaṃsu pubbe assutapubbā – ‘‘Kicchena me adhigataṃ, halaṃ dāni pakāsituṃ; Rāgadosaparetehi, nāyaṃ dhammo susambudho. ‘‘Paṭisotagāmiṃ nipuṇaṃ, gambhīraṃ duddasaṃ aṇuṃ; Rāgarattā na dakkhanti, tamokhandhena āvuṭā’’ti. ‘Itiha me, brahme, paṭisañcikkhato appossukkatāya cittaṃ nami, no dhammadesanāyā’ti.
67. Thus addressed, monks, the Blessed Vipassī, worthy and perfectly enlightened, said to that Mahābrahmā: ‘Brahmā, this occurred to me also: “Why don't I teach the Dharma?”’

This occurred to me, Brahmā: “This Dharma that I have attained is profound, hard to see, hard to understand, peaceful, excellent, beyond reasoning, subtle, to be experienced by the wise.”

But this generation delights in attachment, is devoted to attachment, gladdened by attachment.

But for a generation delighting in attachment, devoted to attachment, gladdened by attachment, this state is hard to see, namely, specific conditionality, dependent origination ( pratītyasamutpāda).

This state too is hard to see, namely, the stilling of all formations ( sarvasaṃskārasamatha), the relinquishing of all acquisitions ( sarvopadhipaṭinissagga), the destruction of craving ( tṛṣṇākṣaya), dispassion (virāga), cessation (nirodha), nirvāṇa (nirvāṇa).

And if I were to teach the Dharma, others would not understand me; that would be wearisome and troublesome for me.’

Furthermore, Brahmā, these amazing verses, never heard before, occurred to me:

“With difficulty I attained it, enough of revealing it now;

This Dharma is not easily understood by those overcome with passion and hate.”

“Going against the stream, subtle, profound, hard to see, minute;

Those dyed in passion will not see it, shrouded in a mass of darkness.”

‘Thus, Brahmā, reflecting in this way, my mind inclined to dwelling at ease because of lack of enthusiasm, not to teaching the Dharma.’
68
68. ‘‘Dutiyampi kho, bhikkhave, so mahābrahmā…pe… tatiyampi kho, bhikkhave, so mahābrahmā vipassiṃ bhagavantaṃ arahantaṃ sammāsambuddhaṃ etadavoca – ‘desetu, bhante, bhagavā dhammaṃ, desetu sugato dhammaṃ, santi sattā apparajakkhajātikā, assavanatā dhammassa parihāyanti, bhavissanti dhammassa aññātāro’ti.
"68. "Again, monks, that Great Brahmā… and a third time, monks, that Great Brahmā said this to the Blessed Vipassī, worthy and rightly self-awakened: ‘Let the Blessed One teach the Dhamma, let the Well-Gone One teach the Dhamma. There are beings with little dust in their eyes; they are falling away from hearing the Dhamma; they will be knowers of the Dhamma.’"
69
69. ‘‘Atha kho, bhikkhave, vipassī bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho brahmuno ca ajjhesanaṃ viditvā sattesu ca kāruññataṃ paṭicca buddhacakkhunā lokaṃ volokesi. Addasā kho, bhikkhave, vipassī bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho buddhacakkhunā lokaṃ volokento satte apparajakkhe mahārajakkhe tikkhindriye mudindriye svākāre dvākāre suviññāpaye duviññāpaye duviññāpaye bhabbe abhabbe (syā.) appekacce paralokavajjabhayadassāvine dassāvino (sī. syā. kaṃ. ka.) viharante, appekacce na paralokavajjabhayadassāvine dassāvino (sī. syā. kaṃ. ka.) viharante. Seyyathāpi nāma uppaliniyaṃ vā paduminiyaṃ vā puṇḍarīkiniyaṃ vā appekaccāni uppalāni vā padumāni vā puṇḍarīkāni vā udake jātāni udake saṃvaḍḍhāni udakānuggatāni anto nimuggaposīni. Appekaccāni uppalāni vā padumāni vā puṇḍarīkāni vā udake jātāni udake saṃvaḍḍhāni samodakaṃ ṭhitāni. Appekaccāni uppalāni vā padumāni vā puṇḍarīkāni vā udake jātāni udake saṃvaḍḍhāni udakā accuggamma ṭhitāni anupalittāni udakena. Evameva kho, bhikkhave, vipassī bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho buddhacakkhunā lokaṃ volokento addasa satte apparajakkhe mahārajakkhe tikkhindriye mudindriye svākāre dvākāre suviññāpaye duviññāpaye appekacce paralokavajjabhayadassāvine viharante, appekacce na paralokavajjabhayadassāvine viharante.
69. Then, O monks, the Lord Vipaśyin, the Arhat, the Perfectly Enlightened Buddha, having understood Brahmā's request and being moved by compassion for beings, surveyed the world with his Buddha-eye ( buddhacakṣunā lokaṃ volokesi).

He saw, O monks, the Lord Vipaśyin, the Arhat, the Perfectly Enlightened Buddha, surveying the world with his Buddha-eye, beings with little dust in their eyes, beings with much dust in their eyes, those with keen faculties, those with dull faculties, those with good attributes, those with bad attributes, those easy to teach, those difficult to teach, some dwelling seeing fear and blame in the other world, some not seeing fear and blame in the other world.

Just as in a pond of blue lotuses, or red lotuses, or white lotuses, some blue lotuses, or red lotuses, or white lotuses are born in the water, grow in the water, thrive immersed in the water, not rising out of it; some blue lotuses, or red lotuses, or white lotuses are born in the water, grow in the water, and stand at an even level with the water; and some blue lotuses, or red lotuses, or white lotuses are born in the water, grow in the water, rise up out of the water, and stand unsoiled by the water;

Even so, O monks, the Lord Vipaśyin, the Arhat, the Perfectly Enlightened Buddha, surveying the world with his Buddha-eye, saw beings with little dust in their eyes, beings with much dust in their eyes, those with keen faculties, those with dull faculties, those with good attributes, those with bad attributes, those easy to teach, those difficult to teach, some dwelling seeing fear and blame in the other world, some not seeing fear and blame in the other world.
69.Ajjhesananti evaṃ tikkhattuṃ yācanaṃ. Buddhacakkhunāti indriyaparopariyattañāṇena ca āsayānusayañāṇena ca. Imesañhi dvinnaṃ ñāṇānaṃ ‘‘buddhacakkhū’’ti nāmaṃ, sabbaññutaññāṇassa ‘‘samantacakkhū’’ti, tiṇṇaṃ maggañāṇānaṃ ‘‘dhammacakkhū’’ti. Apparajakkhetiādīsu yesaṃ vuttanayeneva paññācakkhumhi rāgādirajaṃ appaṃ, te apparajakkhā. Yesaṃ taṃ mahantaṃ, te mahārajakkhā. Yesaṃ saddhādīni indriyāni tikkhāni, te tikkhindriyā. Yesaṃ tāni mudūni, te mudindriyā. Yesaṃ teyeva saddhādayo ākārā sundarā, te svākārā. Ye kathitakāraṇaṃ sallakkhenti, sukhena sakkā honti viññāpetuṃ, te suviññāpayā. Ye paralokañceva vajjañca bhayato passanti, te paralokavajjabhayadassāvino nāma. Ayaṃ panettha pāḷi – ‘‘saddho puggalo apparajakkho, assaddho puggalo mahārajakkho.… Āraddhavīriyo…pe… kusīto… upaṭṭhitassati… muṭṭhassati… samāhito… asamāhito… paññavā… duppañño puggalo mahārajakkho. Tathā saddho puggalo tikkhindriyo…pe… paññavā puggalo paralokavajjabhayadassāvī, duppañño puggalo na paralokavajjabhayadassāvī. Lokoti khandhaloko, dhātuloko, āyatanaloko, sampattibhavaloko, vipattibhavaloko, sampattisambhavaloko, vipattisambhavaloko. Eko loko – sabbe sattā āhāraṭṭhitikā. Dve lokā – nāmañca rūpañca. Tayo lokā – tisso vedanā. Cattāro lokā – cattāro āhārā. Pañca lokā – pañcupādānakkhandhā. Cha lokā – cha ajjhattikāni āyatanāni. Satta lokā – satta viññāṇaṭṭhitiyo. Aṭṭha lokā – aṭṭha lokadhammā. Nava lokā – nava sattāvāsā. Dasa lokā – dasāyatanāni. Dvādasa lokā – dvādasāyatanāni. Aṭṭhārasa lokā – aṭṭhārasa dhātuyo. Vajjanti sabbe kilesā vajjaṃ, sabbe duccaritā vajjaṃ, sabbe abhisaṅkhārā vajjaṃ, sabbe bhavagāmikammā vajjaṃ. Iti imasmiñca loke imasmiñca vajje tibbā bhayasaññā paccupaṭṭhitā hoti, seyyathāpi ukkhittāsike vadhake . Imehi paññāsāya ākārehi imāni pañcindriyāni jānāti passati aññāti paṭivijjhati, idaṃ tathāgatassa indriyaparopariyatte ñāṇa’’nti (paṭi. ma. 1.112). Loko Vajja Uppaliniyanti uppalavane. Itaresupi eseva nayo. Antonimuggaposīnīti yāni aññānipi padumāni antonimuggāneva posayanti. Udakaṃ accuggamma ṭhitānīti udakaṃ atikkamitvā ṭhitāni. Tattha yāni accuggamma ṭhitāni, tāni sūriyarasmisamphassaṃ āgamayamānāni ṭhitāni ajja pupphanakāni. Yāni samodakaṃ ṭhitāni, tāni sve pupphanakāni. Yāni udakānuggatāni antoudakaposīni, tāni tatiyadivase pupphanakāni. Udakā pana anuggatāni aññānipi sarojauppalādīni nāma atthi, yāni neva pupphissanti, macchakacchapabhakkhāneva bhavissanti, tāni pāḷiṃ nārūḷhāni. Āharitvā pana dīpetabbānīti dīpitāni. Yatheva hi tāni catubbidhāni pupphāni, evameva ugghaṭitaññū, vipañcitaññū, neyyo, padaparamoti cattāro puggalā. Tattha yassa puggalassa saha udāhaṭavelāya dhammābhisamayo hoti, ayaṃ vuccati puggalo ugghaṭitaññū. Yassa puggalassa saṅkhittena bhāsitassa vitthārena atthe vibhajiyamāne dhammābhisamayo hoti, ayaṃ vuccati puggalo vipañcitaññū. Yassa puggalassa uddesato paripucchato yonisomanasikaroto kalyāṇamitte sevato bhajato payirupāsato anupubbena dhammābhisamayo hoti, ayaṃ vuccati puggalo neyyo. Yassa puggalassa bahumpi suṇato bahumpi bhaṇato bahumpi gaṇhato bahumpi dhārayato bahumpi vācayato na tāya jātiyā dhammābhisamayo hoti, ayaṃ vuccati puggalo padaparamo (pu. pa. 148, 149, 150, 151). Uppaliniya Antonimuggaposīnī Udakaṃ accuggamma ṭhitānī ugghaṭitaññū. vipañcitaññū. neyyo. padaparamo Tattha bhagavā uppalavanādisadisaṃ dasasahassilokadhātuṃ olokento – ‘‘ajja pupphanakāni viya ugghaṭitaññū, sve pupphanakāni viya vipañcitaññū, tatiyadivase pupphanakāni viya neyyo, macchakacchapabhakkhāni viya padaparamo’’ti addasa. Passanto ca – ‘‘ettakā apparajakkhā, ettakā mahārajakkhā. Tatrāpi ettakā ugghaṭitaññū’’ti evaṃ sabbākārato addasa. Tattha tiṇṇaṃ puggalānaṃ imasmiṃyeva attabhāve bhagavato dhammadesanā atthaṃ sādheti, padaparamānaṃ anāgate vāsanatthāya hoti. Atha bhagavā imesaṃ catunnaṃ puggalānaṃ atthāvahaṃ dhammadesanaṃ viditvā desetukamyataṃ uppādetvā puna te sabbesupi tīsu bhavesu sabbe satte bhabbābhabbavasena dve koṭṭhāse akāsi. Ye sandhāya vuttaṃ – ‘‘ye te sattā kammāvaraṇena samannāgatā, vipākāvaraṇena samannāgatā, kilesāvaraṇena samannāgatā, assaddhā acchandikā duppaññā abhabbā niyāmaṃ okkamituṃ kusalesu dhammesu sammattaṃ, ime te sattā abhabbā. Katame sattā bhabbā? Ye te sattā na kammāvaraṇena…pe…ime te sattā bhabbā’’ti (vibha. 827; paṭi. ma. 1.114). Tattha sabbepi abhabbapuggale pahāya bhabbapuggaleyeva ñāṇena pariggahetvā – ‘‘ettakā rāgacaritā, ettakā dosamohavitakkasaddhābuddhicaritā’’ti cha koṭṭhāse akāsi. Evaṃ katvā – ‘‘dhammaṃ desessāmī’’ti cintesi . Brahmā taṃ ñatvā somanassajāto bhagavantaṃ gāthāhi ajjhabhāsi. Idaṃ sandhāya – ‘‘atha kho so, bhikkhave, mahābrahmā’’tiādi vuttaṃ.
69. Ajjhesana means thus, threefold requesting.

Buddhacakkhunā means with the knowledge of the capacity of the faculties and with the knowledge of underlying tendencies.

Indeed, the name of these two knowledges is "buddhacakkhū," the name of the knowledge of omniscience is "samantacakkhū," the name of the three knowledges of the paths is "dhammacakkhū."

Among apparajakkhe, those whose passion, etc., is slight in the eye of wisdom, those are apparajakkhā.

Those whose is great, those are mahārajakkhā.

Those whose faculties, beginning with faith, are sharp, those are tikkhindriyā.

Those whose are mild, those are mudindriyā.

Those whose qualities such as faith are beautiful, those are svākārā.

Those who discern the stated reason, who are easily able to be instructed, those are suviññāpayā.

Those who see the next world and fault as fearful, they are named paralokavajjabhayadassāvino.

This is the Pāli here: "The faithful individual is with slight passion, the unfaithful individual is with great passion.

...the one who has aroused energy...the lazy one...the one with established mindfulness...the one with disturbed mindfulness...the concentrated one...the unconcentrated one...the wise one...the unwise individual is with great passion."

Thus, the faithful individual is with sharp faculties...the wise individual is one who sees the next world and faults as fearful, the unwise individual does not see the next world and faults as fearful.

Loko means aggregate-world ( khandhaloko), element-world ( dhātuloko), sense-base-world ( āyatanaloko), attainment-becoming-world ( sampattibhavaloko), adversity-becoming-world ( vipattibhavaloko), attainment-arising-world ( sampattisambhavaloko), adversity-arising-world ( vipattisambhavaloko).

One world: all beings subsist on nutriment ( āhāraṭṭhitikā).

Two worlds: name and form ( nāmañca rūpañca).

Three worlds: three feelings ( tisso vedanā).

Four worlds: four nutriments ( cattāro āhārā).

Five worlds: five aggregates of clinging ( pañcupādānakkhandhā).

Six worlds: six internal sense bases ( cha ajjhattikāni āyatanāni).

Seven worlds: seven stations of consciousness ( satta viññāṇaṭṭhitiyo).

Eight worlds: eight worldly conditions ( aṭṭha lokadhammā).

Nine worlds: nine abodes of beings ( nava sattāvāsā).

Ten worlds: ten bases ( dasāyatanāni).

Twelve worlds: twelve bases ( dvādasāyatanāni).

Eighteen worlds: eighteen elements ( aṭṭhārasa dhātuyo).

Vajja means all defilements are fault ( vajjaṃ), all misconduct is fault ( vajjaṃ), all activities are fault ( vajjaṃ), all karma leading to becoming is fault ( vajjaṃ).

Thus, in this world and in this fault, intense fear is present, just as if a swordsman has raised his sword.

With these qualities of wisdom, he knows, sees, understands, and penetrates these five faculties; this is the knowledge of the capacity of the faculties of the Tathāgata.

Uppaliniya means in the lotus grove ( uppalavane).
And so with the others.

Antonimuggaposīnī means those other lotuses also that nourish only the inner bud.

Udakaṃ accuggamma ṭhitānī means those that stand having risen above the water.

There, those that stand having risen above, those standing awaiting the touch of the sun's rays, today are about to blossom.

Those that stand together with the water, tomorrow are about to blossom.

Those that are submerged in the water, nourished within the water, on the third day are about to blossom.

However, there are also other water-lilies and lotuses that are not submerged in the water, and which are not going to blossom, but will become food for fish and turtles; these are not included in the Pāli.

However, having been brought and shown, they are shown.

Just as those flowers are of four kinds, so too the individuals are of four kinds: ugghaṭitaññū, vipañcitaññū, neyyo, and padaparamo.

There, for whichever individual, at the very moment of mentioning, there is an understanding of the Dharma, this individual is called ugghaṭitaññū.

For whichever individual, when the meaning of what is spoken in brief is analyzed in detail, there is an understanding of the Dharma, this individual is called vipañcitaññū.

For whichever individual, through repeated questioning, through proper attention, through associating with, attending to, and waiting upon good friends, gradually there is an understanding of the Dharma, this individual is called neyyo.

For whichever individual, even though hearing much, speaking much, learning much, retaining much, reciting much, there is no understanding of the Dharma in that birth, this individual is called padaparamo.

There, the Blessed One, looking over the ten thousand world-systems, similar to a lotus grove, saw: "Like those about to blossom today, [are] the ugghaṭitaññū; like those about to blossom tomorrow, [are] the vipañcitaññū; like those about to blossom on the third day, [are] the neyyo; like those that are food for fish and turtles, [are] the padaparamo."

And seeing, "So many have little dust, so many have much dust, and even among them, so many are ugghaṭitaññū," thus he saw in every way.

There, for the three individuals, the Blessed One's teaching of the Dharma achieves its purpose in this very existence; for the padaparamo, it is for the sake of a trace in the future.

Then, the Blessed One, knowing the Dharma-teaching that brings benefit to these four individuals, and generating a desire to teach, again made all those beings in all three existences into two groups, according to whether they are capable or incapable.

Referring to which it was said: "Those beings who are endowed with a hindrance of deeds ( kammāvaraṇa), endowed with a hindrance of results ( vipākāvaraṇa), endowed with a hindrance of defilements ( kilesāvaraṇa), without faith, without desire, unwise, incapable of entering the fixed course, rightly, in wholesome things, these beings are incapable.

Which beings are capable? Those beings who are not with a hindrance of deeds...pe...these beings are capable" (Vibha. 827; Paṭi. Ma. 1. 114).

There, having abandoned all the incapable individuals, and having grasped only the capable individuals with knowledge, he made six groups: "So many are of lustful character, so many are of hateful, deluded, speculative, faithful, and intelligent character."

Having done this, he thought, "I will teach the Dharma."

Brahmā, knowing this, became delighted and addressed the Blessed One with verses.
Concerning this, it was said: "Then, monks, the Great Brahma..." etc.
70
70. ‘‘Atha kho so, bhikkhave, mahābrahmā vipassissa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa cetasā cetoparivitakkamaññāya vipassiṃ bhagavantaṃ arahantaṃ sammāsambuddhaṃ gāthāhi ajjhabhāsi – ‘Sele yathā pabbatamuddhaniṭṭhito, yathāpi passe janataṃ samantato; Tathūpamaṃ dhammamayaṃ sumedha, pāsādamāruyha samantacakkhu. ‘Sokāvatiṇṇaṃ sokāvakiṇṇaṃ (syā.) janatamapetasoko, sokāvakiṇṇaṃ (syā.) Avekkhassu jātijarābhibhūtaṃ; Uṭṭhehi vīra vijitasaṅgāma, Satthavāha aṇaṇa vicara loke. Desassu desetu (syā. pī.) bhagavā dhammaṃ, desetu (syā. pī.) Aññātāro bhavissantī’ti.
"70. Then, monks, Mahā Brahmā, knowing with his mind the thought in the Blessed Vipassī’s, the Arahant, the perfectly enlightened one’s mind, addressed the Blessed Vipassī, the Arahant, the perfectly enlightened one in verses:

‘Just as one standing on the summit of a mountain of rock might see the people all around,

even so, O wise one, all-seeing, ascend the palace of Dharma, made of Dhamma.

‘Look upon the people plunged in sorrow, rid of sorrow yourself,

beholding those overcome by birth and old age;

Arise, O hero, victorious in battle,

Caravan-Leader, Debtless One, travel through the world.

Teach, O Blessed One, the Dharma,

there will be those who will understand.’"
70. Tattha ajjhabhāsīti adhiabhāsi, adhikicca ārabbha abhāsīti attho. Sele yathā pabbatamuddhaniṭṭhitoti selamaye ekagghane pabbatamuddhani yathāṭhitova, na hi tattha ṭhitassa dassanatthaṃ gīvukkhipanapasāraṇādikiccaṃ atthi. Tathūpamanti tappaṭibhāgaṃ selapabbatūpamaṃ. Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepattho, yathā selapabbatamuddhani yathāṭhitova cakkhumā puriso samantato janataṃ passeyya, tathā tvampi sumedha, sundarapaññasabbaññutaññāṇena samantacakkhu bhagavā dhammamayaṃ paññāmayaṃ pāsādamāruyha sayaṃ apetasoko sokāvatiṇṇaṃ jātijarābhibhūtaṃ janataṃ apekkhassu, upadhāraya upaparikkha. Sele yathā pabbatamuddhaniṭṭhito Tathūpama Ayamettha adhippāyo – yathā hi pabbatapāde samantā mahantaṃ khettaṃ katvā tattha kedārapāḷīsu kuṭikāyo katvā rattiṃ aggiṃ jāleyyuṃ. Caturaṅgasamannāgatañca andhakāraṃ assa. Athassa pabbatassa matthake ṭhatvā cakkhumato purisassa bhūmiṃ olokayato neva khettaṃ, na kedārapāḷiyo, na kuṭiyo, na tattha sayitamanussā paññāyeyyuṃ, kuṭikāsu pana aggijālamattameva paññāyeyya. Evaṃ dhammapāsādamāruyha sattanikāyaṃ olokayato tathāgatassa ye te akatakalyāṇā sattā, te ekavihāre dakkhiṇajāṇupasse nisinnāpi buddhacakkhussa āpāthaṃ nāgacchanti, rattiṃ khittasarā viya honti. Ye pana katakalyāṇā veneyyapuggalā, te tassa dūre ṭhitāpi āpāthaṃ āgacchanti, so aggi viya himavantapabbato viya ca. Vuttampi cetaṃ – ‘‘Dūre santo pakāsenti, himavantova pabbato; Asantettha na dissanti, rattiṃ khittā yathā sarā’’ti. (dha. pa. 304); Uṭṭhehīti bhagavato dhammadesanatthaṃ cārikacaraṇaṃ yācanto bhaṇati. Vīrātiādīsu bhagavā vīriyavantatāya vīro, devaputtamaccukilesamārānaṃ vijitattā vijitasaṅgāmo, jātikantarādinittharaṇatthāya veneyyasatthavāhanasamatthatāya satthavāho, kāmacchandaiṇassa abhāvato aṇaṇoti veditabbo. Uṭṭhehī Vīrā vīro, vijitasaṅgāmo, satthavāho, aṇaṇo
70. There, ajjhabhāsī means adhiabhāsi, meaning he spoke concerning and beginning with.

sele yathā pabbatamuddhaniṭṭhito means just as one stands on the peak of a mountain made of rock, one solid mass; there is no need for him to crane his neck or stretch to see. tathūpamanti means comparable to that, similar to a mountain of rock. Herein is the meaning in brief: Just as a man with sight, standing on the peak of a mountain of rock, might see the people all around, so too, O Sumedha, the Blessed One, all-seeing with the knowledge of beautiful wisdom, the all-knowing, having ascended the palace made of the Dhamma, made of wisdom, himself without sorrow, should look upon, consider, and examine the people afflicted by sorrow and overcome by birth and aging,

sele yathā pabbatamuddhaniṭṭhito tathūpama The idea here is this: Suppose someone were to make a large field all around the foot of a mountain, and having built huts in rows in the field, were to light a fire at night. It would be complete darkness with the four constituents. Then, to a man with sight standing on the summit of that mountain and looking down at the ground, neither the field, nor the rows of fields, nor the huts, nor the people lying there would appear; only the blaze of the fire in the huts would appear. In the same way, to the Tathāgata looking down at the assembly of beings after ascending the palace of the Dhamma, those beings who have not done good deeds do not come into the range of the Buddha's eye even when sitting near the right knee in a single monastery; they are like arrows shot at night. But those individuals who have done good deeds, those who are to be trained, come into range even when standing far away; he is like fire, like a mountain in the Himalayas. It was also said:

"The good make themselves known from afar, like a mountain in the Himalayas; The bad are not seen here, like arrows shot at night." (Dhp. 304);
